### 01. BE-Molecular Biology- Session 1 — Thu 02 Oct 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/02_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%201-20251002_142231-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/02_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%201-20251002_142231-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/02_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%201-20251002_142231-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary

This session introduced the molecular biology half of the course (Units 1–3), starting with a broad overview of the human genome. The professor covered why cells are the fundamental unit of life, how DNA stores genetic information, how 2 metres of DNA is packed into chromosomes inside the nucleus, the organisation of genes and intergenic regions, and the central dogma (DNA → RNA → protein). She then introduced nucleic acids: nucleotide structure, the differences between DNA and RNA, base pairing, and why single-stranded RNA folds into a three-dimensional shape. The session ended with a short PyMOL demonstration to visualise an RNA structure.

Key takeaways:

- Cells are the fundamental unit of life, and all organisms share the same genetic code carried by DNA.
- The human genome is approximately 3.2 × 10⁹ nucleotides, split into 23 chromosome pairs (46 chromosomes) and compacted with proteins as chromatin.
- Intergenic regions are not “junk DNA”; they contain information controlling when and how genes are expressed.
- The central dogma: 60,000 genes can be transcribed into RNA, but only about 20,000 of those RNA molecules are translated into proteins.
- Cells differ from each other mainly because they express different sets of proteins, and gene expression is highly dynamic and tightly regulated.
- DNA and RNA are polynucleotides with a sugar-phosphate backbone; DNA is double-stranded with T, RNA is single-stranded with U, and RNA folds according to A–U and C–G base pairing.

#### Key concepts

- **Cell**: the fundamental unit of life; a membrane-enclosed unit filled with a concentrated aqueous solution of chemicals [00:05:15].
- **DNA**: the molecule that carries genetic information; built from four nucleotides (A, C, T, G) [00:07:24].
- **Human genome**: the entire sequence of 3.2 × 10⁹ nucleotides in human DNA; can be represented as an “informatic genome” sequence of A/C/T/G letters [00:08:27][00:09:32].
- **Chromosome**: one of the 23 separate DNA molecules into which the human genome is split [00:09:38].
- **Homologous chromosomes**: the maternal and paternal copies of each of the 23 chromosomes [00:11:57].
- **Karyotype**: the complete set of 46 human chromosomes (23 pairs) [00:11:57].
- **Chromatin**: the complex of DNA packed with proteins via electrostatic interactions, allowing DNA to fit inside the nucleus [00:09:38].
- **Gene**: a segment of DNA containing the instructions to make an RNA molecule; some RNAs are then translated into proteins [00:20:11].
- **Intergenic region**: DNA between genes; contains regulatory information about when and how genes should be expressed [00:21:00].
- **Central dogma of molecular biology**: the flow of genetic information from DNA → RNA → protein [00:24:17].
- **Gene expression**: the process of turning on a gene to produce RNA and, when applicable, protein [00:29:34].
- **Nucleic acid**: a naturally occurring chemical compound that carries information; DNA and RNA are the two types [00:40:40].
- **Nucleotide**: the building block of nucleic acids, made of a phosphate group, a sugar, and a nitrogenous base [00:41:43].
- **Phosphodiester bond**: the bond joining nucleotides into a polynucleotide chain [00:43:49].
- **Base pairing**: the specific hydrogen-bonding complementarity between bases: C pairs with G, and A pairs with T in DNA or U in RNA [00:48:31].

#### Detailed notes

##### 1. Cells as the fundamental unit of life and DNA as the genetic material [00:04:13–00:07:24]

- There are more than 100 million species of living organisms, all made of cells; some are unicellular, others are made of many cells [00:04:13].
- Cells are membrane-enclosed units filled with concentrated aqueous solution and many chemicals [00:05:15].
- Cells vary greatly in shape and size, from micrometres to millimetres [00:05:15].
- Despite differences, cells contain the same broad types of molecules: DNA, RNA, proteins, ions, small chemicals, peptides, and carbohydrates [00:06:20].
- Genetic information needed for survival and reproduction is carried in DNA, and the genetic code is the same across organisms [00:06:20].
- Life depends on the cell’s ability to store, retrieve, and translate the genetic information in DNA, and this information is passed from cell to daughter cell and between generations [00:07:24].

##### 2. The human genome: size, sequence, and packaging [00:08:27–00:11:57]

- DNA is made of nucleotides: thymine (red), cytosine (blue), adenine (yellow), and guanine (green) [00:08:27].
- Human DNA contains 3.2 × 10⁹ nucleotides [00:08:27].
- The genome can be represented simply as a sequence of A, C, T, G; the professor called this the **informatic genome** [00:09:32].
- The total human DNA is about 2 metres long, but the nucleus is only 5–8 μm in diameter [00:09:38].
- To fit into the nucleus, the DNA is first split into 23 chromosomes (two sets: maternal and paternal) and then packed with proteins via electrostatic interactions [00:09:38].
- This DNA–protein complex is called **chromatin** [00:10:50].
- The packing is essential because the DNA must be compacted enough to fit within the nucleus [00:10:50].

##### 3. Chromosomes, karyotype, and chromatin organisation [00:11:57–00:14:45]

- All human cells, except germ cells, contain two copies of each chromosome, giving 46 chromosomes in total [00:11:57].
- Maternal and paternal copies of each chromosome are called **homologous chromosomes** [00:11:57].
- Sex chromosomes are an exception: males have X and Y, females have two X chromosomes [00:11:57].
- The complete set of 46 chromosomes is the **human karyotype** [00:11:57].
- Under the microscope, chromosomes show dark and light bands [00:12:50].
  - Dark regions: highly packed DNA with many proteins, low gene density, less accessible [00:13:00].
  - Light regions: less packed DNA, more accessible, high gene density; genes are found mainly here [00:13:00].
- The accessibility of light regions allows processes such as transcription to start [00:13:30].

##### 4. Genome definition and comparison of eukaryotic and prokaryotic genomes [00:14:45–00:19:10]

- The genome is the entire sequence of nucleotides (A, T, C, G) in an organism’s DNA, containing the total genetic information [00:15:47].
- Most organisms have DNA genomes, but some viruses have RNA genomes [00:15:47].
- The complete genomic sequence has been determined for thousands of organisms, from *Escherichia coli* to humans, mammals, rodents, and plants; sequencing costs keep falling [00:14:45].
- Genome size varies widely: viruses and bacteria have small genomes; animals, plants, mammals, and humans have large genomes [00:16:00].
- **Eukaryotic genome**:
  - Main DNA is in the nucleus [00:16:59].
  - Some DNA is in the cytoplasm, but it is not considered part of the genome [00:16:59].
  - DNA is linear [00:18:04].
  - Replication can start from many origins [00:18:04].
- **Prokaryotic genome**:
  - DNA is in the cytoplasm and is circular [00:16:59].
  - It is not packed with proteins to the same degree as eukaryotic DNA, although some proteins do interact with the bacterial chromosome [00:17:20].
  - There is an additional smaller circular DNA in the cytoplasm, the **plasmid DNA**, which can carry genes such as antibiotic resistance genes [00:18:04].
  - Replication starts from a single origin [00:19:10].
- In both cases, DNA is double-stranded: one strand plus its complementary strand [00:19:10].

##### 5. Genes, intergenic regions, and chromosome anatomy [00:19:10–00:24:17]

- **Gene**: a segment of DNA, thousands of nucleotides long, that contains the instructions for making an RNA molecule; from some RNAs, a protein can be made [00:20:11].
- The professor initially stated the human genome contains 60,000 genes [00:20:11].
  - Later, when comparing species, she gave the number 22,000 genes for humans [00:23:10].
  - These statements appear inconsistent; in context, the 60,000 may refer to all RNA-producing segments of DNA, while only about 20,000 are translated into protein. The 22,000 figure likely refers to protein-coding genes in a species comparison. (This is a note by the student, not stated explicitly by the professor.)
- **Intergenic regions**: DNA between genes; once considered “junk DNA”, but now known to contain information controlling when and how genes are expressed [00:21:00].
- A chromosome has a **P arm**, a **centromere**, and a **Q arm**, plus visible bands [00:22:01].
- A gene can be located by specifying the arm (P or Q) and the band number [00:23:10].
- Gene number is only roughly correlated with species complexity [00:23:55]:
  - Grapes: ~30,000 genes
  - Humans: ~22,000 genes
  - Chicken: ~16,000 genes
- Chromosome number and total genome size are **not** well correlated with complexity [00:24:17].
- In bacteria, genes are packed closely together; in humans, genes are separated by large intergenic regions, and the human genome has more intergenic than genic regions [00:24:17].

##### 6. Central dogma and protein diversity [00:24:17–00:28:31]

- The **central dogma of molecular biology** describes the flow of genetic information: DNA → RNA → protein [00:24:17].
- DNA contains instructions to make RNA; RNA contains the information to make a protein [00:24:17].
- Proteins have a three-dimensional structure and perform most functions inside cells; some RNA molecules also perform functions [00:25:21].
- Of the 60,000 genes that can be transcribed into RNA:
  - 20,000 RNA molecules are translated into proteins [00:25:21].
  - 40,000 RNA molecules are not translated; they function as non-coding RNAs and also regulate gene expression and translation [00:26:30].
- Proteins are made of 20 different amino acids; different sequences and combinations produce different three-dimensional structures and therefore different functions [00:27:50].
- Similar protein shapes tend to produce similar functions; different shapes produce different functions [00:28:31].
- What makes one cell different from another is the set of proteins it contains [00:28:31].
- Proteins must be made at the proper time and place [00:29:34].

##### 7. Gene expression and its regulation [00:28:31–00:40:40]

- **Gene expression** is the process of turning on a gene to produce RNA and, ultimately, protein [00:29:34].
- Cells do not express all 20,000 proteins at once; they regulate expression according to cell type, environment, and needs [00:29:34].
- The large intergenic regions contain the information for when to express a gene and produce the corresponding protein [00:30:00].
- Control of gene expression is extremely complex, and malfunctions can lead to diseases, including cancer [00:30:36].
- Regulation can occur at multiple steps [00:31:00]:
  - Transcription
  - RNA processing
  - RNA transport to the cytoplasm
  - Translation
  - Post-translational modification
- Each step can be stopped or activated to control protein production [00:32:00].
- Different tissues are made of cells with different shapes and functions because they express different sets of proteins [00:32:00].
- Important: a cell type does not express only one protein; it expresses thousands of proteins, with some shared among cell types and some specific [00:35:55].
- The proteome is dynamic; the set of expressed proteins changes with [00:37:31]:
  - Developmental stage (young vs old)
  - Disease state
  - Environment (e.g., living in another country, UV exposure)
  - Exercise or sport
  - Diet
- This environmental influence on gene expression is connected to **epigenetics** [00:38:32].
- During development, regulator proteins bind DNA to control which proteins are produced and thereby differentiate cells into different tissues [00:39:33].

##### 8. Nucleic acids: nucleotide structure and DNA vs RNA [00:40:40–00:46:20]

- Nucleic acids are naturally occurring chemical compounds that carry information; the two types are DNA and RNA [00:40:40].
- A nucleotide consists of [00:41:43]:
  - A phosphate group (negatively charged)
  - A sugar ring
  - A nitrogenous base
- The phosphate group is always the same; the sugar differs between RNA and DNA [00:42:10].
- Bases found in DNA: adenine (A), cytosine (C), thymine (T), guanine (G) [00:42:45].
- Bases found in RNA: adenine (A), cytosine (C), uracil (U), guanine (G) [00:42:45].
- Thymine and uracil are chemically very similar; the main difference is that thymine has an extra methyl group [00:42:45].
- Nucleotides are joined by **phosphodiester bonds**, forming a repeating sugar-phosphate backbone with variable bases [00:43:49].
- A chain of nucleotides is a **polynucleotide chain** [00:43:49].
- RNA is single-stranded; DNA is double-stranded [00:43:49].
- In DNA, the bases are not exposed because they interact with the bases of the complementary strand [00:45:15].
- Sequences are written by convention in the **5′ to 3′** direction; this polarity is important [00:45:15].
- DNA: contains thymine, is double-stranded, and is usually very long [00:46:20].
- RNA: contains uracil, is single-stranded, and is usually shorter [00:46:20].

##### 9. RNA folding and base pairing [00:46:20–00:49:39]

- Because RNA is single-stranded, its bases are exposed and have many potential hydrogen bonds that are not satisfied [00:47:23].
- To satisfy this hydrogen-bonding potential, RNA molecules fold into complex three-dimensional structures [00:47:23].
- Folding brings complementary bases together to form hydrogen bonds [00:48:00].
- Base-pairing rules are strict [00:48:31]:
  - C pairs with G
  - A pairs with U in RNA (with T in DNA)
- There are different types of RNA [00:49:00]:
  - Messenger RNAs (mRNAs) — can be translated into proteins
  - Transfer RNAs (tRNAs)
  - Ribosomal RNAs (rRNAs)
  - Non-coding RNAs
  - Small and long RNAs
- The sequence of an mRNA determines the sequence of the corresponding protein [00:49:39].
- Because folded RNA has a three-dimensional shape, it can perform functions inside the cell [00:49:39].

##### 10. PyMOL visualisation of RNA [00:49:39–00:59:58]

- The professor asked students to install **PyMOL**, a tool for visualising macromolecules [00:50:32].
- Basic procedure: `Fetch 5TPY` to download an RNA structure, then `Display > Sequence` to view the sequence [00:52:00][00:55:00].
- The displayed RNA molecule has:
  - An orange backbone made of alternating sugar and phosphate groups [00:55:00].
  - Bases pointing outward [00:56:06].
- In DNA, these bases would be paired with complementary bases on the opposite strand; in RNA they are exposed and the molecule folds to maximise base-pairing interactions [00:56:06].
- Only A–U and C–G interactions fit the hydrogen-bonding pattern [00:57:09].
- PyMOL visualisation modes shown [00:58:19]:
  - Cartoon: simplified secondary-structure representation
  - Sticks: shows the exact chemical structure
  - Spheres: space-filling representation of atoms
- The purpose was to give a mental image of RNA as a real three-dimensional molecule, not just a sequence [01:00:58].

#### What the professor stressed

- Students should interrupt with questions at any time [00:04:13].
- The genetic code is the same in all organisms [00:06:20].
- Intergenic regions are **not** junk DNA; they are essential for regulating gene expression [00:21:00].
- The central dogma is central to the course: not all transcribed RNA is translated; 40,000 of the 60,000 RNA molecules are non-coding and regulatory [00:25:21].
- Cells are different because of the proteins they express, not because they have different genomes [00:28:31].
- Gene expression is highly dynamic and changes with environment, development, disease, diet, and exercise [00:37:31].
- Malfunction of gene expression regulation can cause diseases such as cancer [00:30:36].
- Polarity (5′ to 3′) of DNA and RNA sequences is important [00:45:15].
- RNA folds according to strict base-pairing rules: A–U and C–G [00:48:31].
- Exams will expect students to be able to use databases and tools, not just recall content [00:01:03].
- PyMOL is not required for now; a tutorial will be provided with the protein sessions [00:59:58].

#### Course logistics mentioned

- The subject is split into two parts [00:00:00]:
  - **Luis Pujadas**: cellular biology and physiology, taught on Mondays.
  - **Mireia Olivella**: molecular biology (human genome, proteome, regulation of gene expression), taught on Thursdays.
- The molecular biology block will cover [00:02:09]:
  - The human genome and its functional elements
  - Human genetic variation and its relationship to disease
  - The human proteome
  - Regulation of gene expression
- Evaluation on the molecular biology part [00:01:03]:
  - Two partial exams (the transcript says partial exam 2 and partial exam 4).
  - Exams consist of short questions expecting short answers.
  - Questions may require using biological databases and tools.
  - Two tasks to deliver.
- From the course page snapshot:
  - **PARTIAL EXAM 1 (Unit 4 – Cellular Biology)**: 10 November 2025.
  - **PARTIAL EXAM 4 (Unit 5 – Physiology)**: 19 January 2026.

- Slides are available on Moodle before class, and exercises with answers are posted [00:03:11].
- The next session will continue with the DNA molecule and move quickly to sequencing of the human genome [00:58:19].

#### Self-test

1. How does 2 metres of DNA fit into a nucleus that is only 5–8 μm in diameter?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The DNA is split into 23 chromosomes, which are then packed with proteins via electrostatic interactions to form chromatin. This compaction allows the long DNA molecule to fit inside the nucleus.
</details>

2. Why are genes more likely to be found in the light-staining bands of chromosomes than in the dark bands?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Light bands represent less tightly packed, more accessible DNA with high gene density. Dark bands are highly compacted and less accessible, so they have low gene density.
</details>

3. Distinguish the terms genome, chromosome, chromatin, and gene as used in the session.

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
- Genome: the entire DNA sequence of an organism.
- Chromosome: one of the separate, packaged DNA molecules into which the genome is divided.
- Chromatin: the complex of DNA and proteins that compacts DNA within the nucleus.
- Gene: a segment of DNA containing instructions to make an RNA molecule.
</details>

4. Why were intergenic regions once called “junk DNA”, and why is that view incorrect?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
They were called junk DNA because they do not contain protein-coding genes. However, they contain important regulatory information that controls when and how genes are expressed, so they are not junk.
</details>

5. Describe the central dogma and the numbers the professor gave for RNA and protein production.

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The central dogma is DNA → RNA → protein. The professor said 60,000 genes can be transcribed into RNA; about 20,000 of those RNAs are translated into proteins, and the remaining 40,000 are non-coding RNAs that perform regulatory or other functions. She later gave 22,000 as the human gene count in a species comparison, so the two figures are inconsistent in the transcript.
</details>

6. Compare eukaryotic and prokaryotic genomes in terms of location, shape, and replication origins.

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Eukaryotic genomes: linear DNA located in the nucleus, with some DNA in the cytoplasm not considered part of the genome; replication starts from many origins. Prokaryotic genomes: circular DNA located in the cytoplasm, often with a small circular plasmid; replication starts from a single origin.
</details>

7. What are the main chemical differences between DNA and RNA?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
They differ in the sugar component and in one base: DNA contains thymine (T) while RNA contains uracil (U). Thymine has an extra methyl group compared with uracil. DNA is also double-stranded and generally longer, whereas RNA is single-stranded and usually shorter.
</details>

8. Why does a single-stranded RNA molecule fold, and what rules govern base pairing?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
RNA folds because its exposed bases have unsatisfied hydrogen-bond potential. Folding brings complementary bases together to maximise hydrogen bonding. Base pairing is specific: C pairs with G, and A pairs with U in RNA (A pairs with T in DNA).
</details>

9. If an RNA fragment has the sequence 5′-AUCG-3′, write the complementary RNA sequence in the 5′ to 3′ direction.

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
5′-CGAU-3′. The antiparallel complement is 3′-UAGC-5′, which written 5′ to 3′ is 5′-CGAU-3′.
</details>

10. All cells in an organism contain the same genome. Why do muscle cells and liver cells look and function differently?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
They express different sets of proteins, and proteins determine cell structure and function. Gene expression is regulated differently in each cell type, so each produces a specific combination of proteins.
</details>

11. What is the “informatic genome”?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
It is the representation of the 3.2 × 10⁹ nucleotides of the human genome as a sequence of letters A, C, T, and G, rather than as chemical structures.
</details>

12. In the PyMOL exercise, what did the RNA structure show about its backbone and bases, and why does the molecule adopt that shape?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The RNA structure showed a sugar-phosphate backbone with bases pointing outward. Because there is no complementary strand, the exposed bases form hydrogen bonds with complementary bases elsewhere in the same molecule (A–U and C–G), causing the RNA to fold into a three-dimensional shape.
</details>

- [1.2-Nucleic Acids](materials/02_1.2-Nucleic%20Acids.pdf)

### 02. BE-Molecular Biology- Session 2 — Thu 09 Oct 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/05_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%202-20251009_140113-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/05_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%202-20251009_140113-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/05_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%202-20251009_140113-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary
This session completed the introduction to nucleic acid structure (RNA recap, then DNA double helix in detail) and moved into the central dogma: how DNA replicates, is repaired, is transcribed into RNA, and how RNA is translated into protein. The professor explained DNA packaging by histones, the anatomy of a eukaryotic gene (promoter, exons, introns, terminator), alternative splicing, and the genetic code. The second half of the class was a practical, computational exercise on going from a raw gene sequence to the complementary strand, the pre-mRNA, and finally the protein sequence. The session ended with the professor walking through the exercise solutions and stressing that this DNA→RNA→protein flow is fundamental.

Key takeaways:
- DNA is a double-stranded, antiparallel helix held together by specific base pairing (A–T with 2 H-bonds, G–C with 3 H-bonds); each turn is 3.4 nm with 10 base pairs.
- Histones pack DNA into nucleosomes/chromatin via electrostatic interactions, and this packing also regulates gene expression by controlling access for transcription and replication.
- The central dogma is DNA → RNA → protein; only ~1.5% of the human genome is transcribed into mRNA, while >90% is transcribed into other functional RNAs.
- Replication and transcription always proceed 5'→3'; a gene on the reverse strand is transcribed from right to left, and the promoter is always upstream of the gene, not part of the transcribed sequence.
- Eukaryotic genes contain exons and introns; splicing removes introns and alternative splicing produces multiple different mRNAs (and therefore proteins) from one gene.
- Translation reads mRNA in codons (three nucleotides); the genetic code is redundant/degenerate, which buffers some mutations.

#### Key concepts
- **DNA double helix**: two antiparallel polynucleotide chains held together by hydrogen bonds between complementary bases (A–T, G–C), wound around each other to form major and minor grooves [00:01:01–00:06:05].
- **Base complementarity**: chemical consequence of H-bonding potential — G–C makes 3 hydrogen bonds, A–T makes 2; other pairings are chemically impossible [00:02:48].
- **Antiparallel strands**: one strand runs 5'→3', the other 3'→5'; named after the free carbon on the terminal sugar [00:03:55].
- **Histones**: positively charged proteins that bind the negatively charged phosphate backbone of DNA to compact it [00:09:17].
- **Nucleosome**: basic repeating unit of chromatin; contains 8 histone proteins and 146 nucleotides of DNA [00:10:26–00:11:26].
- **Chromatosome**: nucleosome plus one additional histone protein, making two full turns of DNA around the octamer [00:11:26].
- **Central dogma**: flow of genetic information: DNA → (transcription) → RNA → (translation) → protein [00:25:45–00:28:56].
- **Gene**: DNA sequence containing the transcribed region (RNA coding sequence + terminator); the promoter is upstream and not transcribed [00:41:00].
- **Promoter**: upstream sequence containing signals (e.g., TATA box) where transcription factors bind to initiate transcription [00:45:11].
- **Transcription factors**: proteins that bind the promoter to activate or repress transcription [00:46:28].
- **Exons/introns**: exons are retained in mature mRNA; introns are removed by splicing [00:52:44].
- **Alternative splicing**: different combinations of exons produce different mRNAs from a single gene [00:53:46].
- **Codon**: group of three nucleotides read during translation; each specifies one amino acid [00:58:36].
- **Genetic code redundancy (degeneracy)**: multiple codons can specify the same amino acid, buffering mutations [00:59:00].
- **CDS (coding sequence)**: portion of mRNA translated into protein; flanked by 5' UTR and 3' UTR [01:00:41].
- **Mutation**: permanent change in DNA sequence caused by replication errors, chemicals, metabolites, thermal collisions, or radiation [00:37:36].

#### Detailed notes

##### Recap: RNA structure
**[00:00:00]**
RNA is single-stranded. To satisfy its hydrogen-bonding potential, the bases form H-bonds within the same strand, folding the RNA into a three-dimensional structure that allows it to perform a function inside the cell. Contrast with DNA, which uses two strands.

##### DNA structure
**[00:01:01]**
DNA (deoxyribonucleic acid) consists of two long polynucleotide chains held together by hydrogen bonds between bases on opposite strands.
- A always pairs with T; G always pairs with C.
- This pairing rule is chemical: G–C can form 3 hydrogen bonds, A–T forms 2. Pairings like T–C or G–A are chemically impossible [00:02:48].
- The two strands are **antiparallel**: one runs 5'→3', the other 3'→5'. The 5' and 3' designations refer to the free carbon atom on the terminal sugar [00:03:55].
- The backbone is alternating phosphate–sugar; bases attach to the sugars and face each other, forming H-bonds [00:05:00].
- Coiling of the two strands creates alternating **major and minor grooves** on the helix [00:05:00–00:06:05].

##### DNA dimensions and genome scale
**[00:06:05]**
- Each turn of the double helix comprises about 10 base pairs; one complete helical turn is **3.4 nm** long; helix diameter is about **2 nm**.
- Each base pair is **0.34 nm** long.
- Human genome: ~3.2 × 10^9 nucleotides (per haploid copy).
- Diploid human genome: 3 billion base pairs; with two copies, **6 billion base pairs** per diploid cell [00:08:08].
- Each diploid cell therefore contains about **2 meters of DNA** (~6 × 10^9 bp × 0.34 nm) [00:08:51].
- The human body has an estimated 50 trillion cells, giving about 100 trillion meters of DNA in a body that must all fit inside cell nuclei [00:09:00].

##### DNA packing: histones, chromatin, nucleosome
**[00:09:17]**
How does 2 m of DNA fit into a nucleus? Histone proteins solve this.
- Histones have **positive charges** that interact electrostatically with the **negative charges** of DNA phosphate groups.
- These interactions compact the DNA, making the DNA–protein complex smaller.
- **Chromatin** = DNA + histone proteins. Its basic repeating structural unit is the **nucleosome**.
- A nucleosome contains **8 histone proteins** and **146 nucleotides** of DNA [00:10:26–00:11:26].
- Adding one more histone protein and two full turns of DNA around the octamer gives the **chromatosome** [00:11:26].
- Packing hierarchy: DNA → chromatin → nucleosomes → chromatosome → chromosome [00:12:33].
- **Regulatory role**: histones are not just packaging. To transcribe or replicate DNA, the strands must be separated, so histones must first unpack. Other proteins modify histones to expose the required DNA region, making histones key regulators of gene expression [00:12:33–00:13:50].

##### PyMOL visualization exercise (RNA vs DNA)
**[00:14:22]**
The professor demonstrated structures in PyMOL:
- RNA structure: fetch `5TPY` (the code was initially said as "5-DPY" but typed as 5TPY). Single-stranded; backbone orange; bases from the **same strand** form H-bonds, giving 3D structure [00:15:22–00:17:35].
- DNA structure: fetch `5DNM`. Double-stranded; shows major and minor grooves clearly; bases from **opposite strands** interact [00:17:35–00:18:42].
- The `5DNM` structure also contains histone proteins. Visualizing the protein as a surface shows it is extremely tightly packed against DNA.
- Extra molecules (sulfate, magnesium ion) in the structure are experimental artifacts used to stabilize the structure, not real parts of chromatin; they can be hidden with the `h` command [00:20:44–00:21:30].
- Selection shortcuts: `s` (show), `h` (hide) [00:21:30].

##### Central dogma of molecular biology
**[00:25:45]**
Information flows: **DNA → RNA → protein**.
- DNA contains all information (genes) for cell survival, replication, etc.
- Transcription produces RNA molecules. Some RNAs fold and function directly; others are translated into proteins.
- Approximate numbers given: 60,000 genomic regions/genes can be transcribed into RNAs; ~20,000 are messenger RNAs translated into proteins [00:29:10].
- **What makes one cell type different from another** is the set of proteins it produces (size, shape, and function of proteins differ per tissue). Gene expression — which genes are transcribed — differs by cell type, disease state, and developmental stage; cells only transcribe/translate what is needed [00:30:00–00:33:08].
- Proteins are what "really do the work" inside the cell, although RNA molecules also have regulatory functions [00:33:30].

##### DNA replication
**[00:34:10]**
- Replication is semiconservative: the double helix opens and each original strand serves as a template for a new complementary strand. Starting from one double strand, you end with two double strands.
- Histones must unpack first; proteins such as **topoisomerases** and **helicases** participate in opening the helix (names given as context, not required detail) [00:34:34].
- **Key rule: replication always proceeds 5'→3'** [00:35:22].
- For the main strand (5'→3') replication goes left-to-right; for the complementary strand (3'→5'), replication goes right-to-left because synthesis must always be 5'→3'.
- Fragments of DNA are synthesized and later joined to the template strand; a linked video explains the full mechanism [00:35:50].

##### DNA repair and mutations
**[00:36:30]**
- DNA is damaged/mutated constantly; repair mechanisms exist.
- A **mutation** is a permanent change in the DNA sequence that remains in the genome across generations [00:37:20].
- Four main causes of DNA mutation: replication errors, chemicals, body metabolites, thermal collisions, and radiation [00:37:36].
- Replication error rate: about **1 mistake per 10^7 nucleotides**. Repair mechanisms reduce this to about **1 permanent mutation per 10^9 nucleotides** per replication [00:38:36–00:39:47].
- The human genome is ~3.2 × 10^9 nucleotides, so every round of replication produces some permanent mutations.
- Consequences: mutations can be neutral, can predispose to disease when combined with other mutations/environment, or can directly cause malfunctioning proteins and diseases (e.g., cancer) [00:39:47–00:40:30].
- Mutations are "good" in the sense that they generate variation; otherwise we would have the same genome as millions of years ago [00:40:30].

##### Transcription: anatomy of a gene
**[00:40:52]**
- Transcription copies a DNA sequence into RNA; the resulting RNA may be translated into protein.
- A **gene** consists of (from upstream to downstream): the **promoter**, the **RNA coding sequence**, and the **terminator**. The promoter is **not part of the gene** and is **not transcribed**; it lies upstream of the coding sequence [00:41:00–00:43:21].
- During transcription, histones unpack, the double helix opens, and **one strand acts as a template** to synthesize a single-stranded RNA molecule.
- RNA uses **U (uracil) instead of T**; otherwise sequence is like the DNA strand [00:42:30].
- **Upstream** = toward 5' region; **downstream** = toward 3' [00:43:21].
- Genes can be on the **forward (main) strand** or the **reverse strand**; transcription always goes 5'→3'.
  - Forward gene: transcription goes left-to-right; promoter is on the **left**.
  - Reverse gene: transcription goes right-to-left; promoter is on the **right**.
  - The promoter is always immediately upstream of the gene [00:43:50–00:45:11].
- Most promoters contain specific sequences such as the **TATA box** (transcribed as "TATA-BOC") recognized by proteins [00:45:11].

##### Regulation of transcription
**[00:45:11–00:48:30]**
- Proteins that bind the promoter and regulate transcription are **transcription factors**.
- Transcription is a complex network: to produce a protein, other proteins must bind the promoter; an RNA polymerase holoenzyme ("alloenzyme" in the transcript) then binds to initiate transcription.
- Distant DNA sequences can also bind proteins that interact with transcription factors; because of 3D folding, sequences far in linear distance can be close in space and regulate gene expression.
- Regulatory proteins include:
  - **Activators**: bind promoter to start transcription.
  - **Co-activators**: bind activators to speed up transcription.
  - **Repressors**: bind promoter to block transcription.
  - **Co-repressors**: bind repressors to further stop transcription.
- Transcription always proceeds 5'→3'; the RNA molecule contains the coding sequence and terminator, and is released after the terminator signals stop [00:48:30–00:49:36].

##### Prokaryotic vs eukaryotic transcription
**[00:49:36]**
- **Prokaryotes (bacteria)**: genes are arranged together with little intergenic space; one RNA molecule can contain sequences for several (5–6) genes, producing several proteins at once (an operon). Usually these proteins have related functions needed simultaneously [00:50:38–00:51:20].
- **Eukaryotes**: more steps are required before translation:
  1. A **pre-mRNA** is made.
  2. It is processed into **mature mRNA** (5' cap addition, poly-A tail addition, splicing).
  3. Then translation occurs [00:51:39–00:52:44].
- Only ~1.5% of the human genome is transcribed into mRNA that will be translated; **>90%** is transcribed into other RNA molecules. RNA molecules live about **7–10 hours** [00:56:20–00:56:52].
- **RNA-seq** is a technique that quantifies RNA molecules and their sequences, enabling comparison of gene expression between cells, tissues, or disease states [00:56:52].

##### RNA processing: splicing and alternative splicing
**[00:52:44]**
- Pre-mRNA processing in eukaryotes:
  - Addition of a **5' cap** (a modified nucleotide at the beginning).
  - Addition of a **poly-A tail** (multiple adenine nucleotides at the 3' end).
  - **Splicing**: removes **introns** (green regions in slides) and joins **exons** (orange) to make mature mRNA [00:52:44–00:53:46].
- **Alternative splicing**: different combinations of exons are included/excluded in different transcripts from the same gene.
  - Example given: one gene with exons 1–5 produces three mRNAs: one with all exons, one lacking exon 3, one lacking exon 4 → three different proteins from one gene [00:53:46–00:55:06].
- The information for which exons to include is **encoded in the DNA sequence itself** (splice sites/signals, in introns and exons). Proteins read these signals, but the "language" is still not fully understood [01:05:00–01:07:50].
- After processing, mature mRNA moves from nucleus to cytoplasm for translation.

##### Translation and the genetic code
**[00:57:25]**
- Translation occurs at the **ribosome** (a complex of RNA and protein); **transfer RNAs** (tRNAs) also participate [00:59:38].
- mRNA is read in **codons** — groups of three nucleotides. Each codon specifies one amino acid via the **genetic code** table [00:58:00].
- The genetic code is **redundant/degenerate**: several codons code for the same amino acid (e.g., phenylalanine is UUU or UUC; leucine can be UUA or UUG). This redundancy means some DNA mutations do not change the amino acid sequence, so the protein stays identical [00:59:00–01:00:00].

##### mRNA anatomy: UTRs, CDS, start and stop codons
**[01:00:41]**
- A mature mRNA has:
  - **5' UTR** (untranslated region at the beginning) — not translated; contains binding sites that control translation initiation and mRNA stability.
  - **CDS (coding sequence)**: the central region actually translated into protein.
  - **3' UTR** — not translated; important for regulation/stability.
- The first codon of the CDS is always **AUG**, coding for **methionine (M)** — the first amino acid of every protein [01:01:46].
- The last codon is a **stop codon**: UAA, UAG, or UGA. The stop codon is not translated into an amino acid; it signals termination [01:02:10].

##### Worked example: from gene sequence to protein
**[01:01:46–01:04:50]**
The professor walked through a slide example:
- A genomic DNA sequence contains a promoter (upstream), then exons (green) and introns (red).
- Transcription initiation site and final transcription site mark the gene boundaries.
- Introns are removed; exons are joined; 5' cap and poly-A tail are added.
- 5' and 3' UTRs are not translated; only the CDS is.
- In the CDS, every three nucleotides form a codon; the first codon is AUG (methionine, M); the last is a stop codon (not included in protein).
- Two alternatively spliced mRNAs from the same gene can produce different protein sequences.

##### Practical exercise (transcription and translation)
**[01:09:16]**
Exercise given from the course page "Exercises on Transcription and Translation":
- A gene on chromosome 1 with defined start/end coordinates and 7 exons (each with coordinates).
- Using any programming language (the professor used Bash; Python/R also fine), students must:
  1. Build the **complementary strand** (A↔T, G↔C), knowing it runs 3'→5' if the given strand is 5'→3' [01:12:20].
  2. Identify the **initiation transcription site** (first nucleotide) and **final transcription site** (last nucleotide) [01:16:17].
  3. Construct the **pre-mRNA**: replace T with U, join exons using the coordinates, remove introns, add 5' cap and poly-A tail [01:12:37–01:13:57].
  4. Using the CDS coordinates, cut the mRNA into 5' UTR, CDS, and 3' UTR, then apply the **genetic code** to the CDS to obtain the protein sequence [01:21:58–01:22:55].
- Key clarification from Q&A: the **promoter is made of nucleotides but is not part of the gene** because the gene is the portion that is transcribed [01:13:57–01:14:40].
- Alternative splicing means other exon combinations are possible, so the same gene can yield multiple transcript and protein sequences [01:23:00].

#### What the professor stressed
- The **base-pairing rules (A–T, G–C)** are dictated by chemistry (2 vs 3 hydrogen bonds); this governs everything about DNA structure and copying [00:02:48].
- **Replication and transcription always go 5'→3'** — repeated several times for both processes [00:35:22, 00:43:50, 00:48:30].
- The **promoter is not part of the gene** and is not transcribed; it lies immediately upstream [00:41:41, 01:14:09].
- Know where the promoter is for a forward vs reverse gene (left vs right) — she quizzed the class on this directly [00:44:10].
- The flow **DNA → RNA → protein** and the structure of a gene (promoter, exons, introns, CDS, UTRs) is "very important to understand" — explicitly flagged as essential [01:23:30].
- She urged students to **do the practical exercise themselves** before looking at the solutions, to consolidate the concepts [01:20:30].
- Histones are not just structural; they **regulate gene expression** by controlling DNA accessibility [00:12:33].
- The genetic code's **redundancy buffers mutations** [00:59:00].

#### Course logistics mentioned
- Class meets every Thursday; next session is the following Thursday [01:24:03].
- Students can email the professor with questions [01:24:03].
- The transcription/translation exercise is available on the course page (with solved version); it is for practice, not submitted in class [01:09:16].

#### Self-test
1. Why are A–T and G–C the only base pairings in DNA, and which pair is stronger?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
G–C forms three hydrogen bonds, A–T forms two. Other pairings (e.g., G–A or T–C) cannot form the correct set of hydrogen bonds, so the chemistry of the bases enforces specificity. G–C is the stronger pair because of the extra hydrogen bond.
</details>

2. A diploid human cell contains about 6 billion base pairs. Using 0.34 nm per base pair, estimate the total length of DNA per cell.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
6 × 10^9 bp × 0.34 nm/bp ≈ 2.04 × 10^9 nm ≈ 2.04 meters. This matches the ~2 m of DNA per diploid cell stated in class.
</details>

3. How do histones allow 2 m of DNA to fit in a small nucleus, and why does this matter for gene expression?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Histones are positively charged and bind the negative phosphate backbone electrostatically, compacting DNA into nucleosomes and higher-order chromatin. This packing is dynamic: to transcribe or replicate, histones must unpack to expose DNA. Thus histone packing/unpacking regulates access to genes, making histones key regulators of gene expression.
</details>

4. A gene is on the reverse strand of a chromosome. In which direction does transcription proceed, and where is its promoter?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Transcription always goes 5'→3'. For a reverse-strand gene, this means transcription proceeds from right to left along the chromosome. The promoter is upstream of the gene's 5' end, so for a reverse gene it lies on the right side (immediately before the transcribed region). It is not part of the transcribed gene.
</details>

5. During DNA replication, why must one strand be synthesized in fragments rather than continuously?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Replication always proceeds 5'→3'. The two template strands are antiparallel, so one strand (the lagging strand template) must be copied in the direction away from the replication fork, which requires synthesizing short fragments that are later joined. The other strand can be copied continuously 5'→3'.
</details>

6. A mutation changes a codon from UUU to UUC. What is the effect on the protein, and what property of the genetic code explains this?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
No effect: both UUU and UUC code for phenylalanine. This is due to the redundancy (degeneracy) of the genetic code, which means some mutations do not change the amino acid and the protein remains identical.
</details>

7. Compare bacterial and human transcription/translation in terms of gene arrangement and RNA processing.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
In bacteria, several related genes can be transcribed together into one polycistronic RNA and translated simultaneously to produce multiple proteins (an operon). In eukaryotes, a single gene produces a pre-mRNA that must be processed (5' cap, poly-A tail, splicing of introns) before it leaves the nucleus and is translated. Humans have introns/exons and alternative splicing; bacteria generally do not.
</details>

8. Explain how one human gene can produce more than one distinct protein.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Alternative splicing: different combinations of exons from a single pre-mRNA are included or excluded, producing multiple mature mRNA variants, each of which can be translated into a different protein sequence (e.g., one transcript may skip exon 3, another exon 4).
</details>

9. In an mRNA, what are the 5' UTR, CDS, and 3' UTR, and which parts are translated?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The 5' UTR and 3' UTR are untranslated regions at the beginning and end of the mRNA; they contain regulatory information (e.g., binding sites, stability control). Only the central CDS is translated into protein, always starting with an AUG (methionine) codon and ending with a stop codon (UAA, UAG, or UGA).
</details>

10. Why is it misleading to say the human genome has 60,000 protein-coding genes, based on the numbers given in class?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The professor gave ~60,000 genomic regions that can be transcribed into RNA and ~20,000 that are translated into proteins. This means most transcribed genes do not code for protein; only ~1.5% of the genome is translated into mRNA/protein, while >90% produces other functional RNAs.
</details>

11. Given a DNA sequence 5'-ATGCA-3', write the complementary strand with its orientation.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
A→T, T→A, G→C, C→G, A→T, and because the strands are antiparallel, the complement runs 3'→5': 3'-TACGT-5'. (Written left-to-right as 5'-TGCAT-3' it would be the same strand in the opposite orientation.)
</details>

12. A protein translation starts at AUG (methionine) and ends with a stop codon. Why is the stop codon not represented as an amino acid in the protein sequence?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Stop codons (UAA, UAG, UGA) do not code for any amino acid; they signal the translational machinery to terminate and release the polypeptide. Therefore they are present in the mRNA/CDS but not in the final protein sequence.
</details>

### 03. BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251013 — Mon 13 Oct 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/06_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251013-20251013_171518-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/06_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251013-20251013_171518-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/06_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251013-20251013_171518-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary

This session continued the cell biology block by moving from the plasma membrane into the **cytoplasm** and **nucleus**, then began the U4-2 organelle slides. The professor explained that the cytosol is a dense protein-packed fluid where metabolism and signalling occur, described the three cytoskeletal filament systems and their molecular motors, then covered nuclear structure, chromatin, and the flow of genetic information via replication, transcription and translation. The second half introduced protein synthesis by ribosomes (with rRNA and tRNA), chaperone-assisted folding, proteasome-mediated protein degradation, and vesicular trafficking through exocytosis and endocytosis.

- Cytosol is not simple water; it is a dense mesh of enzymes, cytoskeleton and other proteins where most metabolic and signalling reactions take place.
- The three cytoskeletal systems differ in composition, diameter, location and transport ability: microfilaments and microtubules transport, intermediate filaments are only structural.
- The nucleus has a double membrane with pores and stores DNA packaged as chromatin; replication and transcription occur inside, translation occurs in the cytosol.
- Ribosomes are RNA-based machines; rRNA and tRNA are essential for decoding mRNA into protein, and chaperones help new proteins fold correctly.
- The proteasome degrades tagged, non-functional or unneeded proteins; failure of degradation is linked to neurodegenerative diseases.
- Vesicular trafficking uses membrane compartments (ER, Golgi, vesicles, plasma membrane) to move proteins and other components into and out of the cell via exocytosis and endocytosis.

#### Key concepts

- **Cytoplasm** [00:00:57]: Everything between the plasma membrane and the nucleus; contains the cytosol and organelles.
- **Cytosol** [00:00:57]: The liquid portion between organelles; a dense environment full of proteins, enzymes, cytoskeleton and other molecules; site of many chemical reactions.
- **Organelles** [00:00:57]: Structures, most of them membrane-delimited, that perform particular functions inside the cell.
- **Metabolism** [00:03:39]: The whole set of chemical reactions taking place inside the cell; divided into catabolic and anabolic pathways.
- **Catabolic pathways** [00:05:19]: Reactions that break large/high-molecular-weight molecules into smaller pieces, producing energy and small building blocks.
- **Anabolic pathways** [00:06:30]: Reactions that build larger molecules from building blocks; they require energy.
- **Signalling pathways** [00:06:30]: Intracellular reaction cascades activated when an extracellular molecule binds to a transmembrane protein and the information—not the molecule itself—is transferred across the membrane.
- **Cytoskeleton** [00:09:04]: Dynamic filamentous structures formed by protein subunits; provides structural support and, in some types, tracks for transport.
- **Molecular motors** [00:14:40]: Proteins that move specific cargoes along cytoskeletal tracks; myosin, kinesin and dynein.
- **Nuclear envelope** [00:19:16]: The double membrane (inner and outer) that borders the nucleus, with a perinuclear space between the two membranes.
- **Nuclear pore** [00:20:33]: Specialized structure that regulates movement of substances between cytosol and nucleus.
- **Chromatin** [00:21:05]: Complex of DNA with proteins, mainly histones, that packages DNA inside the nucleus.
- **Euchromatin / heterochromatin** [00:22:39]: Less-compacted / more-compacted forms of chromatin.
- **Replication** [00:22:39]: Duplication of genomic DNA inside the nucleus before cell division.
- **Transcription** [00:24:52]: Synthesis of RNA using DNA as a template, inside the nucleus.
- **Translation** [00:24:52]: Synthesis of proteins using the information in messenger RNA, in the cytosol.
- **Ribosome** [00:30:32]: Large catalytic complex, made mainly of ribosomal RNA, that synthesises proteins.
- **Chaperones** [00:34:34]: Proteins that stabilise and protect unfinished polypeptides, then allow correct final 3D folding.
- **Proteasome** [00:37:02]: Complex/organelle that recognises and degrades tagged proteins into pieces, generating reusable amino acids.
- **Exocytosis** [00:42:57]: Process by which vesicle contents are released to the plasma membrane/exterior.
- **Endocytosis** [00:42:57]: Process by which substances are internalised from the extracellular space into vesicles.

#### Detailed notes

##### Cytoplasm and cytosol [00:00:57]

Cytoplasm is defined as everything between the plasma membrane and the nucleus. It has two main parts:

- **Cytosol**: the liquid portion.
- **Organelles**: internal structures, most of them also membrane-delimited, that carry out particular functions.

The cytosol is the liquid found between organelles, and it is where many chemical reactions occur during the life of the cell. The professor compared the cell to a factory in which many chemical reactions take place simultaneously [00:02:31].

Much of this chemistry requires enzymes, so the cytosol contains many dissolved proteins. It also contains the **cytoskeleton**, proteins that maintain cell structure and transport organelles and substances across the cell [00:02:31].

The professor stressed that the cytosol is not “water and that’s it”: it is a dense mesh of proteins, as shown in the molecular-representation slide [00:03:39]. These proteins include enzymes that regulate and perform metabolic reactions, plus structural and transport proteins.

##### Metabolism: catabolic and anabolic pathways [00:03:39]

The entire set of chemical reactions inside the cell is called **metabolism**. It is divided into two main types:

- **Catabolic reactions/pathways** [00:05:19]:
  - Convert high-molecular-weight molecules into smaller pieces.
  - Tend to produce smaller molecules and **energy**.
  - Some energy is released as heat; some is used by the cell for other purposes.
  - Produce building blocks, e.g. a protein processed into amino acids that can be reused to build new proteins.

- **Anabolic reactions/pathways** [00:06:30]:
  - Build larger molecules from smaller building blocks.
  - Require energy.

The professor showed a metabolic map: each point is a compound that can be converted into another using an enzyme. Many metabolic pathways exist inside the cell, and most—but not all—occur in the cytosol [00:06:30].

##### Signalling pathways in the cytosol [00:06:30]

Another kind of chemical reaction occurs in the cytosol: **signalling pathways**. Plasma membrane proteins that cross the membrane can bind a molecule on the outside. What is transferred across the membrane is **not the molecule itself, but the information** that binding has occurred [00:06:30–00:07:53].

That binding activates a series of intracellular chemical reactions: the signalling pathway. Signalling pathways are related to, but distinct from, metabolic pathways, and they also happen in the cytosol.

##### Cytoskeleton: the three filament types [00:09:04]

The cytoskeleton is made of **dynamic filamentous proteins**: protein subunits attach one after another to form long multimeric filaments. There are three types:

1. **Microfilaments** [00:09:04–00:10:57]:  
   - Made of subunits of the protein **actin**.
   - Thin: **9 nm** in thickness.
   - Typically found just below the plasma membrane.
   - Help maintain cell shape, especially in protrusions such as **intestinal microvilli**.
   - Also move organelles and substances over short distances near the membrane.

2. **Intermediate filaments** [00:10:57]:  
   - Slightly thicker: **10 nm**.
   - Composition varies: different proteins depending on the cell type.
   - Function is **purely structural**; never related to transport.
   - Found throughout the whole cytosol, maintaining the entire internal distribution of the cell.

3. **Microtubules** [00:12:09]:  
   - Made of the protein **tubulin** in **alpha and beta** subunits.
   - Form a tubular structure made of **13 protofilaments**, with a hollow inner part.
   - Have both structural and transport roles, particularly transport of organelles.
   - In many cells, microtubules are organised from a single point, the **centrosome** (the professor corrected himself when initially calling it a “nucleus”; it is the microtubule-organising centre, not the cell nucleus).

##### Molecular motors and intracellular transport [00:13:31]

Only microfilaments and microtubules have transport roles; intermediate filaments do not. The filaments act as **roads/tracks** for moving organelles. To actually move a cargo (a vesicle, a mitochondrion, etc.), the cell needs additional proteins called **molecular motors** [00:14:40].

- **Myosin motors**: move organelles along **microfilaments**.
- **Kinesin motors**: move organelles along **microtubules** in one direction—from the **minus end to the plus end** (microtubules have polarity).
- **Dynein motors**: also use **microtubules**, but move in the **other direction**.

The system is complex because motors can be specific for cargo (e.g. a vesicle vs. a mitochondrion) and for the cytoskeletal type/direction. All this movement requires **energy**, produced by the cell [00:17:11].

##### The nucleus: structure and exceptions [00:17:11]

Human cells typically have **one nucleus per cell**, but there are exceptions:

- **Erythrocytes** (red blood cells) have **no nucleus**.
- **Polynucleated cells** (cells with more than one nucleus) exist; they typically form by **fusion of previously uninucleated cells** [00:18:15].

In the typical case, all the cell’s DNA/genetic material is contained in the nucleus. The nucleus is limited by the **nuclear envelope** [00:19:16]:

- It is a **double membrane** (inner and outer), similar in nature to the plasma membrane.
- Between inner and outer membranes there is a **space** that the cell uses.
- **Nuclear pores** are specialised structures that regulate movement of substances from cytosol to nucleus and vice versa [00:20:33].
- Inside the nucleus there may be a specialised region, the **nucleolus**, whose specific function is the **synthesis of ribosomes** [00:20:33–00:21:05].

##### Chromatin and DNA packaging [00:21:05]

Inside the nucleus, genetic information is stored as **DNA**:

- DNA is **double-stranded and linear in eukaryotes**.
- It is maintained as more than one molecule: chromosomes.
- Human cells have **46 chromosomes**.
- DNA interacts with specialised proteins, especially **histones**, to form **chromatin**; this stabilises and packages DNA.
- Differences in compaction create:
  - **Euchromatin**: less compacted regions.
  - **Heterochromatin**: more compacted regions.

##### Replication, transcription and translation [00:22:39]

These are the central processes for genetic information:

- **Replication** [00:22:39]: When a mother cell divides into two daughter cells, the genomic material must be duplicated. Replication produces a second copy of the DNA, and occurs **inside the nucleus**.
- **Transcription** [00:24:52]: The cell uses the DNA sequence as a pattern to synthesise **RNA**. This occurs **inside the nucleus**. Transcription creates different RNA types, the most abundant being **messenger RNA (mRNA)**.
- **Translation** [00:24:52]: mRNAs leave the nucleus through the pores, arrive at the cytosol, and in specialised areas the information in the RNA is used to synthesise proteins.

The professor stressed that **proteins are the most important functional products of the cell**: they are needed for replication, transcription and translation themselves [00:24:52].

##### Protein synthesis: ribosomes, RNAs and chaperones [00:28:23]

Protein synthesis consists of joining monomeric **amino acids** into a long polymeric chain. This complex process occurs in specialised places called **ribosomes** [00:29:28–00:30:32].

The **ribosome** is a large catalytic complex made mainly from **RNA**—the main example of RNA acting as a functional product itself [00:30:32]:

- **Messenger RNA (mRNA)** contains the information for protein synthesis.
- **Ribosomal RNA (rRNA)** forms the large and small ribosomal subunits and helps mRNA create proteins. The catalytic/functional core is RNA, not protein.
- **Transfer RNA (tRNA)** helps decode information from nucleotides into amino acids: every **three nucleotides** are linked to **one specific amino acid** [00:31:56–00:33:06].

Amplification [00:33:06]: from one gene, many mRNA molecules can be synthesised; from each mRNA molecule, many protein molecules can be synthesised. The professor showed an electron microscopy image of a single mRNA with multiple ribosomes along it, each ribosome producing a growing protein chain; at the end of the mRNA, the protein is almost complete.

**Chaperones** [00:34:34] are specialised proteins that help during synthesis:

- They stabilise/protect the unfinished protein until the ribosome finishes.
- Once synthesis is complete, chaperones allow the protein to fold into its correct final **3D structure**.
- This is especially important for proteins whose 3D structure requires bringing together the initial portion and the last portion of the chain; without chaperones, such complex proteins could not fold properly.

##### Protein degradation: the proteasome [00:36:01]

Not all newly formed proteins fold correctly, and even correctly folded proteins may later lose function or become unnecessary. They need to be degraded [00:37:02].

If non-functional proteins are not degraded and accumulate, they can create **pathology**. The professor gave the example of **many neurodegenerative diseases** [00:37:02].

The organelle responsible for protein degradation is the **proteasome**:

- It is formed by proteins that recognise proteins to be eliminated.
- Proteins destined for degradation are **tagged with specific labels** via chemical reactions during the life of the protein.
- The label is recognised by the proteasome; the protein is attached and degraded, cut into pieces.
- This generates new **amino acids** that can be reused or used for energy [00:37:02–00:39:25].

The process is well regulated: it must avoid degrading useful proteins while ensuring degradation of all unuseful proteins.

##### Vesicular trafficking: exocytosis and endocytosis [00:39:25]

Many cell components must move from one side of the cell to the other. For example, proteins made in ribosomes need to reach the plasma membrane. To control movement and membrane composition, the cell uses membrane-bound compartments [00:39:25–00:41:49]:

- **Endoplasmic reticulum**
- **Golgi apparatus**
- **Vesicles**
- **Plasma membrane**

These compartments ensure delivery of components to the plasma membrane and uptake of components from the plasma membrane for use or degradation [00:41:49].

Two opposing processes were introduced:

- **Exocytosis** [00:42:57]: components contained in vesicles arrive at and fuse with the plasma membrane, releasing their contents to the outside. This can be used for signalling or for producing components delivered to the extracellular space.
- **Endocytosis** [00:42:57]: components from the extracellular space are taken up into the cell as vesicles.

Both processes change the membrane composition of the cell and exchange substances between the inside and outside.

The professor previewed that the next session would discuss the role of exocytosis/endocytosis in moving substances across the cell, more membrane organelles, non-membrane organelles, and mitochondria [00:44:11].

#### What the professor stressed

- The cytosol is **not just water**: it is a dense mesh of proteins, enzymes, cytoskeleton and other components [00:03:39].
- The three cytoskeletal filaments have distinct characteristics; **intermediate filaments are purely structural and never transport**, whereas microfilaments and microtubules also transport [00:10:57–00:13:31].
- Movement of organelles on cytoskeletal tracks **requires energy** [00:17:11].
- **Proteins are the most important functional products** of the cell; they are needed for replication, transcription and translation themselves [00:24:52].
- Protein synthesis includes several RNAs: **mRNA, rRNA and tRNA**, with rRNA acting as a catalytic/functional RNA [00:30:32].
- Gene-to-protein is an **amplification process**: one gene → many mRNA molecules → many protein molecules per mRNA [00:33:06].
- **Chaperones are essential** for correct 3D folding of complex proteins [00:34:34].
- Failure to degrade non-functional proteins can produce pathology, for example in **neurodegenerative diseases**; proteasome degradation is tightly regulated [00:37:02].
- Exocytosis and endocytosis both modify **plasma membrane composition** and exchange substances between inside and outside [00:42:57].

#### Course logistics mentioned

No exam, deadline, grading or group information was discussed in this session.

#### Self-test

1. Why should the cytosol be thought of as a dense mesh of proteins rather than simply water?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Because it contains many dissolved enzymes that carry out metabolic reactions, signalling proteins, and the cytoskeleton that maintains cell structure and transports organelles. The professor stressed that the cytosol is full of proteins, not just a watery compartment.
</details>

2. Distinguish catabolic and anabolic pathways in terms of molecular size and energy.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Catabolic pathways break large/high-molecular-weight molecules into smaller pieces and produce energy (some as heat, some usable building blocks such as amino acids). Anabolic pathways build larger molecules from smaller building blocks and require energy.
</details>

3. Compare microfilaments, intermediate filaments and microtubules: subunit, diameter, location and transport role.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Microfilaments: actin subunits; 9 nm; just below the plasma membrane and in protrusions such as intestinal microvilli; structural and short-distance transport near the membrane. Intermediate filaments: composition varies by cell type; 10 nm; throughout the cytosol; purely structural, never transport. Microtubules: alpha/beta tubulin; 13 protofilaments forming a hollow tube; structural and long-distance organelle transport, usually organised from the centrosome.
</details>

4. How do molecular motors use the cytoskeleton, and what distinguishes myosin, kinesin and dynein?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Cytoskeletal filaments act as tracks/roads. Molecular motors bind specific cargoes (e.g. a mitochondrion or vesicle) and move them along those tracks. Myosin uses microfilaments. Kinesin uses microtubules and moves from the minus end to the plus end. Dynein also uses microtubules but moves in the opposite direction. The movement requires energy.
</details>

5. What are the exceptions to the “one nucleus per cell” rule mentioned in class?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Erythrocytes (red blood cells) have no nucleus. Polynucleated cells have more than one nucleus and are typically formed by fusion of previously uninucleated cells.
</details>

6. Why is the nuclear envelope described as a double membrane, and what is the function of nuclear pores?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The nuclear envelope has an inner and an outer membrane with a space between them. Nuclear pores are specialised structures that regulate movement of substances between the cytosol and the nucleus.
</details>

7. Trace the flow of genetic information from DNA to protein, naming each process and where it occurs.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
DNA is replicated inside the nucleus before cell division. During transcription, RNA is synthesised in the nucleus using DNA as a template; the most abundant product is mRNA. mRNA exits through nuclear pores into the cytosol. During translation, ribosomes in the cytosol use the mRNA information to synthesise proteins.
</details>

8. What is the role of chaperones during protein synthesis, and why are they needed for complex proteins?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Chaperones stabilise and protect unfinished proteins until the ribosome completes synthesis, then allow the protein to fold into its correct 3D structure. They are needed because some proteins form complex structures that require the beginning and end of the chain to come together; without chaperones, such proteins could not fold correctly.
</details>

9. How does the proteasome decide which proteins to degrade, and why is correct protein degradation important?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Proteins destined for degradation are tagged with specific labels through chemical reactions during their lifetime. The proteasome recognises the label, attaches the protein, and cuts it into pieces, generating amino acids that can be reused or used for energy. Correct degradation is essential because accumulation of non-functional proteins can cause pathology, e.g. in many neurodegenerative diseases.
</details>

10. Compare exocytosis and endocytosis and explain their effect on the plasma membrane.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Exocytosis delivers vesicle contents to the plasma membrane and releases them outside; endocytosis takes extracellular components into the cell as vesicles. Both change the membrane composition of the cell and exchange substances between the inside and outside, using membrane compartments such as the ER, Golgi, vesicles and plasma membrane.
</details>

11. Explain why protein production from a single gene is considered an amplification process.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
One gene can be transcribed into many mRNA molecules, and each mRNA molecule can be translated by multiple ribosomes at the same time. Thus, from one gene, many copies of the protein can be produced.
</details>

12. How does signalling across the plasma membrane differ from transporting the signalling molecule itself into the cell?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
In signaling pathways, an extracellular molecule binds to a transmembrane protein. What crosses the membrane is the information that binding has occurred, not the molecule itself. This binding then activates a series of intracellular chemical reactions in the cytosol.
</details>
