### 04. BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251020 — Mon 20 Oct 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/07_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251020-20251020_171145-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/07_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251020-20251020_171145-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/07_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251020-20251020_171145-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary

This session completed the overview of membrane-bound organelles: the endoplasmic reticulum (rough and smooth), Golgi apparatus, endosomes, lysosomes and peroxisomes, and how they move material through the cell. The second half covered mitochondria in depth: its double-membrane structure, endosymbiotic origin, maternal inheritance, and central role in bioenergetics via the citric acid cycle and electron transport chain. The final part introduced cell functions, starting with cell division: the cell cycle phases, the need to duplicate cellular contents and organelles, and the checkpoints that control division.

- Rough ER synthesises plasma membrane proteins; smooth ER makes lipids and stores calcium.
- Vesicle traffic is bidirectional: ER → Golgi → cell surface, and plasma membrane → endosomes → lysosomes.
- Mitochondria originated by symbiosis, retain a 37-gene genome, and are inherited only from the mother.
- Cells cannot “burn” glucose in one step; mitochondria complete glucose oxidation and produce most ATP via the inner membrane.
- The cell cycle is divided into interphase (G1, S, G2) and M phase; non-dividing cells such as most neurons exit into G0.
- Cell division is controlled by checkpoints: the restriction point before S, a pre-M checkpoint for DNA replication, and the metaphase–anaphase transition.

#### Key concepts

- **Endomembrane system**: the interconnected set of membrane-limited compartments (ER, Golgi, vesicles, endosomes, lysosomes) used to synthesise, process, transport and degrade cellular material.
- **Rough endoplasmic reticulum (RER)**: ER with attached ribosomes where plasma membrane proteins are synthesised.
- **Smooth endoplasmic reticulum (SER)**: ribosome-free ER involved in lipid synthesis, metabolic pathways and calcium storage.
- **Golgi apparatus**: organelle made of stacked disc-like sacs that receives proteins/lipids from the ER, continues their processing and dispatches them onward.
- **Endosome**: vesicle formed by endocytosis from the plasma membrane.
- **Lysosome**: degradative vesicle containing enzymes and low pH; it degrades extracellular material and worn-out cell components.
- **Peroxisome**: small vesicular compartment containing enzymes specialised in oxidation reactions.
- **Mitochondrion**: double-membrane organelle with its own genome; the main site of aerobic energy production.
- **Endosymbiosis**: origin of mitochondria by stable uptake of a prokaryotic organism into a pre-eukaryotic cell.
- **Mitochondrial inheritance**: maternal-only transmission of mitochondrial DNA through the oocyte.
- **Citric acid cycle / Krebs cycle**: mitochondrial matrix pathway that converts pyruvate to CO2 and generates energy carriers.
- **Electron transport chain**: inner mitochondrial membrane protein system that transfers electrons, pumps protons, and drives ATP synthase.
- **Cell cycle**: ordered sequence of growth, DNA replication and division: G1, S, G2, M.
- **Checkpoint**: control mechanism ensuring the cell only proceeds when conditions are adequate and previous steps are completed correctly.
- **Mitosis**: somatic/non-reproductive cell division producing two daughter cells genetically equal to the mother cell.

#### Detailed notes

##### Membrane organelles and intracellular trafficking

Membrane organelles communicate and transfer contents between different cell compartments by forming vesicles. Material can enter the cell by endocytic uptake into vesicles, or be exported by exocytosis. This allows the cell to move membranes, extracellular material, synthesised proteins and lipids from one compartment to another [00:01:12].

Trafficking is bidirectional. In one direction, proteins are synthesised in the ER, processed in the Golgi, and transported by vesicles to the plasma membrane. In the other direction, the plasma membrane forms endosomes that can fuse with lysosomes for degradation [00:12:42]. This means the cell can precisely control what is present on its surface: adding material when needed, and taking up/reducing surface components or extracellular material when needed [00:12:42].

##### Endoplasmic reticulum

The endoplasmic reticulum is a network of interconnected membrane sacs connected to the nuclear envelope [00:01:12]. It is not uniform: some regions have ribosomes attached, others do not. This defines two functional types [00:02:33]:

- **Rough endoplasmic reticulum (RER)**: contains ribosomes. All proteins destined to be added to the plasma membrane are synthesised here [00:03:42].
- **Smooth endoplasmic reticulum (SER)**: lacks ribosomes. It performs lipid production and other metabolic pathways, and is important as a calcium reservoir. The professor gave the example of muscle cells: calcium stored in the smooth ER is needed for muscle contraction, which will be revisited when discussing skeletal and cardiac muscle [00:03:42–00:04:58].

##### Golgi apparatus and the secretory pathway

The Golgi apparatus is not one single compartment but an organelle composed of organised stacks of disc-like sacs lying close together [00:05:13–00:06:28]. It receives proteins and lipids by vesicle trafficking from the ER, continues their processing—for example further lipid modifications or protein processing—and then passes them onward toward the plasma membrane [00:06:28].

The professor traced the path of a membrane protein: it is synthesised in the ER, removed in a vesicle, travels to the Golgi, moves from one Golgi sac to another by repeated vesicle formation and fusion, undergoes processing, and finally travels in a new vesicle to the cell surface, where it is released by exocytosis [00:07:29].

##### Endosomes, lysosomes and peroxisomes

Endosomes are vesicles formed during endocytosis: the plasma membrane invaginates, pinches off and forms an internal vesicle [00:08:54]. Some endosomes contain material that must be degraded.

Lysosomes are vesicles that contain mainly degradative enzymes and extreme conditions such as low pH. When an endosome fuses with a lysosome, or becomes a lysosome, its contents are degraded [00:10:19]. This is important in cells specialised in taking up bacteria or other dangerous materials from outside the cell. Lysosomes also degrade internal cell components that are no longer functional: an old organelle can be encapsulated into a vesicle, fused with a lysosome and then degraded [00:10:19–00:11:30].

Peroxisomes are small vesicular membrane compartments containing enzymes specialised in oxidation reactions. They have a much more particular, oriented function than the large ER and Golgi organelles [00:11:30].

##### Mitochondria: structure and origin

Mitochondria are membrane organelles with a distinctive double-membrane structure. Electron microscopy shows clear outer limits, but the organelle has two membranes [00:13:51–00:14:54]:

- The **outer membrane** is rounder.
- The **inner membrane** forms many invaginations and is much larger than the outer membrane.
- Between the two membranes is the **intermembrane space**.
- Inside the inner membrane is the **matrix** [00:14:54–00:16:16].

The two membranes have similar general composition—phospholipid bilayers with proteins—but their specific composition and proteins differ, giving them different functions. The inner membrane has more proteins and a larger surface area [00:16:16].

Mitochondria are the only organelle in animal cells with this double-membrane structure. The professor explained this by endosymbiosis: in advanced stages of evolution, a pre-eukaryotic cell with a nucleus took up a prokaryotic organism by endocytosis. The prokaryote stayed alive inside the larger cell, creating mutual dependence: the larger cell received useful materials produced by the mitochondrion, and the mitochondrion benefited from the protected environment. This was a “win-win” symbiosis selected by evolution [00:17:18–00:18:30].

##### Mitochondrial genome and inheritance

Because mitochondria originated from an independent prokaryotic organism, they still contain their own genomic material. The mitochondrial genome contains **37 genes** required for the whole cell to be alive [00:18:30].

Mitochondrial DNA is inherited only through the **oocyte**, not through the spermatozoid. Therefore the female progenitor is responsible for transmitting mitochondria and their genes to the next generation [00:19:43].

Consequence: if a woman has a pathogenic mutation in a mitochondrial gene, all her progeny will inherit the same alteration. If the mitochondrial mutation is in the male progenitor, none of the progeny will inherit it, because spermatozoids do not contribute mitochondria to the embryo [00:19:43–00:20:49]. This pattern is called **mitochondrial inheritance**.

##### Mitochondrial bioenergetics

The main role of mitochondria is bioenergetics: extracting as much energy as possible from chemical substances such as glucose [00:20:49].

If glucose is burned directly in oxygen, it releases a large amount of energy as it is converted to CO2 and water. However, this direct burning requires a very large activation energy, which is not possible inside a living cell. Cells therefore modify glucose slightly in many small steps. Each step has a much lower activation energy, and the total energy released is the same as direct burning [00:22:17–00:23:35].

Without mitochondria, the cell can only convert glucose to **pyruvate**, so it never extracts all the possible energy. With mitochondria, the final, largest part of the process can occur, because the complete oxidation of glucose takes place inside mitochondria [00:23:35–00:24:47].

Inside the mitochondrion:

1. **Pyruvate** enters the mitochondria and enters the **citric acid cycle** (Krebs cycle) in the matrix.
2. This multi-step chemical network converts pyruvate into **CO2** and generates energy.
3. The cell captures this energy as **ATP**, the molecule cells use to store and transfer energy for chemical reactions.
4. Pyruvate enters; **CO2, water and ATP** are produced. ATP can leave the mitochondria and be used anywhere in the cell [00:25:17–00:26:27].

The ATP-producing part occurs in the **inner mitochondrial membrane**, not in the matrix. The citric acid cycle occurs in the matrix, but the process that yields ATP is embedded in the inner membrane. This is why the inner membrane is so long and folded [00:27:34].

The mechanism is the **electron transport chain**. Proteins in the inner membrane transport electrons. This electron transport helps the proteins pump protons from the matrix into the intermembrane space, creating a proton gradient. Protons then flow back through **ATP synthase**, which produces ATP. The citric acid cycle in the matrix is coupled to electron transport and proton pumping in the membrane, converting pyruvate and oxygen into water, CO2 and ATP [00:27:34–00:30:33].

Because mitochondria are separate membrane-bound compartments, many transport steps are needed. Gases can cross membranes freely, but pyruvate and ATP require additional help to cross the mitochondrial membranes [00:30:33].

Finally, the professor placed the citric acid cycle in the centre of the cell’s metabolic map. The left side of the map represents **catabolism** (degradation of products to generate energy and smaller compounds); the right side represents **anabolism** (synthesis of new products). The citric acid cycle is the final step of catabolism and also the initial point for anabolism because it produces energy, so it plays a central role in cell metabolism [00:31:50–00:32:55].

The professor also noted the biomedical data relevance: measurements of protein alterations are increasingly important in neurodegeneration; metabolite analysis in blood is common; and genomic studies can include analysis of mitochondrial inheritance [00:33:56].

##### Cell division: why duplication must precede division

Cells are able to copy themselves, and the information to do so is contained inside the cell. After division, the two resulting cells are equal in the information they contain [00:36:37–00:37:46].

Before division, a cell must duplicate its contents: the genomic material/chromosomes must be duplicated, and also other cell parts such as organelles. Most organelles **cannot be synthesised de novo**; they must be inherited from the mother cell [00:38:55–00:39:11].

After duplication, there must be a precise division, so each daughter cell receives exactly half of the materials. This is not random but a highly organised separation [00:39:11–00:39:40].

##### Phases of the cell cycle

Cell division is structured into phases [00:39:40]:

- **S phase**: DNA synthesis; duplication of the genomic material. This is the central part of the cycle.
- **M phase**: segregation of the genomic material and division of the cell itself.
- **G1 phase**: growth phase before DNA synthesis.
- **G2 phase**: growth phase just before M phase.

G1, S and G2 together form **interphase**. The cycle can repeat: grow, duplicate DNA, grow more, divide into two, and start again [00:40:37–00:41:47].

Some cells exit the cell cycle and never divide again, for example **most neurons**. These cells are said to be in **G0** [00:41:47].

##### Cell cycle checkpoints and mitosis

The cell cycle is precisely regulated and controlled. The professor described three checkpoints [00:42:52]:

1. **Restriction point / start checkpoint**: before S phase, before the cell commits to division. Once the cell passes this point and starts DNA duplication, it cannot go backwards. The cell requires a favourable environment and adequate conditions for proliferation.
2. **Pre-M checkpoint**: after DNA replication, before separation of the genomic material. Full completion of DNA replication must be confirmed to avoid errors in segregation.
3. **Metaphase-to-anaphase transition**: a checkpoint inside M phase [00:42:52–00:44:37].

For **somatic cell division**—division of any body cell that will not generate spermatozoids or oocytes, i.e. non-reproductive division—the process is **mitosis**. During mitosis, DNA is duplicated so chromosomes contain two copies of the genomic material; these are then segregated so each daughter cell receives one copy, making the daughter cells exactly equal to the mother cell [00:44:37–00:45:40].

#### What the professor stressed

- Mitochondria are “really, really relevant” for understanding bioenergetics; complete glucose oxidation occurs only inside mitochondria [00:24:47].
- The inner mitochondrial membrane is larger because that is where ATP is produced; its folded structure is not incidental [00:27:34].
- Most organelles are not made from scratch; they must be inherited from the mother cell before division [00:38:55].
- Mitochondrial inheritance is strictly maternal: a mother with a mitochondrial mutation passes it to all children; a father with such a mutation passes it to none [00:19:43–00:20:49].
- The citric acid cycle occupies a central position in metabolism: it is the final step of catabolism and the starting point for anabolism [00:31:50–00:32:55].
- The cell cycle has critical checkpoints; the restriction point before S is a point of no return [00:42:52–00:44:00].
- For health data science, mitochondrial genome data, metabolite measurements and protein alteration measurements are relevant biomedical data sources [00:33:56].

#### Course logistics mentioned

None in this session.

#### Self-test

1. Why can the same endoplasmic reticulum contain both rough and smooth regions, and what functional difference does this create?

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
The ER is a continuous membrane network, but ribosomes are attached only in some regions. Regions with ribosomes are rough ER and synthesise plasma membrane proteins. Regions without ribosomes are smooth ER and carry out lipid synthesis, other metabolic pathways and calcium storage.
</details>

2. Trace the route of a newly synthesised plasma membrane protein from its synthesis to the cell surface.

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
It is synthesised on the rough ER, packaged into a vesicle, travels to the Golgi apparatus, moves through Golgi sacs by vesicle formation and fusion while being processed, then travels in a vesicle to the plasma membrane and is released by exocytosis.
</details>

3. Why do lysosomes need both degradative enzymes and low pH?

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
The enzymes break down macromolecules, and the low pH provides the extreme acidic conditions required for their optimal activity. This allows lysosomes to degrade bacteria taken up from outside and old cell components.
</details>

4. How is the structure of the inner mitochondrial membrane related to its role in ATP production?

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
The inner membrane is much larger than the outer membrane and folded into many invaginations. The electron transport chain and ATP synthase are located there, so more membrane surface allows more protein complexes and more ATP production.
</details>

5. A man has a disease caused by a mitochondrial gene mutation. Explain why his children are unaffected, but if his wife had the same mutation all their children would be affected.

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
Mitochondria are inherited only through the oocyte. Spermatozoids do not contribute mitochondria to the embryo, so a father’s mitochondrial mutation is not passed on. The mother’s mitochondrial DNA is inherited by all children, so her mutation would be transmitted to all of them.
</details>

6. Why can’t cells obtain energy from glucose by simply “burning” it in one reaction with oxygen?

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
Direct burning releases a lot of energy but requires a very high activation energy that is not compatible with living cells. Cells instead modify glucose in many small steps, each with a low activation energy, releasing the same total energy in a controlled way.
</details>

7. Where do the citric acid cycle and the electron transport chain take place, and what does each accomplish?

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
The citric acid cycle occurs in the mitochondrial matrix and converts pyruvate into CO2. The electron transport chain occurs in the inner membrane, transports electrons, pumps protons into the intermembrane space, and the resulting proton gradient drives ATP synthase to produce ATP.
</details>

8. Why is the citric acid cycle described as the central point between catabolism and anabolism?

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
It is the final step of catabolism, where degradation products converge, and it produces energy that feeds anabolism, the synthesis of new cell products. Thus it connects the two sides of the metabolic network.
</details>

9. Why must organelles be duplicated or inherited before cell division, rather than synthesised from scratch afterward?

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
Most organelles cannot be synthesised de novo by a cell. Each daughter cell must inherit organelles from the mother cell, so the contents must be duplicated before division and then shared precisely between the two daughter cells.
</details>

10. List the phases of the cell cycle and explain what happens in each. Where does G0 fit?

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
G1: growth before DNA synthesis. S: DNA synthesis/duplication of genomic material. G2: growth and preparation before division. M: segregation of chromosomes and cell division. G1, S and G2 form interphase. G0 is a non-dividing state that some cells, for example most neurons, enter after leaving the cycle.
</details>

11. What are the three checkpoints mentioned in the session, and what does each ensure?

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
1. The restriction point before S ensures conditions are favourable and the cell is committed to division. 2. The pre-M checkpoint ensures DNA replication is fully completed before chromosome segregation. 3. The metaphase-to-anaphase checkpoint controls correct progression within M phase.
</details>

12. What distinguishes somatic cell division (mitosis) from reproductive division as introduced in the session?

<details>
<summary>Answer</summary>
Somatic cell division/mitosis occurs in body cells that will not generate spermatozoids or oocytes. It produces two daughter cells that are not transmitted to the next generation and are genetically equal to the mother cell, each with one copy of the duplicated DNA.
</details>

### 05. BE-Molecular Biology-Session 3 — Thu 23 Oct 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/08_BE-Molecular%20Biology-Session%203.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/08_BE-Molecular%20Biology-Session%203.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/08_BE-Molecular%20Biology-Session%203.md)*

#### Summary
This session covered how the human genome was sequenced and what that sequencing revealed about human genetic variation. It started with a recap of gene structure (exons/introns and alternative splicing in humans vs. bacteria), then moved through the history of DNA sequencing: Sanger sequencing, the public Human Genome Project, the private Celera race, shotgun sequencing strategies, next-generation sequencing, and the collapse in sequencing cost. The second half focused on human genomic variation: SNPs, structural variants, haplotypes, copy-number variants, the HapMap and 1000 Genomes projects, and catalogues such as ExAC/gnomAD. The class ended with a hands-on demonstration of the NCBI Genome database for exploring the human genome and retrieving sequences, plus exercises to practise genome and gene visualisation.

Key takeaways:
- The human reference genome is a representative consensus genome, not the genome of any single individual.
- Sequencing cost fell from ~$3 billion to roughly $500–800 per whole human genome.
- Millions of nucleotide positions differ between humans, but interpreting which variants cause disease is still hard.
- Whole-genome, whole-exome, and targeted sequencing trade off coverage, cost, and speed.
- Haplotypes are blocks of SNPs inherited together, which reduces the number of variants that must be genotyped.
- You must be able to retrieve any sequence, gene, transcript, or protein from public genome databases using tools such as NCBI.

#### Key concepts
- **Central dogma of molecular biology** — the flow of genetic information from DNA to RNA to protein. Not all genes are translated; some are only transcribed into RNA [00:04:09].
- **DNA sequencing** — determining the order of nucleotides (A, T, C, G) in a DNA fragment. RNA can also be sequenced by first converting it to complementary DNA with reverse transcriptase [00:06:26].
- **Sanger sequencing** — the first sequencing method, also called chain-terminating inhibition. Developed in 1977; high accuracy but low throughput [00:08:31].
- **Reference genome** — a representative genome built from multiple individuals, using the most frequent nucleotide at each position. It is not the genome of any real individual [00:23:??].
- **Euchromatin** — loosely packed, gene-rich chromatin accessible to transcription machinery [00:17:03].
- **Heterochromatin** — tightly packed chromatin with few genes, less accessible for transcription [00:17:03].
- **Single nucleotide polymorphism (SNP)** — a single-base difference between individuals at a given genomic position [00:25:22].
- **Structural variant** — a larger DNA difference between individuals, such as an inversion, translocation, duplication, or large deletion [00:29:59].
- **Haplotype** — a block of SNPs that are inherited together because recombination tends not to break the region [00:31:22].
- **Copy-number variation (CNV)** — difference between individuals in the number of copies of a large DNA segment [00:34:17].
- **Whole-genome sequencing (WGS)** — sequencing the complete genome, including exons, introns, and intergenic regions [00:38:29].
- **Whole-exome sequencing (WES)** — sequencing only the exon-containing regions of the genome [00:38:29].
- **Targeted sequencing** — sequencing only a specific known region, such as a disease-associated variant [00:39:??].
- **Pseudogene** — a gene remnant in the genome that is no longer transcribed/translated and currently has no function [01:12:15].

#### Detailed notes

##### 1. Recap: human vs. bacterial gene structure [00:00:00]
The professor recapped the previous session:
- Human genes have exons and introns. Introns are not translated into protein.
- Alternative splicing allows different combinations of exons to generate different RNA molecules from one gene.
- As a result, one human gene can produce multiple mRNA isoforms and multiple protein isoforms of the same protein.
- The isoforms are usually very similar, differing only in some regions (an extra or missing exon). This may affect stability or reaction speed, but the overall three-dimensional structure and function remain essentially the same.
- Bacterial genomes are circular, with mostly coding regions and no large intergenic regions or exon/intron structure.
- In bacteria, one coding gene produces one protein; there is no alternative splicing like in humans.

##### 2. DNA sequencing: definitions and early technology [00:06:26]
- DNA sequencing is the process of determining the sequence of nucleotides in any piece of DNA.
- RNA can also be sequenced by first converting RNA to complementary DNA using reverse transcriptase.
- **Sanger sequencing**
  - Method: chain-terminating inhibition.
  - Developed in 1977.
  - Could produce DNA fragments of more than 500 nucleotides.
  - Very low error rate: 99.9% accuracy.
  - Very slow; splitting the 3 × 10⁹-nucleotide human genome into ~500-nucleotide fragments was impractical at scale.
  - First genome sequenced: a bacteriophage genome in 1977.
  - 1986: semi-automatic sequencing machine using the Sanger method.
  - 1987: fully automatic machine able to sequence 1,000 nucleotides per day.
  - Sanger sequencing could not easily achieve high-throughput sequencing [00:08:31].

##### 3. The Human Genome Project (public consortium) [00:09:39]
- In 1990, a consortium of 20 public research institutes from the USA, France, Germany, UK, Japan, and others started the Human Genome Project.
- Goal: sequence the entire human genome in 15 years, with completion expected by 2005.
- Budget: $3 billion.
- Aim: make all data openly available to the scientific community, clinicians, and researchers, with no restrictions.
- They used DNA samples from eight individuals from different ethnic backgrounds, assuming they would show some nucleotide sequence differences [00:11:06].

##### 4. Celera Genomics and the public–private race [00:12:48]
- Eight years into the public project, the private company Celera Genomics announced it would sequence the human genome in three years.
- Celera’s aims were different:
  - Patent hundreds of genes.
  - Sell gene sequences to pharmaceutical companies.
  - Validate a new sequencing method the company was developing.
- Celera used 150 sequencing machines, 65 technicians, and intensive computing.
- They sequenced five individuals: Chinese, two Caucasian, one African American, and one Hispanic (as transcribed; total five).
- Their data were not open; access was restricted even for researchers.
- The public consortium and Celera entered a race, not sharing information.
- In 2000, both groups published a draft human genome simultaneously in *Nature* and *Science*.
- The draft identified about 26,000 protein-coding genes plus an additional 12,000 genes [00:15:32].

##### 5. Shotgun sequencing strategies [00:14:29]
Two strategies were used to sequence the genome:
- **Whole-genome shotgun sequencing (Celera)**
  - The genome is randomly broken into short fragments.
  - These short fragments are easy to sequence.
  - Because the fragments overlap, computational algorithms are needed to reassemble the original sequence.
  - Assembly is harder because many short reads must be put together correctly.
- **Hierarchical shotgun sequencing (public consortium)**
  - The genome is broken into larger fragments, which are harder to sequence.
  - Because the fragments are much longer and their position is better known, reassembly is easier.
- In both cases, computing was essential to reconstruct the original genome from overlapping pieces.

##### 6. Quality and content of the first draft [00:17:03]
- Error rate: approximately one error every 100,000 bases.
- The draft covered 99% of the euchromatic genome.
  - Euchromatin is loosely packed and gene-rich, so genes are accessible for transcription.
  - Heterochromatin is tightly packed and usually gene-poor because transcription proteins cannot access it easily.
- The draft included about 94% of the genes in the human genome.
- Data were deposited under the Genome Reference Consortium and made available to the community [00:18:03].

##### 7. Next-generation sequencing and the collapse in cost [00:18:03]
- Around 2005, new-generation sequencing methods appeared.
- Costs dropped sharply compared with the HGP era.
- These methods use massively parallel DNA sequencing reactions: thousands to millions of sequences are produced simultaneously.
- Low-cost, high-throughput machines began to be commercialised for individual laboratories.
- The main platforms/methods discussed: Illumina, Roche/454(?), and Thermo Fisher Scientific.
- Important features to compare between sequencing technologies:
  - Read length.
  - Cost per gigabase.
  - Error rate.
- Trade-off: sometimes a slower, more expensive method with fewer errors is preferable depending on the project [00:19:40].

##### 8. Genome sequencing growth and model organisms [00:20:44]
- The original HGP budget was $3 billion.
- Now sequencing an individual whole genome costs roughly $500–800.
- As cost fell, the number of sequenced genomes increased greatly across eukaryotes, prokaryotes, and viruses.
- Timeline examples:
  - 2000: *Drosophila melanogaster*, *Arabidopsis thaliana*, *Mus musculus* (mouse) genomes.
  - 2002: mouse draft; genome browsers made genomes and annotations accessible.
  - 2005: *Pan troglodytes* (chimpanzee) and *Oryza sativa* (rice, first crop genome).
- Annotations include gene positions, sequence variations, and other information known about a genome [00:21:49].

##### 9. HapMap and the need to study variation [00:22:51]
- The reference genome is a representative genome: at each position it contains the nucleotide with the highest frequency in the studied population.
- It is not the genome of any actual individual.
- The HapMap project started to determine common patterns of DNA sequence variation in the human genome.
- Individuals from Africa, Asia, and Europe were sequenced.
- They obtained genotypes for one million sequence variants, showing that different individuals have extensive sequence variability.
- HapMap results were published in 2003 [00:24:12].

##### 10. Types of human genomic variation [00:25:22]
- **Single nucleotide polymorphism (SNP)**
  - Example: individual 1 has A-G-C at a position; individual 2 has A-G-T.
  - One nucleotide is different.
  - SNPs are the most frequent type of variation in the population.
- **Structural variants**
  - Large regions of DNA can differ between individuals.
  - Examples: inversions, translocations, duplications, large deletions, insertions.
- SNPs and structural variants can have different consequences:
  - Some are neutral.
  - Some predispose to complex diseases.
  - Some directly cause monogenic disease.
  - If a variant falls outside a gene, it often has no effect.
  - If it falls inside a coding region, it can change the protein and cause disease [00:28:53].

##### 11. De novo mutations and disease [00:28:53]
- Every newborn carries about 60 new nucleotide changes not present in the parents.
- These arise from unrepaired DNA replication errors.
- Most fall outside genes and do nothing.
- Some fall inside genes and can alter proteins.
- Examples:
  - Eye colour is encoded by gene regions, so changes there can affect phenotype.
  - Many epileptic and rare diseases are caused by de novo variants that affect a functionally important region of a protein [00:29:59].

##### 12. Haplotypes [00:31:22]
- During recombination, some DNA regions are always kept together.
- A haplotype is a specific combination of SNPs that is inherited together.
- Example: if three positions form a haplotype, seeing an A at the first position implies a T and a C at the other positions.
- This means you do not need to genotype every SNP; identifying a few tag SNPs per haplotype lets you infer the rest.
- This reduces the number of variants that must be tested [00:33:16].

##### 13. Copy-number variations (CNVs) [00:34:17]
- Individuals can have different numbers of copies of large DNA regions.
- Example: one individual may have three copies on the maternal chromosome and three on the paternal chromosome; another may have five, one, three, then five copies in a region.
- CNVs are less frequent than SNPs but can also be related to disease.

##### 14. 1000 Genomes Project and population variation [00:35:26]
- Goal: sequence 1,000 individuals and catalogue common genetic variants in the population.
- They obtained 88 million variants, mostly SNPs (84.7 million), plus indels and structural variants.
- Because these are common population variants, most are probably not directly disease-causing.
- It was the first project to sequence the genomes of a large number of people and provide a map of human population variation.
- People were sampled from the five continents.
- Different ethnic and isolated populations have different frequencies of variants.
- Examples of isolated groups: Amish and other populations isolated by religion, geography, or history [00:36:30].

##### 15. Current sequencing strategies [00:38:29]
- **Whole-genome sequencing (WGS)**
  - Sequences the full genome: exons, introns, genic and intergenic regions, from first to last nucleotide.
- **Whole-exome sequencing (WES)**
  - Sequences only exons, the regions most likely to affect protein function and disease.
  - Much cheaper and faster than WGS because it skips introns and intergenic regions.
- **Targeted sequencing**
  - Sequences only a known region of interest, such as a variant associated with breast cancer or celiac disease.
  - Fastest and cheapest; used to test for a specific mutation or genetic predisposition.

##### 16. ExAC and gnomAD catalogues [00:40:07]
- ExAC obtained more than 60,000 exomes and built a catalogue of human genetic variation.
- They observed an average of one variant every eight nucleotides.
- gnomAD is the successor to ExAC.
  - Initially about 125,000 exomes and 15,000 genomes.
  - Version 3: about 70,000 genomes.
  - Version 4: around 300,000 genomes.
- Many worldwide initiatives sequence either disease cohorts or healthy people to understand human variation and disease.

##### 17. Why variant interpretation is complex [00:41:08]
- With millions of differences between any individual and the reference genome, identifying which variant causes disease is difficult.
- A patient’s genome will show millions of differences from the reference.
- The challenge is deciding which of these changes is responsible for the disorder.
- Many algorithms and research efforts try to predict variant pathogenicity.
- Some variants are known to be highly penetrant, e.g. BRCA1/BRCA2 in breast cancer and variants linked to colon cancer.
- For complex diseases, many variants contribute small effects; no single variant explains the disease [00:51:41].

##### 18. Clinical questions: monogenic vs. complex disease, compatibility testing [00:42:27]
- **Carrier testing / couple compatibility**
  - Private companies analyse genomes to estimate risk of recessive disease in offspring.
  - If both parents carry a harmful mutation in the same gene, the child has about a 50% chance of inheriting both bad copies.
  - Interpretation is still incomplete: for many variants we do not yet know if they destroy protein function or cause disease.
  - These tests are often used in families that already have an affected child, to estimate risk for future children.
- **De novo risk**
  - Even if both parents are negative, the baby can still carry new mutations not present in the parents.
  - Germ-cell mutations can occur at any time during life due to chemicals, radiation, etc.
  - It is impossible to analyse all germ cells; only inherited DNA is tested.
- **Somatic mutations**
  - Mutations after birth in non-germ cells are somatic.
  - These often underlie cancer, rather than inherited disease.
- **Monogenic vs. complex diseases**
  - Monogenic disease: a single mutation is enough to cause disease.
  - Complex disease: genetic variants only predispose; environmental factors and other genes are also involved.
  - Celiac disease example: a person may carry risk variants but not develop disease until an environmental trigger, such as a rotavirus infection, triggers it.
- **Preventive medicine**
  - For known high-risk genes (e.g. BRCA1/BRCA2, colon cancer genes), patients can be monitored more closely.
  - For complex diseases, genetic risk can guide screening frequency but does not guarantee disease [00:48:11].

##### 19. NCBI database walkthrough [00:54:01]
Three major centres host genome sequence information:
- NCBI (National Center for Biotechnology Information, USA)
- EBI (European Bioinformatics Institute)
- DDBJ (DNA Data Bank of Japan)

The professor demonstrated the NCBI Genome database:
- Open NCBI, select the **Genome** database, and search.
- The database showed:
  - 2.54 million genome records/assemblies.
  - 38,650 species with a reference sequence.
  - Example counts by group: >18,000 eukaryote genomes, 703 archaea, 19,000 bacteria, 194,000 virus genomes [01:00:08].
- Search for *Homo sapiens*:
  - 1,583 human genome records/releases.
  - Select the reference genome with the green tick.
  - The current reference assembly is from 2022.
  - It is a representative genome, not from any individual [01:02:20].
- On the human genome page:
  - Genome size: 3.1 gigabases (3.1 × 10⁹ nucleotides).
  - About 59,000 genes total.
  - About 20,000 coding genes.
  - Chromosomes 1–22, X, Y, and mitochondrial chromosome are shown with their sizes.
  - NC accessions are from the RefSeq database, a highly curated database with one reference sequence for each gene, genome, and protein; the professor recommended using RefSeq nomenclature [01:04:10].
- Retrieving sequence:
  - Open a chromosome, e.g. chromosome 1.
  - Use the FASTA view to see the nucleotide sequence.
  - Beginning of chromosome 1 contains many Ns, meaning undetermined nucleotides.
  - You can enter a coordinate range (e.g. 100,000–200,000) and update the view to display only that region [01:06:51].

##### 20. Gene annotation in NCBI [01:11:12]
- From the genome page, go to the gene annotation list / “Bionnotate Genes” section.
- The human genome annotation showed 68,325 genes.
- Filtering by gene type:
  - Protein-coding genes: about 23,000.
  - Non-protein-coding genes: about 19,000.
  - Pseudogenes: about 19,000; pseudogenes are gene remnants that are no longer transcribed/translated and currently have no function [01:12:15].
- Genes can be on either DNA strand:
  - Genes on the main strand are transcribed to the right.
  - Genes on the complementary strand are transcribed to the left.
  - Transcription always proceeds 5′ to 3′, regardless of strand.
- Filters can be used to list all genes on a specific chromosome, e.g. chromosome 7, or to count protein-coding genes on a chromosome [01:14:57].

##### 21. Exercises for practise [01:13:52]
- The professor presented exercises on genome and gene visualisation.
- Objectives: explore different genomes, find genes, and retrieve sequences.
- For next class, students should:
  - Check the solved transcription/translation exercises from the previous week.
  - Start the first section of the genome-database visualisation exercises.
- If questions arise, students can contact the professor by email.

#### What the professor stressed
- Understand the structural difference between human and bacterial genomes: exons/introns and alternative splicing explain why one human gene can produce multiple proteins [00:00:00].
- The human reference genome is a consensus, not any individual’s genome [00:23:??].
- Sequencing cost has fallen from $3 billion to roughly $500–800 per genome, which enabled population-scale projects [00:20:44].
- Human genomes are full of variation; the main problem is not finding variants but knowing which variant causes disease [00:41:08].
- Genes can be on either strand, but transcription always goes 5′ to 3′ [01:12:15].
- Use RefSeq accessions and nomenclature when working with sequences [01:06:51].
- By the end of the course, students should be able to extract any sequence, gene, transcript, or protein from public databases [00:53:??].
- Complete the exercises before the next class; database practice is essential for understanding the abstract genome concepts [01:16:??].

#### Course logistics mentioned
- The solved exercises on transcription and translation are now available; students should check their answers and contact the professor with questions [00:02:03].
- There is no class next week because the professor is attending a conference. The next class is in two weeks.
- Students should use the gap to ensure they understand the concepts and complete the genome/gene visualisation exercises.
- If content is missed due to the cancelled session, the professor said she may record additional videos at the end of the course if needed [01:16:??].

#### Self-test
1. Why can one human gene produce multiple protein isoforms, while a typical bacterial gene produces only one protein?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Human genes contain exons and introns. Alternative splicing combines exons in different ways to generate different mRNAs and proteins from the same gene. Bacterial genes generally lack introns, so each coding gene produces one mRNA and one protein.</details>

2. What was the main difference in data-sharing philosophy between the public Human Genome Project and Celera Genomics?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>The public HGP aimed to release all sequence data openly to the scientific community. Celera aimed to patent genes and sell sequence information, keeping data under restriction.</details>

3. Why was Sanger sequencing alone considered too slow for sequencing the whole human genome?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Sanger reads were around 500–1,000 nucleotides, with early automatic machines producing about 1,000 nucleotides per day. The human genome is 3 × 10⁹ nucleotides, so this throughput was far too low without an industrial-scale effort and new methods.</details>

4. Explain the difference between whole-genome shotgun sequencing and hierarchical shotgun sequencing.  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Whole-genome shotgun randomly breaks the entire genome into short fragments, which are easy to sequence but harder to assemble. Hierarchical shotgun uses larger fragments, which are harder to sequence but easier to reassemble because their positions are better known.</details>

5. Why is the reference genome not the genome of a specific person?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>It is a representative assembly built from multiple individuals. At each position, it typically uses the most frequent nucleotide, so it is a consensus rather than one person’s actual sequence.</details>

6. What is a haplotype, and why does it reduce the number of SNPs that need to be genotyped?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>A haplotype is a block of SNPs inherited together because recombination rarely breaks the region. If you genotype a few tag SNPs in the block, you can infer the rest, so not every SNP must be tested.</details>

7. Distinguish a SNP from a copy-number variation.  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>A SNP is a single-base difference between individuals. A copy-number variation is a larger segment of DNA present in different numbers of copies between individuals. SNPs are much more frequent than CNVs.</details>

8. How can a baby have a disease-causing mutation even if both parents are healthy and test negative?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>The mutation can be de novo, arising during gamete formation or early development. Germ-cell mutations can occur at any time due to replication errors or environmental exposure, so they are not present in the parents’ inherited genomes.</details>

9. Compare whole-genome, whole-exome, and targeted sequencing in cost and use.  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Whole-genome sequencing covers everything but is most expensive. Whole-exome sequencing covers only exons and is faster/cheaper while capturing most protein-related variants. Targeted sequencing examines only a specific known region and is the cheapest, used for known disease-associated variants.</details>

10. In a genome browser, a gene is shown on the complementary strand. In which direction is it transcribed?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Transcription always occurs 5′ to 3′. If the gene is on the complementary strand, it is transcribed in the opposite direction relative to the main strand, i.e. from right to left in the browser view.</details>

11. How would you retrieve only positions 100,000–200,000 of chromosome 1 from NCBI?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Open the reference assembly for Homo sapiens, enter chromosome 1, open the FASTA view, enter the coordinate range 100,000–200,000, and update the view.</details>

12. Why are common variants found in the 1000 Genomes Project usually unlikely to directly cause severe monogenic disease?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Because they are common in healthy populations, so they are generally compatible with normal development. Severe monogenic diseases are more often caused by rare or de novo variants with strong functional effects.</details>

### 07. BE-Molecular Biology- Session 4 — Thu 23 Oct 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/10_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%204-20251023_140220-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/10_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%204-20251023_140220-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/10_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%204-20251023_140220-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary

This session was largely a hands-on walkthrough of the NCBI Genome and Gene databases. The professor showed how to locate reference genomes, count genes in a species or chromosome, distinguish coding from non-coding genes, download genome/transcript/protein sequences, and interpret a gene page (strand, exons, UTRs, CDS, canonical transcript, isoforms). A major theme was the difference between eukaryotic and prokaryotic genomes, and how to move between the different sequence “levels”: genome → transcript → CDS → protein.

- Use the NCBI Genome database with the **green-tick reference genome** and **RefSeq** records, not older GenBank records.
- On a gene page, the **canonical transcript is not necessarily the first one listed**.
- All transcription goes **5′ to 3′**, so arrow direction on the gene graphic tells you whether the gene is on the plus or minus strand.
- When extracting sequences, the right database matters: **genomic sequence for promoter/introns**, **transcript/CDS for coding sequence**, **protein record for amino acid sequence**.
- Counting **proteins** from a gene list should not be confused with counting isoforms from alternative splicing.

#### Key concepts

- **Reference genome / RefSeq status** — A curated, reviewed genome represented by a green tick; use the reference genome (one per species) rather than all deposited versions. RefSeq codes include `NC_`, `NM_`, `NP_`.
- **GenBank vs RefSeq** — GenBank is the older sequence database and may contain partial, unreviewed sequences. RefSeq is curated and validated and is the one to work with.
- **Gene database query fields** — Search syntax such as `GRIN2B[Gene] AND Homo sapiens[Organism]` to find the correct gene entry.
- **Gene structure** — A gene consists of exons (coding/UTR parts kept in mature mRNA) and introns (removed by splicing). Untranslated regions: 5′ UTR and 3′ UTR; coding sequence (CDS) is translated into protein.
- **Alternative splicing** — One gene can give several transcripts by joining different exon combinations. These produce protein **isoforms**, still considered the same protein.
- **Canonical transcript** — The most frequently observed/reference transcript used for mutation reporting and standard experiments.
- **Forward vs reverse gene** — Determined by the sense of transcription relative to the reference chromosome. Transcription is always 5′→3′; an arrow pointing left means the gene is on the complementary strand.
- **Promoter** — Upstream (5′) of the gene; for forward genes it lies to the left, for reverse genes to the right.
- **Orthologs** — Genes in different species derived from a common ancestral gene; similar sequence/structure/function.
- **Prokaryotic vs eukaryotic genomes** — Prokaryotic genomes are circular, compact, mostly coding, no introns or large intergenic regions. Eukaryotic genomes have chromosomes, introns/exons, large intergenic regions, many non-coding genes and pseudogenes.

#### Detailed notes

##### NCBI Genome database: finding and choosing a reference genome [00:01:59]

- Start at the NCBI website, select the **Genome** database.
- Search for an organism, e.g. `Homo sapiens`.
- The result list contains many deposited genomes. Select the one with the **green tick** (curated/reviewed reference genome).
- In the reference genome page, the files listed at the bottom represent the different molecules:
  - For **Homo sapiens**, there is one file per chromosome.
  - To download the whole genome, you would need all chromosome files.

##### Counting genes on human chromosome 7 [00:03:37]

- From the *Homo sapiens* reference genome, use **View annotated genes**.
- Filter by genomic location to chromosome 7.
- Results:
  - **Total genes on chromosome 7: 3,475**
  - **Protein-coding genes on chromosome 7: 1,004**
- Total gene count includes coding genes, non-coding genes, and **pseudogenes**.
- Pseudogenes are genes that have lost their function; they remain in the genome but are not transcribed/functional. Mutations can accumulate and eventually erase the information.

##### Alternative splicing and counting proteins [00:06:42]

- In humans, one gene can produce multiple proteins through alternative splicing.
- Different exon combinations generate different transcripts and therefore different **isoforms** of the same protein.
- These isoforms have small variations; some may be less stable or more active, and tissues may express one isoform rather than another.
- Therefore, **1,004 protein-coding genes = 1,004 proteins**, but the number of isoforms would be larger and is not how you count proteins.

##### RefSeq vs GenBank [00:07:48]

- Question asked: what is the difference between the chromosome views from GenBank and RefSeq?
- RefSeq (`NC_` codes) is highly curated and annotated.
- GenBank includes older, sometimes partial or unreviewed sequences.
- Transcript sequences from RefSeq begin with `NM_`, and corresponding proteins begin with `NP_`.
- `NM`/`NP` are the curated, validated sequences to use.

##### Counting mammalian and rodent genomes [00:10:58]

- To count how many mammals have been sequenced:
  - Search `mammalia` in Genome.
  - Total deposited mammalian genomes: **4,448**.
  - But many species have multiple versions, so filter to **reference genomes**: **880 mammals** sequenced.
- Rodents:
  - Filtering to reference genomes gives **156 rodents**.

##### Prokaryotic genome: Staphylococcus aureus [00:13:06]

- Prokaryotic genomes are very different from human:
  - Circular genome, no chromosomes.
  - Coding regions close together; almost no intergenic regions.
  - No intron/exon structure.
  - Very high percentage of genes are protein-coding.
- Human genome has about 60,000 genes, of which roughly 20,000 code for protein; the rest are non-coding RNAs, pseudogenes, etc.
- **Staphylococcus aureus** example:
  - Huge number of deposited genomes, e.g. 118,000 versions, because bacterial genomes are small, fast and cheap to sequence.
  - Reference genome size: **2,821,361 nucleotides**.
  - Total genes: **2,872**.
  - Protein-coding genes: **2,767**.
  - In the annotated view, genes are adjacent on plus and minus strands.
- To download the complete bacterial genome, open the reference record and use **FASTA**.

##### Escherichia coli exercise [00:22:48]

- Search `Escherichia coli K12`, select the reference genome.
- Genome size: **4,641,000 nucleotides**.
- Total genes: **4,651**.
- Protein-coding genes: **4,290**.
- Again, almost all genes are protein-coding, unlike the human genome.

##### NCBI Gene database: searching for a gene [00:26:16]

- The Gene database has one entry per gene per organism.
- You must specify both the gene name and organism, for example:
  - `GRIN2B[Gene] AND Homo sapiens[Organism]`.
- The gene page gives a curated summary: protein-coding status, RefSeq status, organism, orthologs, genomic location, exon count, and coordinates.

##### GRIN2B gene example [00:28:53]

- GRIN2B is a protein-coding gene.
- **Location**: chromosome 12, p arm.
- **Exons**: 15.
- **Coordinates**: on the chromosome 12 sequence `NC_000012`, between two defined coordinate positions.
- GRIN2B is a **reverse gene**, meaning it sits on the complementary strand.

##### Reading gene orientation from arrows [00:32:15]

- The gene graphic shows neighbouring genes and an arrow indicating transcription direction.
- Transcription is **always from 5′ to 3′**.
- Arrow pointing **right** = forward gene on the plus strand.
- Arrow pointing **left** = reverse gene on the complementary strand.
- For a reverse gene:
  - The 5′ end of the gene is on the **right**.
  - The promoter is **upstream of the gene**, so it is on the **right**.
- For a forward gene:
  - Promoter is on the **left**.

##### Transcripts and alternative splicing [00:34:23]

- Below the gene graphic, the page shows several transcript variants.
- For GRIN2B, the professor identified **five transcript variants** produced by alternative splicing.
- Vertical green boxes = exons; intronic sequence is removed in the mRNA.
- Pale green = non-coding regions (UTRs); dark green = coding regions.
- The **canonical transcript** is the most frequent, reference transcript shown at the top of the graphic.
- The transcript contains:
  - **5′ UTR** at the start.
  - **CDS** in the middle.
  - **3′ UTR** at the end.

##### Transcript and protein sequence records [00:42:32]

- Scroll down to the **NCBI Reference Sequence (RefSeq)** table.
- `NM_` codes = transcript/mRNA sequences.
- `NP_` codes = protein sequences.
- Example from the GRIN2B canonical transcript:
  - Transcript length: **30,609 nucleotides**.
  - CDS coordinates: **709–5163**.
  - Therefore:
    - 5′ UTR = positions **1–708**
    - 3′ UTR = positions **5164–end**
  - The CDS is translated into the corresponding protein.
- RNA normally contains U, but in these FASTA files sequences are written with **T instead of U** by convention, to allow direct alignment with DNA.

##### Extracting specific sequence regions [00:46:00]

- Inside a transcript record, you can see exon coordinates:
  - Example GRIN2B exon 1: nucleotides **1–261**
  - Exon 2: **262–690**
- You can use **Change region shown** / **update view** to display any part:
  - CDS only
  - One exon
  - 5′ UTR
- For **promoter region or introns**, you must use the genomic sequence (`NC_`), not the transcript.
- For **CDS/exons**, use the transcript (`NM_`).
- For **protein sequence**, use the protein record (`NP_`).

##### Reverse gene sequence and reverse complement [00:50:41]

- The GRIN2B genomic span in this example is **444,798 bp**.
- When viewing the genomic sequence of a **reverse gene**, using the plus-strand coordinates alone will give the wrong strand.
- Use the option **“show reverse complement”** to obtain the gene sequence in the correct 5′→3′ orientation.

##### RXFP2 exercise [00:54:18]

- Search for `RXFP2` in the Gene database.
- RXFP2 is:
  - A **forward** gene (arrow to the right).
  - Located on chromosome 13.
  - Contains **18 exons**.
  - Has three transcript variants.
- Of the three transcripts, only some are curated:
  - Two are `NM_` curated transcripts.
  - One is `XM_`, meaning computationally predicted, not experimentally validated.
- The **canonical transcript is not the first one listed** in the table; in this case it was the second one.
- Canonical RXFP2 transcript example:
  - Transcript length: **3,336 nucleotides**.
  - CDS: positions **88–2352**.
  - Therefore:
    - 5′ UTR = **1–87**
    - 3′ UTR = **2353–3336**

##### Extracting promoter and gene segments [01:07:45]

- To extract a promoter region of, e.g., **1,000 nucleotides upstream of the gene**:
  1. Open the genomic sequence (`NC_`).
  2. Determine the first nucleotide of the gene.
  3. Use coordinates from `start − 1000` to `start − 1`.
- Example used in class for a forward gene:
  - If the first nucleotide of the gene is **31,739,526**, the 1,000 bp promoter region is **31,738,526–31,739,525**.
- To extract the **first 500 nucleotides of the gene**:
  - For a forward gene, use `start` to `start + 499`.
- The professor stressed that we do not know exactly how long the promoter is; practical extractions often use 3,000 or 5,000 bp upstream depending on the study.

##### Ortholog exercise: LDHC vs DLD [01:15:14]

- Two genes were given for homework:
  - **LDHC** in *Homo sapiens*.
  - **DLD** in *Escherichia coli*.
- They are described as orthologous: derived from a common ancestral gene, similar in sequence/structure/function despite being in very different species.
- Use two browser windows to compare:
  - Genomic sequence structure.
  - Transcript length.
  - Protein length.
  - Number of transcript variants.
  - Coordinates in the genome.
- Use the **zoom tool** to compare the landscape:
  - In *Homo sapiens*, large intergenic regions with no genes.
  - In *E. coli*, genes packed one after another.

#### What the professor stressed

- **Use the green-tick reference genome** and RefSeq curated records (`NC`, `NM`, `NP`).
- **Check that you have the canonical transcript**; it is not always first in the RefSeq table.
- **Transcription is always 5′→3′**; never 3′→5′. Arrow direction tells you the strand.
- **Choose the right sequence level**:
  - Genomic sequence → promoter, introns, full gene region.
  - Transcript sequence → exons, CDS, UTRs.
  - Protein sequence → amino acid sequence.
- **Draw the gene model on paper** to understand coordinates, strand, transcription and translation.
- **Do the database exercises carefully**, especially the LDHC/DLD comparison, because they consolidate genome → transcript → protein concepts.
- Next week there is no class; the session continues in two weeks.

#### Course logistics mentioned

- The previous week’s recording was recovered and posted as “Molecular Biology session 3.”
- The genome/gene database exercises are for homework; complete them carefully.
- Exercise comparing LDHC and DLD should be done using two browser windows and drawings.
- No class the following week; next class in two weeks.
- Questions can be sent to the professor by email.

#### Self-test

1. In the NCBI Genome database, how would you find the number of protein-coding genes on human chromosome 7? What numbers did the professor report?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Select Genome, search Homo sapiens, choose the green-tick reference genome, use View annotated genes, filter to chromosome 7, then filter to protein-coding. The professor reported 3,475 total genes and 1,004 protein-coding genes on chromosome 7.
</details>

2. Why should you not count protein isoforms when asked how many proteins are encoded by a chromosome?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Alternative splicing can produce several isoforms from one gene, but these are variants of the same protein. The number of proteins corresponds to the number of protein-coding genes (in the simple class example, 1,004 proteins for chromosome 7), not the number of isoforms.
</details>

3. What is the difference between an `NM_` transcript and an `XM_` transcript in RefSeq?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
`NM_` transcripts are curated, validated reference transcripts. `XM_` transcripts are computationally predicted and lack experimental validation; they may later be upgraded to `NM_`.
</details>

4. A gene is shown with an arrow pointing to the left. Which DNA strand is it on, and where is its promoter?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
It is on the complementary (reverse) strand because transcription is always 5′→3′. Therefore the promoter is on the right, which is upstream (5′) for that gene.
</details>

5. For the GRIN2B canonical transcript, the CDS is positions 709–5163. What are the 5′ UTR and 3′ UTR positions?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
5′ UTR = positions 1–708. 3′ UTR = positions 5164 to the end of the transcript.
</details>

6. Which NCBI sequence record should you use to extract a promoter region: `NC_` genomic or `NM_` transcript? Why?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Use the `NC_` genomic sequence, because the promoter is upstream of the transcribed region and is not included in the `NM_` transcript.
</details>

7. Which record should you use to download the coding sequence (CDS) of a gene?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Use the `NM_` transcript record and select the CDS coordinates, because the CDS is the joined coding portion of the mature transcript after intron removal.
</details>

8. Why do FASTA files of RNA sequences show T instead of U?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
By convention, nucleotide sequences are written with T so they can be easily aligned to DNA sequences. RNA-specific differences are not shown in these files.
</details>

9. Why does a bacterial genome such as *Staphylococcus aureus* have a much higher percentage of protein-coding genes than the human genome?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Bacterial genomes are compact, circular, mostly coding, with no introns and little intergenic DNA. Human genomes have large intergenic regions, introns, many non-coding genes and pseudogenes.
</details>

10. Is the canonical transcript always the first row in the RefSeq transcript table?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
No. The professor stressed that the canonical transcript can be listed second or later. In the RXFP2 example, the canonical transcript was the second one; always check the gene graphic/table rather than assuming the first row.
</details>

11. How would you extract the first 500 nucleotides of a forward gene?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Open the genomic `NC_` sequence, identify the first nucleotide of the gene, and select coordinates from the start position to start + 499.
</details>

12. What features would you compare between orthologous genes from human and *E. coli* to see how genome organisation differs?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Compare transcript length, protein length, number of transcript variants, genomic coordinates, surrounding gene density, and intron/exon structure. In humans you see large intergenic regions; in *E. coli* genes are packed one after another.
</details>
