### 08. BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251027 — Mon 27 Oct 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/11_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251027-20251027_171118-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/11_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251027-20251027_171118-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/11_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251027-20251027_171118-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary

This session continued the cellular functions topic by going through cell division in detail: the ordered cell cycle, mitosis for somatic cells, and meiosis for gamete formation. It then covered how cells die, contrasting necrosis with programmed cell death (apoptosis), including the caspase cascade and the extrinsic and intrinsic pathways that trigger it. Finally, the professor introduced cell differentiation, explaining how cells with identical genomes become functionally specialised. Key takeaways:

- The cell cycle is regulated at specific checkpoints to ensure faithful DNA replication and chromosome segregation [00:02:51].
- Mitosis gives two diploid daughter cells with identical genomic material; it proceeds through prophase, metaphase, anaphase, telophase, and cytokinesis [00:05:10].
- Meiosis is the reductive division that produces haploid gametes; meiosis I separates homologous chromosomes, and meiosis II separates sister chromatids [00:13:17].
- Apoptosis is a controlled, silent form of cell death that avoids inflammation, unlike necrosis [00:27:08].
- Apoptosis is executed by a proteolytic caspase cascade that can be triggered by external signals (extrinsic pathway) or internal damage such as mitochondrial cytochrome c release (intrinsic pathway) [00:34:59].
- Different cell types arise from the same genome by differential gene expression during differentiation [00:45:17].

#### Key concepts

- **Interphase** – the part of the cell cycle in which the cell grows and replicates its DNA; divided into G1, S, and G2 phases [00:00:00].
- **M phase** – the phase in which duplicated DNA and cell components are segregated into two daughter cells [00:01:46].
- **Checkpoint** – a regulatory point in the cell cycle where the cell can pause or check that processes have been completed correctly [00:02:51].
- **Chromatid** – one copy of a replicated chromosome; two sister chromatids are joined at the centromere before segregation [00:07:39].
- **Centromere** – the region where two sister chromatids are attached [00:07:39].
- **Centrosome** – the microtubule-organising structure at each pole of the mitotic spindle [00:09:36].
- **Metaphase plate** – the equatorial plane where chromosomes align during metaphase [00:09:36].
- **Cytokinesis** – division of the cytoplasm and organelles to fully separate the two daughter cells [00:06:37].
- **Diploid** – having two copies of each chromosome; human somatic cells have 23 pairs, i.e. 46 chromosomes [00:14:24].
- **Haploid** – having one copy of each chromosome; human gametes have 23 chromosomes [00:15:37].
- **Tetrad** – the paired alignment of homologous chromosomes during metaphase I of meiosis [00:20:15].
- **Homologous recombination** – exchange of corresponding segments between homologous chromosomes during meiosis I [00:22:46].
- **Necrosis** – cell death by membrane rupture and uncontrolled release of cell contents, causing inflammation [00:27:08].
- **Apoptosis** – programmed cell death in which the cell shrinks and its contents are degraded internally without inflammation [00:28:17].
- **Caspases** – proteases that cleave specific proteins and execute the apoptotic cascade [00:34:59].
- **Procaspases** – inactive caspase precursors that become activated by cleavage [00:37:46].
- **Death receptors** – cell-surface receptors that trigger the extrinsic apoptosis pathway when bound by extracellular signals [00:39:14].
- **Differentiation** – the process by which cells with the same genome become specialised by using different sets of genes [00:45:17].

#### Detailed notes

##### Cell cycle and checkpoints [00:00:00–00:04:06]

The cell cycle is the ordered mechanism by which cells reproduce. It has two broad parts:

- **Interphase**: cell growth and DNA synthesis. It is subdivided into:
  - **G1 phase**
  - **S phase** – new DNA synthesis/replication
  - **G2 phase**
- **M phase**: segregation of the duplicated DNA and cell components into two daughter cells.

Some cells can exit the cycle and become non-dividing cells. The typical example given by the professor is **neurons**, which may not divide again [00:01:46].

The cell cycle is regulated at specific checkpoints because the cell must ensure that the new cells contain the appropriate genomic material. The professor highlighted three positions:

1. A checkpoint before DNA synthesis (start point) [00:02:51].
2. A checkpoint before chromosome segregation in M phase [00:02:51].
3. Another checkpoint within M phase to regulate the finishing of division [00:02:51].

##### Mitosis [00:04:07–00:13:16]

Somatic cells, which are most of the cells of the body, divide by mitosis. One mother cell produces two daughter cells with the exact same genetic material as the original cell. DNA duplication occurs during interphase; mitosis then segregates the duplicated DNA equally. The professor focused on genomic content, noting that daughter cells may not always be identical in all cellular composition, but they are identical in genomic content [00:05:10].

The phases of mitosis are:

- **Prophase** [00:06:37–00:08:46]:
  - Chromatin condenses into visible chromosomes.
  - Each replicated chromosome consists of **two chromatids** joined at the **centromere**.
  - The nuclear envelope disassembles, releasing chromosomes into the cytoplasm.

- **Metaphase** [00:08:47–00:09:35]:
  - Condensed chromosomes align at the equatorial **metaphase plate**.
  - Each chromatid is oriented toward one of the two poles of the cell.
  - At each pole there is a **centrosome**, from which microtubules arise and attach to chromosomes.

- **Anaphase** [00:09:36–00:10:37]:
  - The centromere linking the two chromatids splits.
  - Each chromatid moves toward one centrosome/pole, pulled by microtubules.
  - After separation, each chromosome consists of only one chromatid.

- **Telophase** [00:10:37–00:11:56]:
  - Chromosomes reach the poles and decondense.
  - New nuclear envelopes assemble around each set of chromosomes.
  - The cell begins to divide.

- **Cytokinesis** [00:11:56–00:13:16]:
  - The cytoplasm is divided, completing separation of the two daughter cells.
  - A **contractile ring** of cytoskeletal structures forms and pinches the cell in the middle.
  - Completion of cytokinesis gives two fully independent daughter cells, each able to enter a new interphase.

##### Meiosis [00:13:17–00:27:08]

Meiosis occurs only for sexual reproduction, producing gametes in the reproductive system (spermatozoa in the testicles and oocytes in the ovaries) [00:13:17].

Human somatic cells are **diploid**: they have 23 pairs of chromosomes, i.e. two copies of chromosome 1, two copies of chromosome 2, and so on. Gametes, however, must be **haploid**, carrying only one copy of each chromosome. Fertilisation combines two haploid gametes to restore a diploid cell that can then divide by mitosis [00:14:24–00:16:44].

Meiosis begins like mitosis: the cell goes through interphase, grows, and replicates its DNA. Therefore, at the start of meiosis each chromosome also contains two chromatids. The difference appears after DNA duplication in how chromosomes are distributed [00:17:56].

###### Meiosis I [00:19:02–00:23:49]

- During **prophase I**, the nuclear envelope disappears and chromosomes condense, each chromosome containing two chromatids.
- During **metaphase I**, chromosomes do not align individually. Instead, homologous chromosomes pair side by side as **tetrads** at the metaphase plate.
- During **anaphase I**, whole homologous chromosomes separate to opposite poles, not individual chromatids. Each chromosome still contains two chromatids because the centromere is not lost.
- After **telophase I and cytokinesis I**, two haploid cells are formed. Each cell has 23 chromosomes, but each chromosome still consists of two chromatids linked at the centromere.

An important event during meiosis I is **homologous recombination** [00:22:46]. Homologous chromosomes aligned at the metaphase plate can exchange segments. This generates new combinations of genes in the next generation. The professor stressed that:

- Recombination does **not** create new gene forms or mutations; it only creates new combinations of existing genes.
- Exchange occurs only between **homologous** chromosomes and homologous regions (e.g. chromosome 1 with the other chromosome 1), not between non-equivalent chromosomes.

###### Meiosis II [00:23:49–00:25:59]

Meiosis II is much more similar to mitosis.

- In each of the two haploid cells from meiosis I, chromosomes align individually at the metaphase plate.
- During anaphase, the two **sister chromatids** of each chromosome separate to opposite poles.
- After telophase and cytokinesis, up to **four haploid gametes** are produced from one original cell.

The professor noted that in the testicles this process produces up to four spermatozoa. In oocytes, the process is more complex and only one of the four possible cells typically reaches the final functional stage [00:25:59].

###### Comparison of mitosis and meiosis [00:25:59–00:27:08]

- Mitosis: one diploid cell with duplicated DNA → **two diploid daughter cells**.
- Meiosis: one diploid cell with duplicated DNA → up to **four haploid gametes**.
- Mitosis resembles **meiosis II** more closely; **meiosis I** is the specific reductive division that generates haploid cells.

##### Cell death: necrosis vs apoptosis [00:27:08–00:30:57]

Cells can die by two very different processes:

1. **Necrosis** [00:27:08–00:28:17]:
   - The cell is damaged or broken, e.g. by physical injury to the cell membrane.
   - Intracellular contents spill into the surroundings.
   - This promotes **inflammatory responses** in the surrounding tissue.
   - It is not the typical, controlled way cells die.

2. **Apoptosis** [00:28:17–00:30:57]:
   - The cell shrinks and condenses.
   - Contents are degraded from the inside, not spilled.
   - Also called **programmed cell death**.
   - During apoptosis: cytoskeleton collapses, nuclear envelope disassembles, chromatin/DNA is broken into fragments, and specific intracellular compartments degrade components.
   - Surrounding cells can engulf and degrade the residual parts.
   - There is **no inflammatory response**, so it is a very organised and silent way of dying.

Apoptosis is necessary for correct physiology. Examples given:

- During development, it eliminates the tissue between fingers and toes to shape the digits [00:30:58].
- It adjusts cell numbers: cells are often overproduced and some must be eliminated, e.g. nerve cells that arrive at their target but are in excess [00:32:14].
- It can eliminate abnormal, misplaced, or non-functional cells [00:32:14–00:33:47].

##### Caspase cascade and apoptosis pathways [00:33:47–00:44:02]

The initial death signal can arise from different points, but apoptosis always involves a **cascade of reactions**. Each activated step activates the next, and the signal is amplified at each step [00:33:47].

The key proteins are **caspases**, which are **proteases**: proteins that cleave other specific proteins. Before activation, caspases exist as inactive **procaspases**. Cleavage activates them, and the active caspase then cleaves and activates the next caspase in the cascade [00:34:59–00:37:46].

The professor stressed that once the caspase cascade starts, it is difficult to stop because each active caspase amplifies the signal further. The final result is degradation of intracellular components: cytosolic proteins, nuclear components, and other essential parts of the cell [00:37:46].

###### Extrinsic pathway [00:37:46–00:41:53]

- Initiated by **external signals** detected by cell-surface receptors.
- These receptors are called **death receptors** because their activation signals the cell to die.
- When an extracellular signal binds a death receptor, the signal is transferred into the cell and activates the caspase cascade.

Cells can receive both death-inducing and **protective** signals. For example, neurons that reach the correct target may receive protective signals through membrane receptors; neurons that fail to arrive or arrive late and do not receive protection may undergo apoptosis. Thus, the outcome can depend on the balance between death and survival/protection signals [00:40:44].

###### Intrinsic pathway [00:41:53–00:44:02]

- Initiated by **internal signals**, often because the cell recognises damage that could be dangerous, e.g. loss of mitochondrial integrity.
- If the outer mitochondrial membrane is broken, contents of the intermembrane space are released into the cytosol.
- A key example is **cytochrome c**. Normally it functions in mitochondria for energy production, but if it escapes to the cytosol, it triggers apoptosis.
- Cytochrome c promotes aggregation of the protein **APAF1**, which leads to activation of the caspase cascade.
- It is called intrinsic because the signal originates inside the very same cell that will die.

##### Cell differentiation [00:44:03–00:46:35]

Not all cells in the body are equivalent. Multicellular organisms are organised so that different body parts have different functions, and this requires cells with different properties. The process by which a cell becomes specialised is called **differentiation** [00:45:17].

Even though all cells have the same genomic material, different cell types use different sets of genes. The set of genes being transcribed in each cell type, at each moment, is responsible for the cell’s function. The professor noted:

- Some cells are already committed to a differentiated fate after being born.
- Other cells change their gene expression and function only transiently.
- Approximately **200 different cell types** are present in the human body [00:46:35].

#### What the professor stressed

- Cell cycle regulation is **important** to ensure that new cells contain the appropriate genomic material; there are checkpoints before DNA synthesis, before chromosome segregation, and within M phase [00:02:51].
- During mitosis, proper **alignment at the metaphase plate** and separation of chromatids are key to understanding how genetic material is segregated equally [00:09:36].
- Homologous recombination in meiosis is **important for generating new combinations of genes**, but it does **not create new mutations** and occurs only between homologous chromosomes [00:22:46].
- Apoptosis is important because it allows cell death **without spilling contents** and therefore **without inflammation**; surrounding cells engulf the remains [00:29:48].
- The caspase cascade is an **amplifying**, step-by-step process that is **difficult to stop once started** [00:37:46].
- Cells can integrate **death and survival signals**; protection signals can prevent apoptosis, for example in developing neurons [00:40:44].

#### Course logistics mentioned

- The next session will cover the **last PDF/slides before the first partial exam**.
- The professor will explain the **contents and organisation of the first partial exam** in the next session [00:46:35–00:47:50].

#### Self-test

1. What are the main phases of the cell cycle, and what happens during interphase versus M phase?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Interphase is the growth and DNA replication phase, subdivided into G1, S, and G2. M phase is when duplicated DNA and cell components are segregated into two daughter cells. Interphase includes DNA synthesis in S phase; M phase includes chromosome segregation and cell division. [00:00:00–00:01:46]
</details>

2. Why are checkpoints necessary in the cell cycle?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Checkpoints ensure the cell cycle proceeds efficiently and accurately, so that new cells contain the appropriate genomic material. The professor mentioned checkpoints before DNA synthesis, before chromosome segregation in M phase, and within M phase to regulate the finishing of division. [00:02:51]
</details>

3. During metaphase of mitosis, how are chromosomes arranged, and why does this matter for anaphase?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Chromosomes align at the metaphase plate, with each chromatid oriented toward opposite poles. This high-order alignment is essential because in anaphase the centromere splits and each chromatid moves to an opposite pole, ensuring each daughter cell receives one copy of each chromosome. [00:08:47–00:10:37]
</details>

4. What is the main difference between metaphase of mitosis and metaphase I of meiosis?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
In mitosis, individual replicated chromosomes align at the metaphase plate. In metaphase I of meiosis, homologous chromosomes pair side by side as tetrads. Then, in anaphase I, whole homologous chromosomes separate, not sister chromatids. [00:20:15–00:21:25]
</details>

5. What is homologous recombination, and what does it achieve?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
It is the exchange of corresponding segments between homologous chromosomes during meiosis I. It generates new combinations of existing genes in the next generation. It does not create new gene forms or mutations, and it occurs only between homologous chromosomes and homologous regions. [00:22:46]
</details>

6. Compare necrosis and apoptosis in terms of cell contents and inflammation.  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Necrosis involves membrane rupture and spilling of intracellular contents into the surroundings, promoting inflammation. Apoptosis involves cell shrinkage, internal degradation of contents, and no spilling; surrounding cells can engulf the remains, so there is no inflammatory response. [00:27:08–00:29:48]
</details>

7. Why is the caspase cascade considered an amplifying process that is hard to stop?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Caspases are proteases that cleave and activate other caspases. Each active caspase can activate several downstream procaspases, so the signal is amplified step by step. Once the cascade starts, this positive amplification makes it difficult to stop. [00:34:59–00:37:46]
</details>

8. Distinguish the extrinsic and intrinsic pathways of apoptosis.  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The extrinsic pathway begins with external signals binding to death receptors on the cell surface, which then activate the intracellular caspase cascade. The intrinsic pathway begins with internal damage, such as mitochondrial outer membrane damage releasing cytochrome c into the cytosol; cytochrome c promotes APAF1 aggregation and activation of the caspase cascade. [00:37:46–00:44:02]
</details>

9. Why do some developing neurons die even though they have reached the correct target area?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Developing neurons can receive both death-inducing and protective signals. Cells that reach the target in time receive protection signals and survive. Cells that arrive late or do not receive the protective signal may receive only the death signal and undergo apoptosis. It is a balance between induction and protection. [00:40:44]
</details>

10. How can the same genome give rise to approximately 200 different cell types?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
All cells have the same genomic material, but different cell types use different sets of genes. The set of genes being transcribed in a given cell type at a given moment determines its function. This process of becoming specialised is called differentiation. [00:45:17–00:46:35]
</details>

### 09. BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251103 — Mon 03 Nov 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/12_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251103-20251103_171343-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/12_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251103-20251103_171343-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/12_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251103-20251103_171343-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary
This session finished Unit 4 with histology and organography. It covered how cells organise into tissues, the role of the extracellular matrix and cell junctions, the four basic tissue types (epithelial, connective, muscle, nervous), and how tissues combine into organs. The professor then explained the histology techniques used to visualise tissues (biopsy, fixation, staining with haematoxylin and eosin) and introduced Task 1, which asks students to study the tissue organisation of one selected organ.

- A **tissue** is a community of similar cells working together; cells are held together by direct cell-to-cell junctions or by attachment to the **extracellular matrix**.
- There are **four basic tissue types**: epithelial, connective, muscle and nervous.
- Epithelial tissue forms barriers and is classified by **cell shape** (squamous/cuboidal/columnar) and **number of layers** (simple/stratified/pseudostratified), and can form exocrine/endocrine glands.
- Connective tissue is defined by **few cells and abundant extracellular matrix**, which is why bone, cartilage and even blood belong to this group.
- Organs are structures made of **two or more tissue types**; routine histology uses fixation and staining, typically **haematoxylin and eosin (H&E)**.

#### Key concepts
- **Tissue** – a community of similar cells found together in one part of the body, functioning together.
- **Extracellular matrix (ECM)** – network of proteins and other components secreted by cells that occupies the space between cells; abundance and composition are tissue-specific.
- **Anchoring junctions** – cell-to-environment junctions linked to the intracellular cytoskeleton, especially intermediate filaments; include desmosomes (cell-to-cell) and adhesions to the ECM.
- **Occluding junctions** – seal gaps between adjacent cells to form a barrier; example: tight junctions in some epithelia.
- **Channel-forming junctions** – create pores/channels connecting the cytoplasm of adjacent cells, allowing passage of ions and small molecules; example: gap junctions in cardiac muscle.
- **Signal-transmitting junctions** – specialised contacts allowing chemical communication, e.g. synapses between neurons.
- **Epithelial tissue** – continuous layer(s) of cells attached side-by-side, creating a barrier between two compartments.
- **Connective tissue** – tissue with a low density of cells and abundant ECM; functions include protection, connecting organs, energy storage and support.
- **Muscle tissue** – tissue of specialised contractile cells that generate movement and heat.
- **Nervous tissue** – tissue made of neurons and neuroglia that detects changes, integrates information and induces responses.
- **Basal lamina** – the specialised basal ECM found at the basal side of an epithelium.
- **Simple vs stratified epithelium** – one layer of cells vs more than one layer; pseudostratified has one layer with nuclei at different levels.
- **Exocrine gland** – secretory structure that releases products to an external surface/lumen.
- **Endocrine gland** – secretory cells of epithelial origin that lose contact with the surface and secrete hormones into the body interior.
- **Merocrine/apocrine/holocrine secretion** – secretion by exocytosis; secretion by loss of part of the cell; secretion by destruction of the whole cell.
- **Organ** – structure composed of two or more tissue types with a specific function, recognisable by shape and location.
- **Epithelial membrane** – an epithelium plus an underlying connective tissue working together.
- **H&E stain** – haematoxylin and eosin staining used to observe tissue/cell organisation under the microscope.

#### Detailed notes

##### Scope of the session and exam context
This is the last presentation with content evaluated in the first partial exam. The first partial exam covers all Unit 4 presentations: cell biology and basic material from the chemical level to the cellular level, plus today’s histology content. The exam will take place the following Monday, 10 November 2025, synchronously. [00:00:00]

##### From cells to tissues: extracellular matrix and cell adhesion
A tissue is a community of similar cells found together in one part of the body. Because different cells function together, they need to communicate and interact with each other and with the surrounding components. [00:01:19]

A fundamental part of this interaction is how cells are maintained together. Around cells there is a network of proteins and other components secreted by the cells in the area; this material occupies the space between cells and is called the **extracellular matrix (ECM)**. The abundance and composition of the ECM varies and is specific for each tissue type. [00:02:00]

For a multicellular organism, cells must become attached and stabilise in position. Two main attachment modes exist:

1. **Direct cell-to-cell attachment** via cell junctions.
2. **Indirect attachment**: each cell attaches to the ECM rather than directly to the next cell.

Most cells in the body do not move freely; exceptions include some immune/phagocytic cells, which move more freely. [00:03:19]

##### Functional types of cell junctions
Cell junctions are grouped by function: [00:05:23]

1. **Anchoring junctions**
   - Connect cells to their environment, either ECM or another cell.
   - They are linked to cytoskeletal filaments, especially **intermediate filaments**, which have a structural role.
   - Membrane proteins link the extracellular environment to the intracellular cytoskeleton.
   - Examples:
     - **Desmosomes** and **adherens junctions**: cell-to-cell anchoring.
     - Other anchoring junctions bind cells to the ECM (the professor showed an example of ECM attachment).

2. **Occluding junctions**
   - Seal the gaps between adjacent cells.
   - This tight binding prevents movement of substances from one side to the other, creating a barrier.
   - Example: **tight junctions**, found in some epithelia. [00:07:07]

3. **Channel-forming junctions**
   - Create a porous structure/channel bridging the cytoplasm of two adjacent cells.
   - Allow the exchange of ions and small molecules between cells.
   - Example: **gap junctions**, also present in cardiac muscle. [00:08:27]

4. **Signal-transmitting junctions**
   - Specialised cell contacts for transmitting signals.
   - Example: **chemical synapses**, where two neurons establish contact and the attachment allows chemical communication. [00:09:30]

##### The four basic tissue types
The body contains four basic tissue types. Two have highly specialised cells (**muscle** and **nervous**), while the other two are defined more by how cells are organised (**epithelial** and **connective**). [00:10:36]

1. **Epithelial tissue**
   - Formed by cells aggregated side-by-side into a surface/barrier.
   - Separates two compartments: one side contacts one part of the body, the other side contacts another part.
   - Found on the external skin surface, in hollow organs such as stomach/intestine, and in vessels. [00:11:37]
   - Function: regulates interaction/exchange of components between two spaces, e.g. external environment and interstitial fluid.

2. **Connective tissue**
   - Usually creates spaces between other tissues; found between tissues.
   - Functions: protection, keeping organs together, energy storage, and other roles.
   - Defined by **few cells and abundant ECM** between them, opposite to epithelial tissue. [00:12:55]

3. **Muscle tissue**
   - Composed of specialised cells able to contract, becoming shorter and thicker.
   - Contraction generates body movement, and because contraction requires energy, some energy is released as heat. [00:14:10]

4. **Nervous tissue**
   - Composed of **neurons** and **neuroglia (glia)**.
   - Detects changes in the body, integrates information, and induces responses, e.g. contacting muscles to contract. [00:15:12]

##### Epithelial tissue in detail
Epithelial cells are disposed in a continuous layer, but their morphology can vary: [00:16:24]

- **Squamous**: flat cells.
- **Cuboidal**: cube-shaped cells.
- **Columnar**: cylindrical cells.

Adjacent epithelial cells attach by cell-to-cell junctions, which may include anchoring junctions, occluding junctions, and channel-forming junctions. Cells also attach to the ECM on the basal side. The epithelium has little ECM between its cells. [00:17:39]

The basal ECM forms the **basal lamina**, always found near the basal part of the epithelium. For example, in the intestine the apical side faces the lumen, while the basal side contacts the rest of the body. [00:18:46]

Epithelial cells are **polarised**: the apical surface and basal surface are very different. [00:19:54]

Epithelia are also classified by the number of layers:

- **Simple epithelium**: one layer of cells.
- **Stratified epithelium**: more than one layer.
- **Pseudostratified epithelium**: one layer of cells, but not all cells are aligned at the same position, so it looks stratified.

Combining shape and layering gives names such as **simple columnar epithelium** or **stratified squamous epithelium**. [00:21:00]

###### Functions of epithelia
1. **Protection / lining**: e.g. skin as a protective barrier with the external environment.
2. **Secretion**: specialised **glandular epithelium**.

Glandular epithelium can organise in different ways: [00:22:02]

- **Single secretory cells scattered within the epithelium**, e.g. **goblet cells** (caliciform cells) in the intestine, each secreting content into the intestinal lumen.
- **Secretory cells grouped into a specialised part of the epithelium**, forming a **gland**.

Exocrine glands arise as invaginations of an epithelium with secretory cells at the base/bottom, releasing secretions to an external surface or lumen (e.g. skin surface or intestinal lumen). [00:23:16]

During embryogenesis, some epithelial invaginations separate from the surface epithelium. These cells then secrete into the body interior rather than to the exterior, becoming **endocrine glands**. Most endocrine glands secrete **hormones**, which have important communication functions. [00:24:17]

###### Modes of exocrine secretion
Exocrine glands are classified by how secretions are produced: [00:25:15]

1. **Merocrine secretion**
   - Products are made in vesicles, vesicles fuse with the membrane, and contents are released by exocytosis.
   - Example: **sweat glands**.

2. **Apocrine secretion**
   - The cell loses part of itself as the secretion.
   - The cell grows and a portion separates; the cell remains alive.
   - Example: **mammary glands** producing milk.

3. **Holocrine secretion**
   - The whole cell is used as the secretion; the cell dies and all its components become the secretion.
   - Example: **sebaceous glands**, which produce the oily secretion on the skin surface.

Exocrine glands can also be classified by structure, e.g. tubular, branched/ramified tubule, coiled tubule, etc. [00:26:13]

##### Connective tissue in detail
Connective tissue consists of a large amount of ECM with relatively few cells dispersed in it. The composition of the ECM determines the properties of each connective tissue. [00:28:18]

Types of connective tissue:

- **Embryonic connective tissue**: present only during development.
- **Proper connective tissue**: found between many organs; can be subdivided into **loose** and **dense** connective tissue. [00:29:20]
- **Supportive connective tissue**: provides structural support to the body.
  - **Cartilage**
  - **Bone**: cells in bone are dispersed, and the ECM contains minerals, but the overall cell/ECM organisation is still typical of connective tissue. [00:30:21]
- **Blood**: a liquid connective tissue.
  - Has the same defining pattern: few cells and abundant ECM.
  - But in blood the ECM is liquid, and cells move freely within it. [00:31:23]

##### Muscle tissue in detail
Muscle cells are contractile. The contractile units are based on proteins **myosin and actin** (discussed in later sessions). Muscle functions include movement of the body, movement of contents within hollow organs, and heat generation. [00:31:40]

Three types of muscle: [00:32:24]

1. **Skeletal muscle**
   - Cells are large and have **more than one nucleus**, because each mature cell forms by fusion of several precursor cells (a syncytium).
   - Requires nerve activation to contract.
   - Under voluntary control.

2. **Cardiac muscle**
   - Cells are **mononucleated** and **branched/ramified** rather than cylindrical.
   - Cells attach to each other through specialised junction structures (e.g. intercalated discs/gap junctions seen later).
   - Contraction is involuntary; we cannot control heart rhythm.

3. **Smooth muscle**
   - Contractile cells found in **hollow organs** and **around vessels**.
   - Controls movement of internal fluids/substances through hollow organs and vessels.
   - Involuntary.

##### Nervous tissue in detail
Nervous tissue contains two broad cell types: neurons and neuroglia. [00:34:44]

###### Neurons
- Highly differentiated cells with three main parts:
  - **Soma** (cell body)
  - **Dendritic arbor** (dendrites)
  - **Axonal compartment** (axon)
- Neurons are **excitable**: they generate electrical currents in the cell membrane, producing **action potentials** that propagate through the cell.
- Very diverse in size and shape; can be distinguished by dendritic branching pattern, etc.
- Neurons **do not divide by mitosis** (according to the professor). [00:34:50]

###### Neuroglia
- As abundant as neurons.
- Can divide throughout life, unlike neurons.
- Non-excitable cells.
- Smaller than neurons.
- Six types, distributed between the central and peripheral nervous systems:
  1. **Astrocytes**
  2. **Oligodendrocytes**
  3. **Microglia**
  4. **Ependymal cells**
  5. **Schwann cells**
  6. **Satellite cells**

Some are in the central nervous system, others in the peripheral nervous system. [00:35:51]

##### Organs and histology
Different tissues can work together. An **epithelial membrane** always consists of an epithelial tissue attached to an underlying connective tissue working together. Because epithelial cells are tightly attached to each other, the epithelium itself cannot receive blood vessels; nutrients arrive from the surrounding connective tissue. [00:36:52]

An **organ** is a structure of the body composed of **two or more types of tissue** with a very specific function. Most organs are recognised by their shape and location. [00:38:24]

To analyse the inner structure of an organ:

1. Obtain a piece of tissue, e.g. by **biopsy** (liver, muscle, etc.).
2. **Fixate** it to prevent destruction and maintain proteins in place.
3. **Stain** with specific dyes to visualise structures such as the nucleus, membrane, or interstitial components.

A common method is **haematoxylin and eosin (H&E)** staining, which allows differentiation of cells in almost any tissue. Thin stained slices are observed under the microscope. Examples visible in preparations: artery, nerve, vein, muscle, and epithelium with subjacent connective tissue. [00:40:36]

##### Task 1 and exam logistics
The professor introduced Task 1 (Unit 4 – Cellular Biology). [00:41:45]

- **Step 1**: Select an organ from a proposed list. Organ choices are limited per student, so not everyone can choose the same organ.
- **Step 2**: Use the template document, study how the selected organ is organised into tissues, and observe examples of staining for that organ.
- Useful resources:
  - Course textbook general information.
  - University virtual microscope websites.
  - **University of Vigo Atlas of Plant and Animal Histology**, in Spanish and English, with information on organ tissue organisation, cell organisation, and cell types.
- Expected working time: about **two hours**.
- Deliverable: completed template submitted in PDF via the **Task 1** activity in the Graded Activities section.
- Deadline: **14 November 2025** (two full weeks after the session).
- The first partial exam is on **Monday, 10 November 2025**, synchronous; students must be present in the session to participate. [00:45:13]

#### What the professor stressed
- **Today’s histology content is the last material for the first partial exam**, which covers the entire Unit 4, from chemical level to cellular level plus histology. [00:00:00]
- The **four basic tissue types** are a central framework: epithelial, connective, muscle and nervous; remember which have specialised cells and which are defined by organisation. [00:11:37]
- For connective tissue, the defining feature is **few cells + abundant ECM**, which explains why bone, cartilage and blood are connective tissues. [00:30:21]
- Epithelial classification by **cell shape and number of layers**, and the concept of **epithelial polarity / basal lamina**, were emphasised. [00:19:54]
- The three modes of exocrine secretion (**merocrine, apocrine, holocrine**) were presented with concrete examples; these are exam-relevant. [00:26:13]
- The exam is **synchronous**; you must be present on 10 November. [00:46:50]

#### Course logistics mentioned
- **First partial exam**: Monday, 10 November 2025, synchronous; covers all Unit 4 presentations, including today’s histology content.
- **Task 1 (Unit 4 – Cellular Biology)**:
  - Step 1: select an organ in Campus Virtual; selections are limited and cannot be modified.
  - Step 2: complete the template document, using textbooks and virtual microscope atlases (e.g. University of Vigo Atlas).
  - Submit in PDF through the **Task 1** assignment.
  - Deadline: **Friday, 14 November 2025, 11:59 PM**.
  - Expected workload: about 2 hours.

#### Self-test
1. Define a tissue. How does the extracellular matrix contribute to tissue organisation?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
A tissue is a community of similar cells found together in one part of the body, functioning together. The ECM is the network of proteins and other secreted components between cells; its composition is tissue-specific and cells attach to each other directly or to the ECM, which stabilises their positions. [00:01:19]
</details>

2. Compare epithelial and connective tissue in terms of cell density and ECM.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Epithelial tissue has many cells closely attached side-by-side, with very little ECM between them. Connective tissue has relatively few cells dispersed in abundant ECM. [00:12:55]
</details>

3. Match each junction to its function: anchoring, occluding, channel-forming, signal-transmitting.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Anchoring junctions attach cells to the ECM/other cells and link to the cytoskeleton (desmosomes, adherens junctions). Occluding junctions seal gaps between cells to form a barrier (tight junctions). Channel-forming junctions create channels between cytoplasms for ions/small molecules (gap junctions). Signal-transmitting junctions allow chemical communication (chemical synapses). [00:05:23]
</details>

4. Why do epithelial cells have polarity, and what is the basal lamina?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Epithelial cells form a barrier, so their apical surface faces one compartment (e.g. external environment or lumen) and the basal surface faces the body; these two surfaces are very different, making the cell polarised. The basal lamina is the specialised extracellular matrix found at the basal side of the epithelium. [00:18:46]
</details>

5. Classify the following epithelia: one flat layer; many flattened layers; one columnar layer with nuclei at different heights.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Simple squamous; stratified squamous; pseudostratified columnar. [00:21:00]
</details>

6. Explain the difference between exocrine and endocrine glands.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Exocrine glands release their secretion to an external surface or a lumen (e.g. skin surface, intestinal lumen), usually via an invagination of the epithelium. Endocrine glands separate from the surface during development and secrete into the body interior; most secrete hormones. [00:23:16]
</details>

7. Give one example for each exocrine secretion mode: merocrine, apocrine, holocrine.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Merocrine: sweat glands (exocytosis of vesicles). Apocrine: mammary glands (part of the cell is released). Holocrine: sebaceous glands (whole cell dies and becomes the secretion). [00:26:13]
</details>

8. Why are bone and blood classified as connective tissues?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Because they share the defining connective tissue organisation: few cells dispersed in abundant extracellular matrix. In bone the ECM is mineralised; in blood the ECM is liquid and cells move freely. [00:30:21]
</details>

9. Compare skeletal, cardiac and smooth muscle on: nuclei, voluntary control, and location/function.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Skeletal: multinucleated (syncytium), voluntary, requires nerve activation, attached to skeleton/movement. Cardiac: mononucleated, branched, involuntary, heart; cells attached by specialised junctions. Smooth: found in hollow organs and around vessels, involuntary, controls movement of contents/fluids. [00:32:24]
</details>

10. What are the main differences between neurons and neuroglia?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Neurons are excitable, highly differentiated, have soma/dendrites/axon, generate action potentials, and do not divide by mitosis. Neuroglia are non-excitable, smaller, as abundant as neurons, can divide throughout life, and include astrocytes, oligodendrocytes, microglia, ependymal cells, Schwann cells and satellite cells. [00:34:44]
</details>

11. Define an organ. Why must an epithelium be associated with connective tissue?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
An organ is a body structure composed of two or more tissue types with a specific function. Epithelium is too tightly packed to receive blood vessels, so nutrients arrive from the underlying connective tissue; the combination is called an epithelial membrane. [00:37:57]
</details>

12. What are the standard steps to prepare a tissue sample for microscopic observation, and what does H&E stand for?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Obtain a piece of tissue (biopsy), fixate it to preserve structure, stain with dyes, cut thin slices, and observe under the microscope. H&E is haematoxylin and eosin, a common stain that differentiates cells in almost every tissue. [00:39:50]
</details>

#### Related course materials
- [U4-4-Histology and Organography](materials/14_T4-4-HISTOLOGY.pdf)
- [Task 1-Template document](materials/15_TASK%201.docx)

### 10. BE-Molecular Biology- Session 6 — Thu 13 Nov 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/13_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%206-20251113_140221-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/13_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%206-20251113_140221-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/13_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%206-20251113_140221-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary
This session moved from the NCBI Gene queries done previously to the overall architecture of the human genome and then into hands-on use of the UCSC Genome Browser. The professor reviewed the nuclear genome’s size and composition, the difference between all genes and protein-coding genes, and why the non-coding 99% of the genome is not “junk” but largely regulatory. She covered chromosome structure, euchromatin/heterochromatin, CpG islands, non-coding RNAs, tandem repeats, segmental duplications, retrotransposons, and pseudogenes. The second half was a guided UCSC exercise on chromosome 7: displaying genes, pseudogenes, repeats, sequence conservation across vertebrates, and common SNPs.

- Only about 1.1% of the nuclear genome codes for protein; most of the rest is involved in gene regulation, chromosome architecture, and epigenetic inheritance.
- The genome is packed with repetitive DNA: micro/mini/macrosatellites, segmental duplications, and LINE/SINE retrotransposons.
- Pseudogenes are non-functional copies or remnants of genes and can act as a stored “library” of old genes.
- Exons are highly conserved across vertebrates because changes in coding sequence are more often harmful; introns mutate much more freely.
- UCSC Genome Browser integrates more than 100 annotation databases and is the main tool used to explore genome elements visually.

#### Key concepts
- **Nuclear genome**: 23 pairs of chromosomes in the nucleus; 3.1 gigabases (Gb) on the main strand, 6.2 Gb if both strands are counted [00:04:15].
- **Mitochondrial genome**: small, circular, bacterial-like genome; only 13 genes, no introns, little intergenic DNA, no histone packing; mainly metabolism/energy genes [00:09:52].
- **Gene**: a DNA sequence that is transcribed into RNA; not every gene is translated into protein. About 40,000 human genes exist, of which about 20,000 code for protein [00:05:18].
- **Euchromatin / heterochromatin**: white, less-packed chromosome regions with high gene density vs black, highly packed regions with low gene density [00:10:54].
- **Non-coding DNA**: about 99% of the genome; includes intergenic regions, introns, 5′/3′ UTRs, promoters, and non-coding RNA genes [00:08:37].
- **CpG islands**: regions where CpG cytosines are not methylated; often found upstream of genes and can mark promoter regions because methylation would block transcription-factor binding [00:19:33].
- **Non-coding RNAs**: RNA genes not translated into protein; function through their three-dimensional structure; divided into short (<200 nt) and long (>200 nt) classes [00:14:11][00:21:49].
- **Tandem repeats**: adjacent repeated sequences; microsatellites 1–9 nt, minisatellites 10–100 nt, macrosatellites >100 nt; used in parentage testing and linked to disease/phenotypic traits [00:24:59].
- **Segmental duplications**: large duplicated blocks of 10–300 kb with ~95% identity; recent evolutionary events; contribute to copy-number variation and chromosomal rearrangements [00:26:00].
- **Retrotransposons / jumping genes**: mobile DNA elements that move via an RNA intermediate and reverse transcription; LINEs ~7,000 nt and SINEs 100–700 nt; make up roughly 20% + 13% of the genome [00:29:36][00:30:44].
- **Pseudogenes**: duplicated or retrotransposed gene copies that have lost function; processed pseudogenes arise from retrotranscribed mRNA, non-processed pseudogenes arise from gene duplication [00:31:57][00:34:17].
- **Sequence conservation**: degree to which a genomic position/region is unchanged across species; high in coding exons because deleterious mutations are removed by selection [01:10:44].
- **Common SNP**: a single-nucleotide variant with appreciable frequency in the population; not directly disease-causing by itself, but combinations plus environment can predispose to complex disease [01:20:42].

#### Detailed notes
##### Recap: NCBI Gene work before this session
[00:00:00] The previous session used the NCBI Gene database. With only an organism and a gene name, students located gene information, sequence, chromosome position, transcript and protein sequences, and extracted exons, coding regions, 5′ UTRs, and 3′ UTRs.

##### The post-sequencing question: what does the sequence mean?
[00:02:13] Sequencing technology has improved dramatically; a genome is now cheap and fast to sequence. Sequence is always written 5′→3′ on the main strand; the complementary strand runs 3′→5′ and can be computed, so databases store only one strand [00:03:13]. The hard problem after the Human Genome Project was not obtaining sequence, but understanding what those millions of nucleotides mean [00:03:13].

##### Nuclear and mitochondrial genomes
[00:04:15] The human genome has two parts:
- **Nuclear genome**: 23 chromosome pairs, 3.1 Gb on the main strand.
- **Mitochondrial genome**: small circular genome in mitochondria.

The mitochondrial genome is bacterial-like: genes are packed one after another, there are no introns and little intergenic DNA, only about 13 genes involved in metabolism/energy, and no histone packaging because it does not need to fit into a nucleus [00:09:52].

##### Human genes: number, coding fraction, and independent transcription
[00:05:18] There are about 40,000 human genes, but only about 20,000 are translated into protein. The other 20,000 are transcribed into non-coding RNAs with three-dimensional structures that perform regulatory and other functions [00:14:11].

Human genes are usually transcribed independently, unlike bacteria, where several genes can be carried on one RNA molecule [00:06:22].

##### Why the 99% non-coding genome is not “junk”
[00:06:22] Protein-coding sequence is only about 1.1% of the genome. The other ~99% was initially called “junk DNA,” but in the last ~15 years it has become clear that it has essential functions [00:07:36]:
- regulating gene expression;
- chromosome architecture;
- control of epigenetic inheritance.

This non-coding fraction includes intergenic regions, non-coding genes, introns, 5′/3′ UTRs, and promoter regions [00:08:37].

##### Chromosome organization and gene density
[00:10:54] Chromosomes vary greatly in size, from about 1 Mb to around 50 Mb. In chromosome banding:
- **black bands = heterochromatin**: tightly packed, genes not accessible, low gene density;
- **white bands = euchromatin**: less packed, high gene density, accessible to transcription machinery.

Different chromosomes have very different proportions of euchromatin and heterochromatin; there is no single fixed percentage [00:10:54].

About 9% of human genes overlap, often with one gene on the main strand and another on the complementary strand; many are small RNA genes [00:14:11].

##### Gene architecture, exon/intron diversity, and CpG islands
[00:11:56] Human genes vary enormously:
- mean length roughly 26,000 bases;
- range from about 1,000 bases to about 1 million bases;
- from 1 exon to more than 100 exons.

Genes that must be expressed very frequently often lack introns, to reduce the cost of transcribing extra sequence [00:18:30].

**CpG islands** [00:19:33]:
- Cytosines in CpG dinucleotides are often methylated; this is an epigenetic mark.
- Regions where CpGs are not methylated are called CpG islands.
- CpG islands are usually found upstream of genes and can help identify promoter regions.
- Methylation upstream of a gene would block proteins needed for transcription, so promoter regions tend to remain unmethylated.

##### How protein-coding genes were identified
[00:15:14] Protein-coding genes are relatively easy to find because a coding region starts with methionine and continues as a long open reading frame without stop codons. Since genes can be on either strand, there are six reading frames for any genomic sequence: three from the main strand and three from the complementary strand [00:16:16]. Regions that are long, start with methionine, and lack premature stops are candidate coding genes. This approach misses non-coding RNA genes, which are much harder to identify.

A student asked whether every long ORF is a functional protein. The answer: most are, but some long ORFs belong to pseudogenes that no longer produce functional protein [00:17:25].

##### Non-coding RNA genes
[00:21:49] The ~20,000 non-coding RNA genes are classified by size:
- **Short non-coding RNAs (<200 nt)**: include RNAs involved in splicing and RNA maturation, small Cajal body RNAs, transfer RNAs, microRNAs, piwi-interacting RNAs, and short interfering RNAs.
- **Long non-coding RNAs (>200 nt)**: regulate target genes and protein translation.

They work mainly as regulators of transcription and translation through their folded 3D structure.

##### Tandem repeats
[00:23:54] Tandem repeats are clusters of repeat sequence adjacent to one another. They can occur inside coding regions, but are abundant outside coding regions. Repeats may be identical or similar; similar repeats arose because mutations accumulated after duplication [00:24:59].

Classification [00:24:59]:
- **Microsatellites**: 1–9 nucleotides.
- **Minisatellites**: 10–100 nucleotides.
- **Macrosatellites**: >100 nucleotides.

They are involved in gene expression, gene silencing, alternative splicing, mRNA transport, chromatin organization, and cell-cycle regulation. Changes in microsatellite length are associated with diseases and phenotypic traits. Because repeats are inherited, they are used to establish parentage [00:26:00].

##### Segmental duplications and gene duplication
[00:26:00] More than 5% of euchromatin is made of segmental duplications: blocks of 10–300 kb with about 95% sequence identity. High identity means the duplications occurred recently; otherwise they would have accumulated mutations [00:27:02]. They contribute to copy-number variation and chromosomal rearrangements, because duplicated blocks can cause misalignment during chromosome pairing.

Gene duplication follow-up [00:28:00]: an ancestral gene duplicates, the two copies accumulate different mutations, and over time they can become two similar but distinct genes with somewhat different functions. The more mutations, the more functions diverge.

##### Retrotransposons: LINEs and SINEs
[00:29:36] Retrotransposons are mobile DNA elements (“jumping genes”) that can move within and between chromosomes. Mechanism:
1. The retrotransposon is transcribed into RNA.
2. Reverse transcriptase converts the RNA into DNA.
3. The DNA inserts into another genomic region.

They are classified as:
- **LINEs**: long interspersed elements, about 7,000 nt.
- **SINEs**: short interspersed elements, about 100–700 nt.

About 20% of the human genome is LINEs and another 13% is SINEs [00:30:44].

##### Pseudogenes
[00:31:57] Pseudogenes are gene-like sequences that have lost the ability to be transcribed/translated into a functional product. They may be missing a promoter, start codon, or introns, or contain a premature stop codon. About 16,000 pseudogenes are estimated. They can be viewed as a “library” of old genes; under mutation and evolutionary pressure, a pseudogene can occasionally regain function.

Two origins [00:34:17]:
- **Processed pseudogenes**: messenger RNA is retrotranscribed into DNA and inserted into the genome; the copy lacks introns and regulatory elements.
- **Non-processed pseudogenes**: arise by gene duplication, then one copy loses function. Some are transcribed into RNA but not translated.

##### Gene resources and genome browsers
[00:34:17] Key resources:
- **HGNC (HUGO Gene Nomenclature Committee)**: assigns unique gene symbols.
- **RefSeq**: curated database of genome, transcript, and protein sequences.
- **Genome browsers**: UCSC, Ensembl (EBI), and NCBI.

UCSC, from UC Santa Cruz, is the most powerful browser and integrates over 100 annotation databases. It has a genome browser for visualization and a table browser for downloading annotations [00:35:19][00:37:22].

##### UCSC Genome Browser practical: exploring chromosome 7
[00:36:21] The exercises are “UCSC Genome Browser Exercises” on the course page. Steps in the guided demo:

###### Browser setup
[00:37:22] Open UCSC Genome Browser. Select the human genome; the professor used the 2013 human assembly because newer assemblies still lack many annotations [00:38:44]. Type `chr7` in the position search to display chromosome 7 [00:39:48].

###### Chromosome 7 bands
[00:42:25] At full view, the top shows black and white chromosome bands: black = heterochromatin, white = euchromatin. The red window indicates the whole chromosome. Use **hide all** to clear default tracks [00:43:54].

###### Available track categories
[00:44:00] UCSC displays many categories:
- **Sequencing**
- **Genes and gene predictions**: NCBI RefSeq and GENCODE
- **Disease**: OMIM maps genes involved in Mendelian diseases
- **Variation**: pathogenic/disease-associated variants vs common population variation
- **RNA/transcriptome**: transcripts, tissue expression, single-cell RNA-seq
- **Regulation**: enhancers, promoters
- **Comparative genomics**: multi-species alignments
- **Repeats**

###### Displaying genes
[00:48:13] Under “Genes and gene predictions,” select **NCBI RefSeq**, choose a display mode such as **pack**, and click **refresh**. Zoom in to see individual genes:
- **Arrows pointing left** = genes on the complementary strand (reverse).
- **Arrows pointing right** = genes on the main strand (forward).
- **Vertical lines inside a gene** = exons.
- Overlapping genes are visible as well [00:51:28].

###### Displaying pseudogenes
[00:53:16] Use the **GENCODE** track, filter to show pseudogenes only, set to **pack**, and submit. Pseudogenes appear in pink; normal genes are blue. The genome contains many pseudogenes [00:54:19]. The dedicated pseudogene track gives essentially the same information.

###### Displaying tandem repeats
[00:56:30] Under **Repeats**, select **Simple Repeats**, choose **pack**, refresh. Chromosome 7 is full of small tandem repeats such as `TTTA` or `AAA` [00:58:37]. These are the micro/mini/macrosatellites.

###### Displaying segmental duplications
[00:59:43] Under **Repeats**, select **Segmental Duplications**, choose **pack**, refresh. This shows large duplicated regions of 10–300 kb. Clicking a position shows other genomic regions with the same sequence. Difference from tandem repeats: segmental duplications are much larger and can contain several genes; tandem repeats are short adjacent repeats [01:02:37].

###### Displaying retrotransposons
[01:03:00] Use the **RepeatMasker** track under repeats, choose **dense** or **full**, refresh. This shows the jumping genes: short and long interspersed repeat elements.

###### Sequence conservation across vertebrates
[01:06:02] Hide everything, display RefSeq genes in pack, then go to **Comparative genomics** and select **UCSC 100 vertebrates**. After selecting all species and using **squish**, the browser produces a conservation plot [01:08:30]. Zoom to a gene:
- Conservation is very high in exons.
- Conservation is low in introns [01:10:44].

Reason: mutations in coding exons can damage protein structure/function, making individuals less likely to survive/reproduce, so those mutations are not retained. Mutations in introns are less harmful and accumulate [01:11:45]. High peaks mean near/at 100% sequence identity. In full mode, the actual amino-acid/nucleotide alignments are visible; exons show conserved amino acids, introns show many differences [01:14:10][01:16:11]. The genetic code is degenerate, so some nucleotide changes still encode the same amino acid and are tolerated; not every amino-acid change causes disease [01:18:24].

###### Common SNPs in the population
[01:20:42] Under **Variation**, select the SNP database track and display with **squish**, **pack**, or **full**. These are single-nucleotide changes common in the population, not directly disease-related. Example: at some position, half the population may have a G and half a C. Individually they are not disease-causing, but combinations of SNPs plus environmental factors can predispose to complex disease [01:22:30].

The professor recommended repeating the “Exploring the human genome,” “Sequence conservation,” and “Variation” exercises [01:24:58].

#### What the professor stressed
- The 99% non-coding DNA is not junk; it contains regulatory information that is at least as important as coding sequence [00:07:36].
- The genome is dominated by repeats; repeats are not only structural noise but also used for parentage, gene regulation, and disease association [00:24:59][00:58:37].
- The distinction between tandem repeats and segmental duplications came up in a student question: tandem repeats are short adjacent repeats; segmental duplications are huge blocks that can span multiple genes [01:02:37].
- Pseudogenes are non-functional but may become functional again under environmental/evolutionary pressure—a “library” of old genes [00:31:57].
- The strong conservation of exons across 100 vertebrate species is visible evidence of natural selection on coding sequence. She repeated this several times and tested understanding with questions [01:10:44][01:18:24].
- Common SNPs are not individually disease-causing; complex disease arises from combinations and environmental factors [01:20:42].

#### Course logistics mentioned
- Task 2 was opened during the session; deadline for submission is 26 November. The professor recommended starting it early because the partial exam is soon [01:24:58][01:26:24].
- A partial exam covering the concepts worked with all presentations is expected in about two weeks; the professor mentioned 27 November [01:24:58].
- Students can email the professor with questions about the exercises or the task [01:27:25].

#### Self-test
1. Why do genome databases store only one strand of sequence, and what convention is used?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Because the complementary strand can be computed by base-pairing rules. The stored strand is the main strand written 5′→3′; the complementary strand runs 3′→5′ and pairs through hydrogen bonds.
</details>

2. Give at least three functions of the non-coding 99% of the human genome.  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Regulation of gene expression, chromosome architecture, and control of epigenetic inheritance. Structurally it includes promoters, introns, UTRs, intergenic regions, and non-coding RNA genes.
</details>

3. In the UCSC browser, how can you tell a forward gene from a reverse gene when viewing RefSeq transcripts?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Forward genes lie on the main strand and their arrows point right; reverse genes lie on the complementary strand and their arrows point left. Vertical lines within the gene represent exons.
</details>

4. Why are CpG islands often useful for locating promoter regions?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Most CpGs in the genome are methylated, but promoter regions need transcription factors to bind, and methylation blocks that binding. Therefore, regions upstream of genes are often unmethylated CpG islands, making them useful markers for promoters.
</details>

5. Compare tandem repeats and segmental duplications in terms of size, arrangement, and biological relevance.  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Tandem repeats are short adjacent repeated units (microsatellites 1–9 nt, minisatellites 10–100 nt, macrosatellites >100 nt) and are used in parentage and related to gene regulation and disease. Segmental duplications are much larger blocks, 10–300 kb, about 95% identical, recent in evolution, and contribute to copy-number variation and chromosomal rearrangements.
</details>

6. Describe how a retrotransposon moves to a new genomic location, and distinguish LINEs from SINEs.  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The element is transcribed into RNA; reverse transcriptase converts the RNA into DNA; the DNA inserts elsewhere in the genome. LINEs are long (~7,000 nt) retrotransposons; SINEs are short (100–700 nt) retrotransposons.
</details>

7. What is the difference between a processed pseudogene and a non-processed pseudogene?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
A processed pseudogene arises when an mRNA is retrotranscribed into DNA and inserted into the genome, so it lacks introns and regulatory elements. A non-processed pseudogene arises from gene duplication where one copy loses function through mutations. Both are non-functional.
</details>

8. Why are exons more conserved than introns across vertebrate species? Does any change in an exon necessarily cause disease?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Exons code for protein, so mutations there are more likely to damage protein structure/function and reduce survival or reproduction; such mutations are not fixed. Intron mutations are usually neutral and accumulate. Not all exon changes cause disease: the genetic code is degenerate, so some nucleotide changes give the same amino acid, and not every amino-acid change compromises function.
</details>

9. How can sequence-conservation profiles help identify protein-coding genes?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Because coding exons are under strong purifying selection, they appear as high-conservation peaks in multi-species alignments, whereas intronic and intergenic regions usually show lower conservation.
</details>

10. What is a common SNP, and why is it not directly disease-causing?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
A common SNP is a single-nucleotide variant present at meaningful frequency in the population. Individually it is not directly associated with disease, but combinations of many such variants together with environmental factors can predispose to complex disease.
</details>

11. Why is the mitochondrial genome so compact compared to the nuclear genome?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
It has a bacterial-like organization: only about 13 genes, no introns, little intergenic DNA, and no histone packaging. Its genes are mainly related to metabolism and energy production.
</details>

12. What did the professor mean by calling pseudogenes a “library of old genes”?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Pseudogenes are non-functional copies or remnants of genes that may be stored in the genome. Under mutation and evolutionary pressure, some could regain function if the environment changes and they become useful again.
</details>
