### 11. BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251117 — Mon 17 Nov 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/14_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251117-20251117_170337-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/14_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251117-20251117_170337-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/14_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251117-20251117_170337-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary
This session opened Unit 5 with an overview of the human body systems, then covered the integumentary system and the locomotor system in detail. The professor explained how skin functions as a physical and chemical barrier, participates in thermoregulation and sensation, and how it is organised into epidermis, dermis, and the underlying hypodermis. The second half focused on the skeletal system: bone functions, bone classification, bone tissue structure, growth, and remodelling. It closed with the organisation of skeletal muscle from cells to whole muscles, including the sarcomere.

- The integumentary system is much more than skin: it is a barrier, a blood reservoir, a thermoregulatory surface, a vitamin D synthesis site, and a sensory organ.
- Skin proper consists of two layers: epidermis (avascular epithelial barrier) and dermis (vascular connective tissue); the hypodermis lies below and is mostly adipocytes.
- Bones are living, highly vascularised connective tissue with mineralised extracellular matrix, not inert structures.
- Bone is constantly remodelled by osteoblasts and osteoclasts, which also helps regulate blood calcium.
- The adult skeleton has 206 bones, with shape-based types (long, flat, short, irregular) and location/formation-based types (sesamoid, sutural).
- Skeletal muscle cells are multinucleated and striated, organised into fascicles; tendons are connective tissue extensions that attach muscle to bone.

#### Key concepts

- **Integumentary system** – the body system formed mainly by skin, plus associated glands, hair, and nails [00:01:01].
- **Locomotor system** – the combination of the skeletal system (bones, cartilage, articulations) and the muscular system (skeletal muscles, tendons) [00:01:01].
- **Epidermis** – the most superficial skin layer; a stratified epithelium with no blood vessels [00:14:55].
- **Dermis** – the connective tissue layer below the epidermis, containing blood vessels, collagen, and glands [00:16:52].
- **Hypodermis** – the layer below the skin proper, formed mainly by adipocytes; contains hair bulbs and sweat glands; not considered part of the skin [00:20:59].
- **Osteon** – the basic structural and functional unit of compact bone, organised as concentric rings around a central blood-vessel channel [00:43:47].
- **Osteoblast / osteocyte / osteoclast** – cells that respectively form bone matrix, maintain bone while trapped in it, and resorb/degrade bone [00:49:23].
- **Sarcomere** – the functional contractile unit of a muscle cell, made of highly organised thick and thin filaments [00:54:57].

#### Detailed notes

##### Unit 5 overview: the body systems
The unit covers the systems that coordinate or sustain the body [00:00:00].

- **Integumentary system**: skin, associated glands, hair, nails [00:01:01].
- **Locomotor system**: skeleton (bones, cartilage, articulations) plus skeletal muscles and tendons [00:01:01].
- **Cardiovascular system**: heart, arteries, veins; distributes blood and components throughout the body [00:02:20].
- **Lymphatic and immune system**: lymph drainage from tissues back to blood, immune-cell formation in bone marrow, and body protection [00:02:20].
- **Respiratory system**: conducts air to the lungs; eliminates carbon dioxide [00:03:41].
- **Digestive system**: obtains most nutrients for cells [00:03:41].
- **Urinary system**: eliminates waste; main role of the kidney [00:04:48].
- **Reproductive system**: male and female differences; hormonal regulation of gamete production (oocytes, spermatozoids) [00:04:48].
- **Endocrine system**: glands producing hormones (thyroid, hypophysis); coordinates body systems via hormones [00:05:57].
- **Nervous system**: brain and central nervous system, plus peripheral nervous system; coordinates body function [00:07:06].

##### Integumentary system: functions
The skin is about more than covering the body [00:08:08].

- **Physical protection**: a physical barrier separating the inside of the body from the environment [00:08:08].
- **Chemical/immune protection**: skin surface secretions help block pathogen entry; the barrier is also chemical, not only physical [00:09:10].
- **Prevents dehydration**: reduces strong evaporation of internal liquids to the environment [00:09:10].
- **UV protection**: reduces strong ultraviolet irradiation reaching internal structures [00:09:40].
- **Secretion**: sweat from sudoriferous glands and secretions from sebaceous glands are produced and released to the surface [00:10:23].
- **Absorption**: some substances contacting skin can enter the body depending on chemical composition. Lipid-soluble substances are more permeable, gases can cross, and some drugs or vitamins can enter through the skin depending on their composition [00:10:23].
- **Blood reservoir**: about 10% of total blood flow goes through the skin; this blood can be mobilised when needed [00:11:55].
- **Thermoregulation**: regulating blood flow through skin changes heat exchange with the environment. Sweating also cools the body because evaporation from the skin surface removes energy as heat [00:11:55].
- **Vitamin D synthesis**: a vitamin D precursor requires ultraviolet irradiation to become active; this transformation happens in the skin [00:13:04].
- **Sensation**: receptors in the skin detect touch, vibration, pressure, temperature (thermoreceptors), and pain, and send this information to coordination systems such as the nervous system [00:14:11].

##### Skin structure: epidermis, dermis, hypodermis
Skin covers almost 100% of the body surface [00:14:55].

**Epidermis**
- The most superficial layer; formed by epithelial tissue [00:14:55].
- It is a stratified epithelium, meaning several layers of cells [00:14:55].
- Cells in the deeper internal layer proliferate; newly formed cells differentiate and eventually die as they progress toward the surface [00:15:20].
- As an epithelium, the epidermis has no blood vessels; it is avascular [00:15:50].
- Its role is to form the barrier between the inner body and the outside [00:16:20].

**Dermis**
- Below the epidermis; a connective tissue [00:16:52].
- Contains few cells and abundant extracellular matrix, including collagen [00:16:52].
- Together, dermis and epidermis form the “skin proper” [00:16:52].
- Blood vessels run through the dermis; the epidermis receives nutrients by diffusion from the dermis because the two layers are adjacent [00:17:30].
- Contains sebaceous glands. In most of the body, sebaceous glands are associated with hair follicles and secrete into them; in hairless areas such as the lips, they secrete directly onto the skin surface [00:18:47].
- Hair follicles are invaginations of the epidermis that follow the hair down to the bulb and back to the surface [00:20:59].

**Hypodermis**
- The layer below the skin; not considered part of the skin proper [00:20:59].
- Contains the hair bulbs (origin of hair) [00:19:55].
- Contains sweat glands [00:22:00].
- The rest is mainly adipocytes, cells specialised in storing lipids; so the hypodermis is a fatty connective tissue [00:22:00].

##### Skeletal system: functions
The skeletal system includes bones, cartilage, and articulations [00:23:47].

- **Support**: structural support for the body [00:23:47].
- **Protection**: protects internal organs; examples are the brain inside the skull and the lungs and heart inside the thoracic cage [00:23:47].
- **Movement**: bones are points of attachment for muscles [00:23:47].
- **Mineral homeostasis**: helps regulate calcium and phosphorus concentrations in the blood/internal medium [00:23:47].
- **Blood cell production**: cavities inside bones contain red bone marrow, which continuously produces blood cells [00:24:40].
- **Energy storage**: yellow bone marrow inside bone cavities is formed by adipocytes, storing lipids, especially triglycerides, as an energy reservoir [00:25:00].

##### Muscular system: role in locomotion
- Skeletal muscles produce body movement and stabilise positions; they are the muscles of the locomotor system [00:25:47].
- Cardiac muscle pumps blood and is studied with the cardiovascular system [00:25:47].
- Smooth muscle participates in other systems by mobilising substances, for example blood in vessels and food in the intestines [00:26:30].

##### Bone classification
Bones can be classified by shape, and some by how or where they form [00:26:53].

**By shape**
- **Long bones**: one dimension is much longer than the other two; the typical long shape [00:28:00].
- **Flat bones**: high surface area and small thickness; example: bones of the cranium [00:28:19].
- **Short bones**: small bones with all three axes similar in length, roughly cuboid; example: carpal bones of the wrist [00:28:45].
- **Irregular bones**: bones without a simple reproducible shape, with many surface irregularities; example: vertebrae and some facial bones [00:29:51].

**By formation/location**
- **Sesamoid bones**: form inside tendons. The patella is a sesamoid bone and is present in almost all individuals. The number and position of sesamoid bones vary between people [00:30:55].
- **Sutural bones**: small bones found in the suture between two adjacent skull bones; they also vary in number and position between individuals [00:32:02].

**Adult skeleton numbers**
- 206 total bones [00:32:40].
- 80 axial skeleton bones and 126 appendicular skeleton bones [00:33:03].
- 22 skull bones; 26 vertebral column bones in adults because some vertebrae fuse: 5 sacral vertebrae form one bone and 4 coccygeal vertebrae form one [00:33:30].
- Thorax: ribs plus sternum = 25 bones [00:34:00].
- Hyoid: a special single bone in the neck, surrounded by muscle and not articulated with any other bone [00:34:20].
- Auditory ossicles in the ear are also part of the axial skeleton [00:34:52].
- Upper extremities: 60 bones total, including humerus, radius, ulna, carpals (18 per side), metacarpals (5) and phalanges (14) [00:34:52].
- Lower extremities: similar distribution in legs and feet; pelvic girdle included [00:35:56].
- The two patellae are the two sesamoid bones included in the 206; additional sutural and sesamoid bones vary by individual [00:35:56].

##### Bone tissue structure
- Bones are living tissue, not inert rigid material [00:37:10].
- Bone is a connective tissue whose extracellular matrix is mineralised with a high content of calcium phosphate; it also contains proteins such as collagen [00:37:10].
- The outer portion of a bone is dense **compact bone**; the inner portion is less dense **cancellous/spongy bone**, with spaces between mineralised structures [00:38:10].
- The spaces and the central cavity of long bones house bone marrow [00:38:10].

**Long bone regions**
- **Diaphysis**: the central shaft [00:39:14].
- **Epiphysis**: the ends [00:39:14].
- **Metaphysis**: the zone between diaphysis and epiphysis [00:39:14].
- The blood supply of the epiphysis is independent from that of the diaphysis; a line separates the two irrigation systems [00:39:50].
- Bone is highly vascularised. Bone surgery can bleed significantly, and sometimes patients need blood transfusions [00:40:19].
- Bone is also well innervated; bone lesions are painful because of abundant nerve endings [00:41:30].
- In adults, the central diaphysis cavity contains mostly yellow marrow; the epiphysis contains red bone marrow [00:42:21].

**Compact and cancellous bone**
- Compact bone lies on the surface of all bones; its thickness varies [00:43:47].
- The basic structural/functional unit of compact bone is the **osteon**: concentric rings of bone organised around a central channel containing blood vessels [00:43:47].
- Osteocytes trapped in bone have cell-membrane extensions that allow contact with surrounding cells, the central channel, or the bone surface, so they can receive nutrients and signals [00:44:00].
- Cancellous bone has spaces between mineralised structures; yellow marrow may occupy these spaces [00:44:52].

##### Bone growth, separate blood supplies, and remodelling
- During growth in childhood and adolescence, a long bone has a **primary ossification centre** in the diaphysis and a **secondary ossification centre** in the epiphysis [00:46:08].
- The two centres remain separated by a cartilage plate during growth [00:46:30].
- Cartilage is a connective tissue that does not allow blood vessels to pass through it [00:47:09].
- This separation is visible on X-rays of children as a non-rigid cartilage line; each bone closes at a particular age, the last ones in adulthood [00:47:09].
- When the epiphysis and diaphysis fuse after growth, the bone becomes one continuous structure, but the separate blood arrival patterns are maintained [00:48:12].
- Bone is dynamic throughout life: bone is constantly formed and degraded [00:49:00].
- **Osteoblasts** synthesise the mineralised matrix and, after becoming trapped, become **osteocytes** [00:49:23].
- **Osteoclasts** degrade bone [00:49:40].
- If osteoblast and osteoclast activity are similar, net bone growth is zero. During adolescence osteoblast activity predominates and bone forms; in ageing osteoclast activity may predominate, causing net bone loss [00:50:00].
- Bone formation removes calcium from blood; bone degradation releases calcium into blood. Therefore, balancing bone formation and resorption helps regulate blood calcium levels [00:50:38].

##### Skeletal muscle organisation
- There are about 700 muscles in the human body [00:51:39].
- Muscle cells are organised into bundles called **fascicles**; groups of fascicles form a whole muscle [00:51:39].
- Connective tissue surrounds each individual muscle cell, each fascicle, and the entire muscle [00:52:49].
- At the ends of the muscle, this connective tissue continues beyond the muscle cells and attaches to bone; that structure is the **tendon** [00:52:49].

##### Muscle cell structure: introduction
- Skeletal muscle cells are **multinucleated** because several precursor cells fuse to form a single larger cell [00:53:56].
- They have a striated appearance because of the internal organisation of proteins [00:54:00].
- Skeletal muscle cells require nerve arrival (innervation) to contract [00:54:57].
- Inside the cell, two major protein types form thick and thin filaments [00:54:57].
- These filaments are organised so precisely that they create functional units called **sarcomeres**, the contractile units of muscle [00:55:20].
- The next session will explain how the sarcomere organisation enables the multinucleated cell to change shape during contraction [00:56:59].

#### What the professor stressed
- For the integumentary system, the professor said the skin structure and functions covered are “the principal information I am planning for you to understand” [00:22:33].
- Bones are **living tissue**: rigidity does not mean they are inert [00:37:10].
- The **separate blood supply** between epiphysis and diaphysis was highlighted as important for understanding bone formation and irrigation [00:39:14], and he returned to it after explaining growth plates [00:48:12].
- The balance between **osteoblasts and osteoclasts** is central to bone growth, ageing-related bone loss, and calcium regulation [00:49:23].
- The sarcomere was introduced as the functional unit of muscle, with the promise to explain contraction in the next session [00:54:57].

#### Self-test

1. Why can skin be described as both a physical and a chemical immune barrier?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The epidermis/cells form a physical barrier separating the internal body from the outside. Chemical protection comes from secretions on the skin surface that help impede pathogen entry, so the barrier is both physical and chemical.
</details>

2. How does blood flow through the skin contribute to thermoregulation?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Skin acts as a blood reservoir, with about 10% of total blood flow passing through it. Regulating this blood flow changes how much heat is exchanged with the environment. Sweating adds further cooling because evaporation of sweat from the skin surface removes heat energy.
</details>

3. Which layers form the skin proper, and what lies below them?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The skin proper is formed by the epidermis (superficial avascular stratified epithelium) and the dermis (underlying vascular connective tissue). Below them is the hypodermis, which is not part of the skin proper and is mainly composed of adipocytes, while containing hair bulbs and sweat glands.
</details>

4. How does the epidermis receive nutrients if it has no blood vessels?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Nutrients arrive in the blood vessels of the dermis. Because the dermis and epidermis are directly adjacent, nutrients reach the epidermis by diffusion over the short distance between the two layers.
</details>

5. Why are sesamoid and sutural bones not classified as long, flat, short, or irregular?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
They are classified instead by how or where they form: sesamoid bones form inside tendons (e.g., patella), and sutural bones form within the sutures between adjacent skull bones. Their number and position also vary between individuals.
</details>

6. A child’s long-bone X-ray shows a cartilage line between the epiphysis and diaphysis. What does this indicate, and what changes in adulthood?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
It indicates the bone is still growing: a cartilage growth plate separates the primary ossification centre in the diaphysis from the secondary ossification centre in the epiphysis. Cartilage is avascular, so blood supplies are separate. At the end of growth the epiphysis and diaphysis fuse into one continuous bone, but the separate blood-arrival patterns remain.
</details>

7. What would happen to bone mass and blood calcium if osteoclast activity exceeded osteoblast activity?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Bone degradation would exceed bone formation, causing net loss of bone mass. Degrading bone releases calcium into the bloodstream, so blood calcium would tend to increase.
</details>

8. Explain how the structure of an osteon keeps trapped osteocytes alive in compact bone.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
An osteon is formed by concentric bone layers around a central channel containing blood vessels. Osteocytes trapped in the bone extend cell-membrane processes to contact surrounding cells, the central channel, or the bone surface, allowing them to receive nutrients and information.
</details>

9. How is a tendon related to the connective tissue coverings of a skeletal muscle?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Connective tissue surrounds each muscle cell, each fascicle, and the whole muscle. At the muscle ends, this connective tissue continues beyond the muscle cells and attaches to bone; this continuation is the tendon.
</details>

10. Why are skeletal muscle cells described as multinucleated and striated?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
They are multinucleated because several precursor cells fuse to form one larger cell. They appear striated because of the highly ordered internal arrangement of the thick and thin protein filaments that form sarcomeres.
</details>

#### Related course materials
- [U5-1-Systems (Integumentary / Locomotor)](materials/16_T5-1-SYSTEMS-1.pdf)

### 12. BE-Molecular Biology- Session 7 — Thu 20 Nov 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/15_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%207-20251120_140244-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/15_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%207-20251120_140244-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/15_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%207-20251120_140244-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary
This final session before the partial exam focused on practical work with the UCSC Genome Browser. The professor showed how to look up a specific gene (NR4A2) and display its transcript structure, strand, UTRs, CDS, conservation and population variation. The session then covered the Table Browser, a tool for bulk-downloading genome annotations such as transcript sequences, promoter regions, CDS coordinates, repeats and SNPs for whole chromosomes or the whole genome. The professor also explained the exam format, recommended study strategies, and gave guidance on Task 2.

- Next week’s partial exam covers all Unit 1 topics **except human genetic variation**; it combines short theoretical questions with practical database exercises.
- The exam is open-book for slides and databases, but artificial intelligence is not allowed; camera must be on and microphone off.
- UCSC Genome Browser is often faster than NCBI for extracting promoter, transcript, gene and protein sequences.
- In a gene track, thick exon blocks are CDS, thin blocks are UTRs; arrows pointing left mean a reverse gene.
- The Table Browser can download sequence or annotation tables for any track, for one position, a chromosome, or the whole genome.

#### Key concepts
- **Reverse vs forward gene** [00:13:34]: Transcription is always 5′→3′. If the UCSC arrows point left, the gene is transcribed from the complementary strand and is called a reverse gene; a minus strand in the coordinates confirms this.
- **Curated vs predicted RefSeq transcripts** [00:09:11]: NM (mRNA) and NP (protein) accessions are curated/reviewed; XM and XP accessions are computationally predicted and should not be fully relied on.
- **CDS and UTRs** [00:15:23]: CDS = coding sequence, shown as thicker blocks; UTRs are thinner non-coding blocks at the 5′ and 3′ ends of the transcript.
- **Promoter region** [00:19:44]: Sequence upstream of the gene, often the ~2,000 nucleotides before the transcription start, involved in regulating expression.
- **Conservation track** [00:29:29]: Alignment of the human region with orthologous sequences from other species; exons are more conserved than introns, and coding regions more than non-coding exons.
- **Common SNPs** [00:31:45]: Single-nucleotide variants common in healthy populations; not individually disease-causing, but combinations may contribute to complex disease.
- **Table Browser** [00:34:07]: UCSC tool that downloads sequence or annotation tables from any displayed track, for the whole genome, a chromosome, or a specific region.
- **Segmental duplications / retrotransposons / simple repeats** [01:04:55]: Different repeat classes available as separate UCSC tracks under the Repeats group.

#### Detailed notes

##### Exam format and scope
- The next session is the partial exam covering the first unit, the human genome [00:00:00].
- Included topics: introduction, duplication, repair, transcription, translation, human genome sequencing, the human genome and UCSC Genome Browser [00:01:00].
- Excluded: human genetic variation will not be in this exam; it will be covered in a later partial exam together with the human proteome and regulation of gene expression [00:01:00, 01:14:10].
- Exam format: short questions requiring short answers, **not multiple-choice** [00:02:01, 00:03:26].
- There will be theoretical questions and practical exercises using NCBI Gene, NCBI Genome/Genome Data Viewer and the UCSC Genome Browser [00:02:01].
- Slides and class materials may be used; AI is not allowed; camera must be on and microphone off, otherwise the exam is invalidated [00:02:01, 01:15:06].
- Estimated duration: about 1 hour to 1 hour 15 minutes [00:02:01].
- Study recommendation: understand the theoretical slides and repeat all exercises done in class [01:13:09].
- If something is unclear, students can email the professor or ask for a tutoring session [01:15:06].

##### Locating a gene and displaying its structure in UCSC
- Work flow: go to UCSC Genome Browser → Genome Browser; type the gene name, e.g. NR4A2, and select it [00:11:23].
- Hide all tracks, then in **Genes and Gene Predictions** display the **NCBI RefSeq** track in full or compact mode and refresh [00:12:33].
- The displayed region contains the genomic coordinates of the gene; these are the same coordinates found in the NCBI RefSeq genomic record [00:13:34].
- The track shows two curated transcripts with NM accessions. The arrows point left, meaning transcription goes to the left; since transcription is always 5′→3′, this is a **reverse gene** on the minus strand [00:13:34, 00:14:23].
- Reading the exon blocks for a reverse gene:
  - The leftmost block is the last exon (e.g. exon 8 of 8 for NR4A2) and contains the **3′ UTR** [00:15:23].
  - The rightmost exons contain the **5′ UTR** [00:15:23].
  - Thick parts of exon blocks are CDS; thin parts are UTR.
- Clicking on a transcript accession, e.g. NM_006186.4, opens its transcript page [00:16:29]:
  - **Transcript size** = exons only, no introns; in the example NR4A2 transcript size was 3,472 nt [00:17:32].
  - **Strand** is shown as minus for reverse genes [00:17:32].
  - Coordinates of the first and last nucleotide of the transcript [00:17:32].
  - Further down: **gene position** and gene size, including introns and exons [00:17:32].
- From the transcript page, links allow jumping to:
  - **Predicted protein** → FASTA protein sequence of that isoform [00:18:32].
  - **Predicted messenger RNA** → exons joined, no introns [00:18:32].
  - **Genomic sequence** → gene sequence including exons and introns [00:18:32].

##### Extracting specific sequences for one gene
- The genomic sequence page lets you choose what to display [00:18:32]:
  - **Promoter region**: e.g. 2,000 nucleotides upstream of the gene, something difficult to extract from NCBI manually [00:19:44].
  - **5′ UTR exons, CDS exons, 3′ UTR exons**: together equal the transcript sequence [00:19:44].
  - **Exons plus introns**: the full gene sequence [00:19:44].
- Format options include exons in uppercase and introns in lowercase, or only CDS in uppercase [00:20:49].
- Rule of thumb for where to go [00:27:22]:
  - Only exons / transcript → **messenger RNA**.
  - Protein → **predicted protein**.
  - Regions outside exons, intronic sequence, or promoter → **genomic sequence**.

##### Conservation and SNPs for a gene
- Conservation is displayed from the **Comparative Genomics** section, using the **100 vertebrates** track [00:24:50, 00:29:29].
- Select species for the multiple-sequence alignment, then submit; the track shows conservation across the gene window [00:29:29].
- Observed pattern: exonic regions show high conservation, intronic regions show low conservation [00:30:38].
- Explanation: exons are more functionally important; mutations in coding regions are more likely to disrupt protein structure/function. Coding regions are more conserved than non-coding exons, and non-coding exons are more conserved than introns [00:30:38].
- To display SNPs, go to **Variation** and select **dbSNP**; use pack and refresh [00:31:45].
- These are **common SNPs**: variants present in healthy populations, not directly disease-causing. Several SNPs combined, together with environmental factors, can increase disease predisposition [00:31:45].
- In the SNP track, be careful to check that all variant classes are selected if you want everything [00:31:45].

##### Table Browser: bulk data download
- Entry: UCSC main menu → **Tools** → **Table Browser** [00:34:07].
- The first settings choose the data source, equivalent to selecting a UCSC browser track:
  - **Genes and Gene Predictions** → e.g. NCBI RefSeq [00:35:11].
  - **Repeats** → Simple Repeats, Segmental Duplications, RepeatMasker [00:59:19].
  - **Variation** → dbSNP [01:09:23].
  - **Comparative Genomics** → conservation tracks [00:24:50].
- For the NCBI RefSeq track you can choose:
  - All transcripts, **curated only** (NM-coded transcripts), or predicted-only transcripts [00:36:14].
- Position options: whole genome, a chromosome, or a specific region [00:37:17].
- Output format can be **sequence** or **selected fields from primary and related tables** [00:37:17, 00:44:32].

###### Bulk sequence downloads
- To download all curated transcript sequences for the whole genome [00:38:17]:
  - Group: Genes and Gene Predictions; track: NCBI RefSeq; select curated only.
  - Position: genome.
  - Output format: sequence.
  - In sequence options, select 5′ UTR, CDS and 3′ UTR exons and **uncheck introns**; this gives transcripts, not genes.
  - To get gene sequences instead, include introns [00:39:33].
- To download all promoter regions for curated genes [00:40:35]:
  - Same settings as above, but in sequence options select only **promoter region**, e.g. 2,000 nucleotides upstream, and uncheck other boxes.
- To download CDS sequences for chromosome 7 [00:54:18]:
  - Position = chr7; sequence output; select only CDS.

###### Bulk annotation tables
- Instead of sequence output, choose **selected fields from primary and related tables** [00:44:32].
- Example: for all curated transcripts in the genome, select the transcript name, chromosome, strand, CDS start, CDS end, exon count and gene name (name2) [00:45:33].
- This produces a table that can be written to a file and then processed with Python, R, or basic Linux commands [00:57:11].
- Example: to get CDS coordinates for all RefSeq genes on chromosome 7 [00:51:57]:
  - Select all RefSeq genes, position chr7.
  - Output format: selected fields.
  - Choose CDS start, CDS end, transcript name.
  - The coordinates are genomic coordinates on chromosome 7, not transcript coordinates [00:54:18].
- Example: all genes in the genome with transcript name, gene name, strand and exon count [00:55:50].
  - The strand column lets you count forward vs reverse genes later [00:57:11].

###### Repeats and variation bulk downloads
- **Simple repeats** [00:59:19]:
  - Group: Repeats; track: Simple Repeats.
  - Fields: chromosome, start, end, repeat name, number of copies, length.
- **Segmental duplications** [01:04:55]:
  - Group: Repeats; track: Segmental Duplications.
  - Fields: chromosome, start, end of the duplication, strand, and the other chromosome/coordinates where the duplicated copy is found.
  - These are very large duplicated regions that can contain several genes [01:06:32].
- **Retrotransposons / jumping genes** [01:07:37]:
  - Group: Repeats; track: RepeatMasker.
  - Fields: position in the chromosome, repeat name, aligned positions elsewhere in the genome, and strand.
  - This shows that a retrotransposon sequence has “jumped” to another genomic region and that the two sequences can be aligned [01:09:23].
- **dbSNP for chromosome 1** [01:09:23]:
  - Group: Variation; track: dbSNP 155, common SNPs.
  - Position: chr1; selected fields.
  - Fields: chromosome, position, SNP name, reference nucleotide, alternate nucleotide, and variation class [01:10:42].

##### Task 2 guidance
- Task 2, on the human genome, is due the following week; doing it is recommended before the exam [01:12:01].
- Students can use NCBI and cross-check with UCSC Genome Browser [01:12:01].
- If asked for the first 20 nucleotides of the **gene**, use the **NC accession** (genomic RefSeq), not the NM transcript accession, because the question concerns the gene sequence [01:16:09].
- For reverse genes, manual coordinate extraction can be confusing; the UCSC Genome Browser can extract a 100-nucleotide promoter region directly, avoiding manual coordinate arithmetic [01:16:09].
- If using NCBI for a reverse gene promoter, take the highest coordinate of the gene and add 1–99 nucleotides downstream, but the professor recommended validating with UCSC [01:16:09].
- Tasks about exon positions or transcript sequences should be based on the gene sequence (NC) and the exon positions annotated within it; a small script can cut the exonic regions from the gene sequence if needed [01:20:12].

#### What the professor stressed
- The exam will mix theory with practical database exercises; simply memorizing slides is not enough, students must be able to operate NCBI and UCSC [00:02:01, 01:13:09].
- Always remember: NM/NP = curated; XM/XP = predicted and less reliable [00:09:11].
- Transcription is always 5′→3′; use this to decide whether a gene is forward or reverse [00:14:23, 00:22:45].
- Exons are more conserved than introns; coding sequence is more conserved than non-coding exons [00:30:38].
- UCSC is quicker and easier than NCBI for extracting promoter, gene, transcript and protein sequences [00:06:15, 00:19:44].
- The Table Browser is “very powerful” and useful for downloading any type of annotation for the whole genome [00:07:17, 00:34:07].
- Camera on and microphone off are mandatory for the exam; failing to have the camera on invalidates the exam [00:02:01, 01:15:06].
- Complete Task 2 and repeat all exercises before the exam [00:12:01, 01:13:09].

#### Course logistics mentioned
- **Partial exam**: next week; covers all Unit 1 topics except human genetic variation; short-answer only; allowed materials are slides, notes and databases; AI is not allowed; camera on, microphone off [00:00:00–00:03:26, 01:14:10].
- **Task 2**: due Wednesday after the session (26 November 2025); instructions in a Google Doc linked on the course page; uses NCBI and/or UCSC [01:12:01].
- **Later partial exam**: will include human genetic variation, the human proteome and regulation of gene expression [01:14:10].
- **Support**: professor available by email or tutoring session for questions on the concepts [01:15:06].

#### Self-test
1. In a UCSC gene track, the arrows point left. Is the gene forward or reverse, and how do you know?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> It is a reverse gene. Transcription is always 5′→3′, so if the direction of transcription points left, the gene must be transcribed from the complementary strand, which UCSC shows as the minus strand.</details>

2. For the reverse gene NR4A2, which end of the displayed gene contains the 5′ UTR and which contains the 3′ UTR?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> In the UCSC display, the rightmost exons contain the 5′ UTR and the leftmost exon contains the 3′ UTR, because transcription runs from right to left for a reverse gene.</details>

3. What is the practical difference between NM/NP accessions and XM/XP accessions?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> NM (transcript) and NP (protein) accessions are curated/reviewed in RefSeq and can be trusted. XM and XP accessions are computationally predicted and not yet curated, so they are less reliable.</details>

4. In UCSC’s 100-vertebrate conservation track, where would you expect the highest conservation in a gene, and why?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> The highest conservation is in exons, especially coding regions, because mutations there are more likely to disrupt protein structure and function. Introns and intergenic regions are less conserved.</details>

5. You want to download the transcript sequence of one specific gene from UCSC. Which link should you use: predicted mRNA, predicted protein, or genomic sequence?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> Predicted mRNA, because it gives the exons joined together without introns, which is the transcript sequence.</details>

6. In the Table Browser, how would you download all curated RefSeq transcript sequences for the whole human genome?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> Go to Tools → Table Browser; choose group Genes and Gene Predictions, track NCBI RefSeq, curated-only transcripts; set position to genome; set output format to sequence; then select 5′ UTR, CDS and 3′ UTR exons and uncheck introns before getting the sequence.</details>

7. If a task asks for 2,000 nucleotides upstream of every curated RefSeq gene, which UCSC sequence option should you select in the Table Browser?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> Use the gene/gene-predictions NCBI RefSeq curated track, position genome, output format sequence, then select only the promoter region and set the number of nucleotides to 2,000, unchecking the exon/intron options.</details>

8. You need a table with CDS start, CDS end and transcript name for all RefSeq genes on chromosome 7. Which output format and fields should you choose?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> Use NCBI RefSeq all genes, position chr7, output format “selected fields from primary and related tables”. Then select CDS start, CDS end, and transcript name; the coordinates will be genomic coordinates on chromosome 7.</details>

9. What is the difference between the Simple Repeats, Segmental Duplications and RepeatMasker tracks in the context of the UCSC Repeats group?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> Simple Repeats are tandem repeats; Segmental Duplications are large duplicated genomic regions that may contain genes; RepeatMasker can be used to display retrotransposons/jumping elements and where their sequences align elsewhere in the genome.</details>

10. A student is asked for the first 20 nucleotides of a gene. Should they use the NC or NM accession, and why?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> The NC accession, because it represents the genomic sequence of the gene, including introns and flanking regions. NM is the mRNA/transcript sequence, which lacks introns and is not the full gene sequence.</details>

11. Why are common dbSNP variants not considered directly disease-causing, even though they may be relevant to disease?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> They are present in healthy populations and individually do not cause disease. However, combinations of several common SNPs, together with environmental factors, can increase predisposition to complex diseases.</details>

12. How can you quickly extract a 100-nucleotide promoter region for a reverse gene without manually calculating coordinates?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary> Use the UCSC Genome Browser genomic sequence page for the gene, select promoter region, set 100 nucleotides upstream, and download the sequence. This avoids having to work out forward/reverse coordinate arithmetic manually.</details>

### 13. BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251124 — Mon 24 Nov 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/16_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251124-20251124_170718-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/16_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251124-20251124_170718-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/16_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251124-20251124_170718-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary
This session completed the locomotor system by explaining how skeletal muscle cells are organised and how contraction happens at the molecular level (sarcomeres, action potentials, calcium and filament sliding), and by classifying joints. It then started the cardiovascular system: blood as a liquid connective tissue, its cells and plasma, erythrocytes and haemoglobin, and the three mechanisms of haemostasis. The second half focused on the heart: its anatomy, the pulmonary and systemic circuits, the key distinction between arteries/veins and oxygenated/deoxygenated blood, cardiac muscle electrical coupling, autorhythmic cells, the ECG, and the five-step cardiac cycle.

Key takeaways:
- Muscle contraction results from calcium release triggering thin filaments to slide over thick filaments, shortening each sarcomere.
- Joints are classified as synarthrosis (immobile), amphiarthrosis (small movement), or synovial (free movement).
- Blood is a liquid connective tissue with plasma, erythrocytes, leukocytes and platelets, and performs transport, regulation and protection functions.
- Haemostasis has three distinct but interconnected mechanisms: vascular spasm, platelet plug, and coagulation (fibrin clot).
- Artery/vein refers only to direction of flow relative to the heart, not to oxygen content; pulmonary and systemic circuits differ.
- The heart beats autonomously because autorhythmic cells initiate action potentials that propagate through gap junctions between cardiac muscle cells.
- The cardiac cycle can be understood as five steps: atrial contraction, isovolumetric ventricular contraction, ventricular ejection, isovolumetric relaxation, and passive ventricular filling.

#### Key concepts
- **Sarcomere**: the functional contractile unit of skeletal and cardiac muscle, made of thick and thin filaments arranged as thin–thick–thin; shortens during contraction.
- **Transverse (T) tubules**: invaginations of the muscle cell membrane that carry action potentials into the interior of the cell.
- **Sarcoplasmic reticulum**: muscle endoplasmic reticulum; stores and releases calcium when triggered by the action potential.
- **Synarthrosis**: immobile joint formed by connective tissue/ligaments, e.g. between cranial bones.
- **Amphiarthrosis**: joint allowing small movement, typically with a cartilaginous disc, e.g. between vertebrae.
- **Synovial joint**: freely movable joint with an outer ligamentous membrane, an inner membrane producing lubricating synovial fluid, and cartilage over bone ends.
- **Agonist/antagonist**: two muscles acting on the same joint in opposite directions; the agonist contracts while the antagonist relaxes.
- **Liquid connective tissue**: blood; few cells in a liquid extracellular matrix (plasma).
- **Erythrocyte**: red blood cell, anucleate, organelle-free, filled with haemoglobin; lives ~3 months.
- **Leukocyte**: white blood cell; includes granulocytes (neutrophils, eosinophils, basophils) and agranulocytes (monocytes, lymphocytes).
- **Platelet**: small membrane-bound fragment of a megakaryocyte; participates in haemostasis.
- **Haemoglobin**: protein with four globin subunits, each containing a haem group with an iron atom; binds up to four O₂ molecules.
- **Haemostasis**: the set of mechanisms that stop bleeding: vascular spasm, platelet plug, coagulation.
- **Coagulation**: cascade conversion of soluble fibrinogen into insoluble fibrin, forming a clot that traps blood cells.
- **Extrinsic pathway**: coagulation triggered when blood contacts tissue factor outside the vessel.
- **Intrinsic pathway**: coagulation triggered by platelet activation inside the blood.
- **Autorhythmic cells**: cardiac cells in a node that generate action potentials spontaneously at regular intervals.
- **Systole**: contraction of heart muscle; atrial systole precedes ventricular systole.
- **ECG**: recording of electrical activity of the heart from the body surface; P wave, QRS complex, T wave.

#### Detailed notes

##### 1. Skeletal muscle cell structure and contraction
[00:00:00–00:07:41]

- Each skeletal muscle cell has the usual plasma membrane, plus invaginations called **transverse (T) tubules** that let the membrane reach the cell interior.
- Inside the cell there is **endoplasmic reticulum** (sarcoplasmic reticulum) and **myofibrils**.
- Myofibrils are highly organised bundles of proteins forming functional units called **sarcomeres**.
- Each sarcomere contains **thick filaments** (central) and **thin filaments** (at the two sides), arranged as thin–thick–thin; many sarcomeres are attached in series along the long cell.
- Contraction means the whole muscle shortens and thickens; this requires each individual cell to contract.
- Skeletal muscle can only be activated by the arrival of **neurotransmitters** from a nerve terminal; each muscle cell needs one nerve terminal.
- Sequence of contraction:
  1. Neurotransmitter is released at the synapse onto the muscle cell.
  2. The muscle cell membrane undergoes an **action potential** (due to sodium entry and potassium exit).
  3. The action potential propagates along the membrane and into the T tubules.
  4. Because T tubules are close to the endoplasmic reticulum, this triggers **release of calcium** from the ER.
  5. Calcium helps thin filaments attach to thick filaments and move over them toward the centre of the sarcomere.
- As every sarcomere in the cell shortens simultaneously, the whole cell shortens.

##### 2. Joints (articulations)
[00:07:41–00:15:24]

- Joints are the parts that keep adjacent bones in contact; most are formed by connective tissue.
- Three functional/structural classes:
  1. **Synarthrosis** – immobile; connective tissue/ligaments directly attach bones; e.g. bones of the cranium.
  2. **Amphiarthrosis** – allows small movement; has a cartilaginous disc; e.g. discs between adjacent vertebrae.
  3. **Synovial** – allows free movement; complex structure.
- Synovial joint structure:
  - Outer membrane is like a ligament.
  - Inner membrane contains cells that produce **synovial fluid**, filling the cavity and lubricating movement to avoid tissue damage.
  - A layer of **cartilage** covers the bone ends next to the fluid.
- Note: the functional and structural classifications usually match but not always perfectly.
- Many joints have **two muscles** associated, producing opposite movements.
  - Example: at the elbow, contraction of one muscle produces **flexion**; contraction of the other produces **extension**.
  - **Agonist** = the muscle that contracts to produce a given movement; **antagonist** = the muscle that relaxes.
  - In flexion, the flexor is agonist and extensor is antagonist; in extension, the roles reverse.
- Movement types: **angular** (reducing or increasing the angle between bones), **sliding**, **rotation**, and special movements; angular and rotation are most common in limbs. The most important joints for movement are associated with the shoulder girdle and pelvic girdle.

##### 3. Cardiovascular system overview and blood components
[00:15:45–00:25:00]

- Components: **blood** (a liquid connective tissue), **heart**, and **vessels**.
- Blood is a connective tissue: few cells and a large extracellular matrix, here liquid plasma.
- Functions:
  - Transport of nutrients from intestines/lungs to tissues.
  - Transport of residues from tissues to removal sites (kidneys, lungs for CO₂).
  - Transport of heat, helping regulate body temperature.
  - Regulation of internal conditions: pH, water amount in cells, etc.
  - Protection: contains immune system cells; protects against blood loss via coagulation.
- Blood properties: temperature ~38°C, slightly basic pH, dense and viscous, represents ~8% of total body mass, total volume ~4–6 litres depending on the person.
- Two main parts:
  - **Liquid (plasma)**: mostly water, but ~7% proteins (albumin, globulins including antibodies, coagulation proteins), plus small molecules like glucose, cholesterol, urea, creatinine, ions.
  - **Corpuscular elements**: erythrocytes, leukocytes, and platelets.
- **Erythrocytes (red blood cells)** are the most abundant.
- **Leukocytes (white blood cells)** are a mixture:
  - Granulocytes: **neutrophils, eosinophils, basophils** (contain specific granules).
  - Agranulocytes: **monocytes** and **lymphocytes**.
- **Platelets** are not cells but **fragments of cells**, ~1 µm, fully membrane-enclosed.
  - Produced by fragmentation of **megakaryocytes** in red bone marrow.
  - Erythrocytes are produced from **reticulocytes** in red bone marrow; reticulocytes expel their nucleus and become erythrocytes, which therefore have **no nucleus and no organelles**.
- Some blood cells enter tissues for specific tasks:
  - Monocytes can become **macrophages**.
  - B lymphocytes can become antibody-producing cells in tissues.

##### 4. Erythrocytes and haemoglobin
[00:25:00–00:29:35]

- Erythrocytes are small (7–8 µm diameter), biconcave discs, survive ~3 months, produced from reticulocytes after nuclear expulsion.
- They are essentially bags filled with **haemoglobin**.
- Haemoglobin structure:
  - Four protein subunits called **globins**.
  - Each globin contains a **haem group**, an organic molecule (not protein) with an **iron atom** at its centre.
  - Each iron binds one O₂, so one haemoglobin carries up to **four O₂ molecules**.
- After erythrocyte death, components are recycled:
  - Globin proteins → amino acids for reuse.
  - Haem group → **bilirubin**, excreted in urine or faeces, giving the typical colour of these residues; the haem group also gives blood its red colour.
  - Iron atoms are recycled and transported bound to **ferritin**, a blood protein.
- Because erythrocytes are numerous but short-lived, recycling is essential for producing new ones.

##### 5. Haemostasis: stopping bleeding
[00:29:35–00:37:26]

- Blood must be kept inside vessels; if a vessel is damaged, mechanisms are needed to stop bleeding.
- **Three main mechanisms**:
  1. **Vascular spasm** – vasoconstriction at the injury site; muscular contraction reduces blood arriving to the lesion.
  2. **Platelet plug** – platelets aggregate and attach to each other, forming a plug that blocks the lesion.
  3. **Coagulation** – formation of a clot.
- Coagulation is different from the platelet plug:
  - It involves soluble proteins in plasma; among the 7% of plasma proteins, the key one is **fibrinogen**.
  - Fibrinogen is converted into **fibrin**, which aggregates and forms a **clot**.
  - Fibrin fibres trap anything moving nearby, including erythrocytes and platelets.
- Coagulation pathways are **cascades** of sequential steps, ending in fibrinogen → fibrin conversion.
  - **Extrinsic pathway**: initiated when blood contacts **tissue factor** from surrounding tissues after injury.
  - **Intrinsic pathway**: initiated by **platelet activation** from inside the blood.
- The platelet plug and coagulation are interconnected:
  - Platelet plug formation activates the intrinsic pathway, producing secondary clot.
  - Clot formation traps platelets, which become activated and help produce the platelet plug.
- There are also control mechanisms of anticoagulation to avoid unspecific clot formation.

##### 6. Heart, circulation and the artery/vein distinction
[00:37:40–00:43:06]

- Blood moves because heart muscle cells contract and pump it through vessels.
- Blood reaches almost every body area, with exceptions: **epithelia** and **cartilage** are not irrigated.
- Heart anatomy: four compartments — two **atria** and two **ventricles**.
  - Veins bring blood to the atria.
  - Arteries carry blood away from the heart to the body.
- Two circuits:
  - **Pulmonary circulation**: heart → lungs → heart.
  - **Systemic circulation**: heart → all body tissues → heart.
- Colour coding: blood leaving the heart toward the lungs is **deoxygenated** (blue); blood leaving the heart toward tissues is **oxygenated** (red).
  - Blood returning from the body tissues is deoxygenated.
  - Blood returning from the lungs is oxygenated.
- Important distinction stressed by the professor:
  - **Artery** only means a vessel that leaves the heart.
  - **Vein** only means a vessel that returns blood to the heart.
  - Oxygen content is independent of this: the pulmonary artery carries deoxygenated blood, and pulmonary veins carry oxygenated blood.

##### 7. Cardiac muscle, electrical activity and the ECG
[00:43:06–00:49:21]

- Cardiac muscle cells are **striated** like skeletal muscle but are **branched** and touch each other.
- Adjacent cardiac cells have **gap junctions** connecting cytoplasm to cytoplasm, allowing action potentials to propagate from one cell to its neighbours.
- Contraction mechanism is similar to skeletal muscle: action potential → calcium release from endoplasmic reticulum → sarcomere shortening.
- **Autorhythmic cells** in a specific **node** of the heart can spontaneously produce action potentials at regular intervals, without neural stimulation.
- Because all cardiac cells are connected by gap junctions, the impulse from the node spreads through the heart; this explains why the heart pumps regularly without neural input.
- Contraction is called **systole**. There are two contractions per pumping cycle: **atrial systole** first, then **ventricular systole** shortly after.
- This desynchronisation drives blood movement and is detectable externally.
- **Electrocardiogram (ECG)** records the heart’s electrical activity from the body surface:
  - **P wave**: electrical activation leading to atrial contraction.
  - **QRS complex**: activation of ventricular cells (action potential).
  - **T wave**: relaxation of the heart muscle cells.

##### 8. The cardiac cycle: five steps
[00:49:21–00:52:37]

1. **Atrial contraction** – blood moves from atria into ventricles.
2. **Isovolumetric ventricular contraction** – ventricles begin to contract; the valves between atria and ventricles close because they allow flow in only one direction; volume stays constant while pressure rises until it is high enough to open the outflow valves.
3. **Ventricular ejection** – after enough pressure builds, the outflow valves open and blood is ejected from the ventricles.
4. **Isovolumetric relaxation** – ventricles begin to relax; all valves are closed; volume is constant while pressure falls.
5. **Ventricular filling** – when ventricular pressure drops enough, the atrioventricular valves reopen and blood flows into the ventricles, before a new cycle starts.

The professor asked students to review this five-step scheme and be able to connect muscle contraction, the electrical stimulus seen on the ECG, and blood movement.

#### What the professor stressed
- **Do not confuse artery/vein with oxygenated/deoxygenated blood.** Artery means leaving the heart; vein means returning to the heart. The pulmonary artery carries deoxygenated blood; pulmonary veins carry oxygenated blood. This was explicitly flagged as a common mistake.
- **The three haemostasis mechanisms are distinct but interconnected** — vascular spasm, platelet plug, and coagulation; platelet plug and clot formation activate each other bidirectionally.
- **The cardiac cycle five-step scheme is complex and exam-relevant**; the professor explicitly asked students to recheck and ensure they can explain how contraction, electrical activity (ECG), and blood movement are linked.
- **Skeletal muscle contraction absolutely requires a nerve terminal** for each muscle cell; activation cannot occur without neurotransmitter release.
- **Autorhythmic cells and gap junctions** explain how the heart pumps regularly without neural input — an important concept for understanding heart function.

#### Course logistics mentioned
None.

#### Self-test
1. Explain the sequence of events from nerve terminal activation to shortening of a skeletal muscle cell. Which role does calcium play?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Neurotransmitter is released from the nerve terminal and binds to the muscle cell, triggering an action potential in the membrane. The action potential propagates into the transverse tubules, which are close to the sarcoplasmic reticulum. This causes calcium release from the ER. Calcium promotes attachment of thin filaments to thick filaments and their sliding toward the centre of each sarcomere, shortening the sarcomeres and therefore the whole cell. Calcium is the key signal that links electrical excitation to filament movement.
</details>

2. Compare synarthrosis, amphiarthrosis and synovial joints in terms of movement and structure, with an example of each.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Synarthrosis: immobile; bones joined directly by connective tissue/ligaments; example: cranial bones. Amphiarthrosis: allows small movement; contains a cartilaginous disc; example: joints between adjacent vertebrae. Synovial: freely movable; has an outer ligamentous membrane, an inner membrane producing synovial fluid, and cartilage on bone ends; example: shoulder or elbow.
</details>

3. What are the main components and functions of blood?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Blood consists of plasma (water, ~7% proteins including albumin, globulins and coagulation factors, plus small solutes) and formed elements: erythrocytes, leukocytes and platelets. Functions: transport of nutrients, oxygen and waste; transport of heat; regulation of temperature, pH and water; protection via immune cells and prevention of blood loss through coagulation.
</details>

4. Why can an erythrocyte be described as “a bag of haemoglobin”, and how is its contents recycled after cell death?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Erythrocytes lose their nucleus and organelles, leaving a biconcave cell packed mostly with haemoglobin. Haemoglobin has four globin subunits, each with a haem group containing iron that binds one O₂, so each molecule carries up to four O₂. After death, globins are broken down into amino acids; haem is converted to bilirubin and excreted in urine/faeces (contributing colour); iron is recycled and transported by ferritin.
</details>

5. Distinguish the platelet plug and coagulation. How are the extrinsic and intrinsic coagulation pathways initiated?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The platelet plug is formed by platelets aggregating at the lesion site. Coagulation is a separate cascade that converts soluble fibrinogen into insoluble fibrin, forming a clot that traps cells. The extrinsic pathway is triggered when blood contacts tissue factor from surrounding injured tissues. The intrinsic pathway is triggered by platelet activation inside the blood. The two mechanisms are interconnected: platelet plugs activate the intrinsic pathway, and clots trap and activate platelets.
</details>

6. A patient has blood in the pulmonary artery. Is it oxygenated or deoxygenated? Explain the principle behind your answer.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Deoxygenated. Artery means a vessel leaving the heart, regardless of oxygen content. The pulmonary artery leaves the right ventricle to send blood to the lungs for oxygenation, so it carries deoxygenated blood. Similarly, pulmonary veins return oxygenated blood from the lungs to the heart.
</details>

7. How do cardiac muscle cells differ from skeletal muscle cells in terms of activation and impulse propagation?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Both are striated and use sarcomeres for contraction. However, cardiac muscle cells are branched and connected by gap junctions, so an action potential can propagate directly from one cell to adjacent cells. Additionally, the heartbeat is initiated by autorhythmic cells in a node that generate action potentials spontaneously; skeletal muscle requires nerve terminals to activate each cell.
</details>

8. What does each wave of the ECG represent, and how do they relate to the cardiac cycle?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
P wave: electrical activation leading to atrial contraction. QRS complex: activation/action potential of ventricular muscle cells. T wave: relaxation of heart muscle cells. These electrical events correspond to atrial systole, then ventricular systole, then relaxation of the heart.
</details>

9. Describe the five steps of the cardiac cycle, including when the atrioventricular valves are open or closed.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
1) Atrial contraction: AV valves open, blood moves from atria to ventricles. 2) Isovolumetric ventricular contraction: ventricles contract, AV valves close, volume constant, pressure rises. 3) Ventricular ejection: pressure opens outflow valves, blood is ejected. 4) Isovolumetric relaxation: ventricles relax, all valves closed, volume constant, pressure falls. 5) Ventricular filling: AV valves reopen and blood flows passively into ventricles before the next cycle.
</details>

10. Why do skeletal muscle cells need transverse tubules, and what would happen if calcium release from the sarcoplasmic reticulum were blocked?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Transverse tubules carry the surface action potential deep into the cell, allowing the electrical signal to reach the sarcoplasmic reticulum and trigger calcium release. If calcium release were blocked, thin filaments would not be able to slide over thick filaments, so sarcomeres would not shorten and the muscle could not contract.
</details>
