### 15. BE-Molecular Biology- Session 9 — Thu 04 Dec 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/18_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%209-20251204_140459-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/18_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%209-20251204_140459-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/18_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%209-20251204_140459-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary

This session covered the biology and classification of human genetic variation: from the basic vocabulary of population genetics (locus, allele, genotype, phenotype, allele frequencies) to the molecular types of DNA change (chromosomal, structural, small-scale and single-nucleotide variants). The professor explained how germline mutations differ from somatic mutations and why only germline variants are transmitted; how common SNPs are organised into haplotypes by linkage disequilibrium; why most variants are neutral but a minority cause disease; and how monogenic and complex diseases differ. She also introduced loss-of-function versus gain-of-function mutations, inheritance patterns, and how causative variants are identified in patients.

Key takeaways:

- A single-nucleotide variant (SNV) is the most common type of human DNA variation; most variation falls outside coding regions.
- Most variants identified in human populations are rare; most variants carried by a single individual are common.
- Only mutations in germ cells are transmitted to children; somatic mutations can contribute to cancer, neurodegeneration and ageing, but are not inherited.
- Every child is born with roughly 60–80 de novo mutations not present in the parents.
- Gain-of-function mutations are usually more disease-causing than loss-of-function mutations.
- The accuracy of computational tools for predicting variant pathogenicity is still low; many variants of uncertain significance require wet-lab validation.

#### Key concepts

- **Locus / loci**: a region of DNA with a unique chromosomal location.
- **Allele**: one of the different sequence versions present at a given locus.
- **Genotype**: the combination of alleles at a locus, considering both copies of the genome (e.g. AA, AC, CC).
- **Phenotype**: the observable expression of a genotype as a trait (e.g. epilepsy, blindness).
- **Homozygous / heterozygous**: having identical versus different alleles at a locus on the two homologous chromosomes.
- **Allele frequency**: how frequent a particular allele is in a population.
- **Minor allele frequency (MAF)**: the frequency of the less common allele in a population.
- **Germline mutation**: a DNA change in germ cells that can be transmitted to offspring.
- **Somatic mutation**: a DNA change acquired in non-germ cells during life; not transmitted to offspring.
- **De novo mutation**: a mutation not inherited from the parents, arising in the germline or very early.
- **Chromosomal variant**: change in chromosome number (aneuploidy).
- **Structural variant**: DNA change affecting more than 50 nucleotides, up to hundreds of thousands.
- **Small-scale variant**: DNA change affecting fewer than 50 nucleotides: STRs, short indels, SNVs.
- **SNV (single nucleotide variant) / SNP (single nucleotide polymorphism)**: a one-nucleotide change; SNP usually refers to common variants in populations.
- **Frameshift**: insertion/deletion that changes the triplet reading frame, often producing a truncated protein.
- **Synonymous variant**: nucleotide change that does not alter the amino acid, because the genetic code is degenerate.
- **Non-synonymous variant**: nucleotide change that alters the amino acid; includes missense and nonsense.
- **Haplotype**: a set of SNPs that tend to be inherited together as a block.
- **Linkage disequilibrium (LD)**: non-random association of alleles at nearby loci; they are inherited together more often than expected by chance.
- **Tag SNP**: a SNP used to infer the other common SNPs in the same haplotype block.
- **Heritability**: the proportion of phenotypic variation in a trait attributable to genetic effects.
- **Monogenic disease**: disease caused by mutation(s) in a single gene.
- **Complex disease**: disease caused by many genetic variants plus environmental factors.
- **GWAS**: genome-wide association study; case–control method to find SNPs/environmental factors associated with complex disease.
- **Pleiotropy**: one genetic variant affecting two or more unrelated traits.
- **Loss-of-function / gain-of-function**: mutations that reduce or abolish, versus increase, protein activity.

#### Detailed notes

##### 1. Basic genetic variation vocabulary

[00:00:00] The professor began with four individuals, each carrying a pair of chromosome 1 homologues. At a given **locus**—a specific position with a unique chromosomal location—the two copies can have identical or different sequences.

- If the two copies at the locus are identical, the individual is **homozygous** for that locus.
- If they differ, the individual is **heterozygous**.
- Example: individuals can be AA, CC, or AC. [00:01:56]

The **allele** is the specific sequence version at the locus, the **genotype** is the combination of both alleles, and the **phenotype** is how that genotype is expressed as a trait (e.g. a clinical phenotype such as epilepsy or blindness). [00:04:04]

##### 2. Genotype and allele frequencies in populations

[00:05:07] She gave a population example with three genotypes at one locus:

| Genotype | Number of individuals |
|----------|----------------------|
| AA       | 360                  |
| Aa       | 480                  |
| aa       | 160                  |

From these numbers (total 1,000 individuals), genotype frequencies are 0.36, 0.48 and 0.16. Allele frequencies can be computed from the genotype counts: A = (2×360 + 480) / 2000 = 0.6; a = 0.4. The **minor allele frequency** is the frequency of the less common allele, so here a = 0.4. [00:06:11]

These frequencies can be calculated for specific populations or ethnic groups. [00:07:17]

##### 3. Where mutations come from: germline, somatic and de novo changes

[00:07:17] DNA changes occur throughout life. They can occur in:

- **Germ cells**: if the mutation is in the parental germline, all cells of the resulting individual—somatic and germ—may carry it.
- **Somatic cells**: if a mutation arises later in development or post-natally, only the descendants of that cell are affected. The later in development, the fewer cells are affected.
- **De novo mutations**: changes not inherited from parents, arising in the first cells or later. [00:08:20]

Causes of mutation include DNA replication errors, oxidative molecules produced by metabolism, exogenous damage (radiation, X-rays, plant toxins, chemicals, viruses), recombination errors, chromosome segregation errors, and retrotransposition. [00:09:21]

##### 4. Three classes of DNA sequence variation

[00:12:26] DNA sequence variation is classified into three broad groups.

**Chromosomal variants** are changes in chromosome number caused by errors in chromosome segregation. They can produce fewer or more chromosomes than normal. Example: trisomy 21 (three copies of chromosome 21) causes Down syndrome. Sex chromosome aneuploidies are generally better tolerated than autosomal ones. The professor was not aware of a viable monosomy syndrome and suspected that having only one copy of an autosome is generally lethal. [00:12:26]

**Structural variants** affect more than 50 nucleotides, sometimes hundreds of thousands. They include:
- Duplications / copy number variants (CNVs)
- Segmental duplications
- Inversions
- Large deletions
- Large insertions
- Translocations
- Retrotransposition events (RNA → DNA inserted back into the genome)

CNVs can duplicate one gene or a whole set of genes (e.g. 15–20 genes) and are related to disease. [00:16:16]

**Small-scale variants** affect fewer than 50 nucleotides. They include:
- Short tandem repeats (STRs)
- Short indels (insertions/deletions of a few nucleotides)
- Single nucleotide variants (SNVs)

SNVs, plus small indels, are the major type of change caused by DNA replication and repair errors. About 99.9% of these replication/repair errors are SNVs or small-scale insertions/deletions, with SNVs being the most common. Because coding regions are only about 1% of the genome, most small-scale variants fall in non-coding or intergenic regions. [00:17:23]

##### 5. Single nucleotide variants and their coding consequences

[00:18:57] SNVs can be:
- **Substitutions**: one base replaced, e.g. T → C.
- **Insertions**: an extra nucleotide added.
- **Deletions**: a nucleotide removed.
- **Inversions**: nucleotides changing position.

If an insertion or deletion occurs in a coding region and is not a multiple of three, it shifts the **reading frame**. This changes the translated amino acid sequence from that point onward, often creating a **premature stop codon** and a truncated protein. [00:20:22]

SNVs in coding regions can be:
- **Synonymous**: the nucleotide changes but the amino acid does not, because the genetic code is degenerate. Example: TGT and TGC both code for cysteine. [00:21:25]
- **Non-synonymous**:
  - **Missense**: one amino acid is replaced by another. Example: TGT (cysteine) → TGG (tryptophan). [00:24:17]
  - **Nonsense**: a premature stop codon is introduced, truncating the protein.

The professor noted that synonymous variants can still influence phenotype, for example through effects on regulation or splicing, even though the protein sequence is unchanged. [00:23:14]

Insertions and deletions that cause frameshifts, and nonsense substitutions, usually produce shorter proteins that may lose function. Missense changes may be neutral, damaging, or even beneficial depending on the protein region affected. [00:25:21]

##### 6. Haplotypes and linkage disequilibrium

[00:26:53] By analysing many genomes, scientists observed that some SNPs are correlated: if you have an A at one position, you are more likely to have a T at another nearby position. This arises because chromosome recombination occurs at certain positions, so blocks of sequence are inherited together as **haplotypes**.

- **Linkage disequilibrium (LD)** is the occurrence of allele combinations more often or less often than expected by chance.
- Two nearby loci are in LD when they are inherited together more often than expected.
- The human genome can be thought of as made of **blocks of DNA** that are inherited together, each block containing a set of SNPs. [00:29:04]

This is practically useful: by genotyping a **tag SNP**, one can infer the other common SNPs in the same block, without sequencing the whole genome. This applies to common variants. [00:30:14]

##### 7. Human population variation and variant frequency distributions

[00:30:14] The human genome has about 3×10⁹ nucleotides, and populations differ at about **4–5 million positions**, most of them single-nucleotide positions. African populations show the highest genetic diversity. Population studies are based on **germ cells**, not somatic mutations. [00:31:27]

Among human DNA variants:
- SNVs account for about **87%**
- Short indels account for about **13%**
- Structural variants are very low in percentage. [00:32:27]

Considering the 4–5 million variant sites identified in populations:
- **10%** are common (frequency > 5%)
- **14%** are low-frequency variants
- **76%** are very rare (frequency < 0.5%)

Most variants within any single individual are common, but most variants identified across human populations are very rare, present in only a few or even one individual. Only an estimated **1.4%** of variants in an individual are rare. [00:33:32] The haplotype/SNP discussion refers to the **common** variants. [00:35:36]

##### 8. Somatic mutations in cancer, neurodegeneration and ageing

[00:35:36] The mutation rate in somatic cells is higher than in germ cells. A somatic mutation is passed only to the descendants of the mutated cell, so surrounding cells derived from that original cell are affected. If the mutation hits genes regulating **cell proliferation or apoptosis**, uncontrolled cell growth and cancer can result. Most cancer-causing mutations are somatic, not inherited. Somatic mutations are also linked to neurodegenerative disorders and to ageing: accumulated mutations impair gene function and tissue function over time. [00:36:37]

Only mutations in germ cells are transmitted to children. A somatic mutation cannot be passed on. Inherited germline mutations in cancer-related genes, such as **BRCA2**, usually confer high **predisposition** to cancer, not the disease itself. In a monogenic dominant disease, however, having the mutation means having the disease. [00:38:49]

##### 9. Germline mutation rate and de novo mutations

[00:45:13] The germline substitution rate is about **1 × 10⁻⁸ mutations per nucleotide per generation**. This varies between populations and families, and the male germline mutation rate is generally higher than the female germline rate. Many germline mutations arise from unrepaired replication errors.

On average, every child carries about **60–80 de novo mutations** not inherited from the parents. Most fall outside coding regions because most of the genome is non-coding, but both coding and non-coding changes can be disease-causing. [00:46:13]

##### 10. Disease-causing variants vs neutral and beneficial variants

[00:48:33] Most sequence variants in human populations are **not disease-causing**. Only a small fraction lead to disease or disease predisposition. Harmful mutations tend to reduce reproductive fitness and survival, so they are often not transmitted to future generations. [00:49:35]

A very small minority of variants can have a **beneficial** effect and may increase reproductive fitness, so positive selection can increase their frequency. Most variants are **neutral**. The functional consequences and mutation rates vary across genomic regions. [00:51:23]

##### 11. Molecular pathology: how a variant causes a phenotype

[00:52:24] Molecular pathology seeks to explain why a given genetic change causes a clinical phenotype. This is difficult because each person differs from the reference genome at millions of positions, and many variants are unique to one individual.

Factors influencing whether a SNV causes disease:
- Whether it falls in a coding or non-coding region
- Whether it changes an amino acid
- Whether the changed amino acid is in a functionally critical region (e.g. a binding site)
- Whether the affected gene is essential for the organism

Variants can damage the organism through coding-region changes, but also through non-coding changes affecting **gene expression, RNA stability or RNA splicing**. [00:54:39]

Functional variants can be:
- **Loss-of-function**: the protein loses function (e.g. misfolding, truncation, large amino acid change). Some loss-of-function mutations are not disease-causing if the gene is non-essential or if 50% of normal product remains sufficient.
- **Gain-of-function**: the protein has higher or constitutive activity. Most gain-of-function mutations are disease-causing. It is often worse to increase protein activity than to reduce it. [00:57:48]

Therapeutic logic:
- Loss-of-function → design therapies that **activate** the protein or provide agonists.
- Gain-of-function → design therapies that **reduce or block** protein activity. [00:59:24]

Example: **NMDA receptors** are membrane channels in neurons activated by glutamate. A loss-of-function mutation prevents the channel from opening. A gain-of-function mutation makes the channel open constantly, which is associated with **epilepsy**. Gain-of-function often acts like a switch stuck “on”. [01:00:24]

##### 12. How clinicians try to identify a causative mutation

[01:01:19] For a patient with an undiagnosed condition, usually a rare or neurological disorder, clinicians may perform:
- Whole exome sequencing
- Whole genome sequencing
- A gene panel for known disease-related genes

They compare the patient’s variants to the reference genome and filter out variants present in healthy population databases. Often this leaves a few candidate variants in relevant genes. Evidence that a mutation is causative can come from:
- Another patient with the same clinical phenotype and the same/similar mutation
- De novo status (absent in healthy parents)
- Functional experiments in the wet lab

Computational tools such as **SIFT, PolyPhen and AlphaMissense** attempt to predict pathogenicity, but their accuracy is still low; the professor said they cannot be relied upon. Structural studies and molecular simulations are more informative but very time-consuming. [01:05:11]

##### 13. Monogenic diseases and inheritance patterns

[01:07:25] **Monogenic diseases** are caused by mutation in a single gene. If you have the necessary mutation (one or two copies depending on dominance), you have the disease. There are about **5,000–8,000 monogenic diseases**, and about **3% of the population** is affected. Examples: **cystic fibrosis and sickle cell anaemia**. Most disease-causing rare variants in monogenic disease are de novo because affected individuals often have reduced reproductive fitness. [01:10:29]

Inheritance patterns:
- **Autosomal dominant**: one mutated copy is enough. An affected heterozygous father with an unaffected mother has a 50% chance of transmitting the condition to each child. [01:14:50]
- **Autosomal recessive**: two mutated copies are needed. Two carrier parents (each with one mutated copy) have a 25% chance of having an affected child. [01:15:54]
- **X-linked recessive**: females need two mutated X copies; males need only one. An affected father cannot pass the X-linked mutation to his sons (they get his Y); his daughters become carriers. A carrier mother has a 50% chance that each son is affected and a 50% chance that each daughter is a carrier. [01:16:56]
- **X-linked dominant**: one mutated X copy is enough. An affected father passes the X to all daughters and none of his sons, so all daughters are affected. An affected mother has a 50% chance of passing it to any child, sons or daughters. [01:18:02]
- **Y-linked**: only males can be affected; an affected father passes it to all sons. [01:18:50]

When an inherited monogenic disease runs in a family, affected and unaffected family members can be compared to identify the variants segregating with the phenotype. [01:12:40]

##### 14. Complex diseases, heritability and GWAS

[01:08:27] **Complex diseases** are more common than monogenic diseases. They involve multiple mutations in several genes plus environmental factors; no single mutation is sufficient to cause the disease. **Heritability** is the proportion of total phenotypic variation in a trait that can be attributed to genetic effects. Monogenic disorders have high heritability; complex disorders have lower heritability because environment also matters. [01:09:28]

The main approach for complex diseases is **GWAS (genome-wide association study)**, which compares large groups of patients with healthy controls, looking for SNPs and environmental factors enriched in patients. Because of haplotypes, genotyping tag SNPs lets researchers infer other SNPs in the same blocks. Since 2008, many complex diseases have been studied. [01:19:08]

Despite these efforts:
- Each complex trait is associated with many variants of **small effect**.
- The individual effects are small and, globally, they still do not explain all of the heritability.
- **Pleiotropy** is common: one genetic variant can affect two or more unrelated traits. [01:20:11]

The professor concluded that next week’s session will be practical, using databases of healthy populations and patient databases to examine genetic reports and variants related to disease. [01:22:35]

#### What the professor stressed

- The distinction between **germline and somatic mutations**: only germline mutations are transmitted to children; somatic mutations are not. [00:38:49]
- Most cancer is caused by **somatic mutations**, but inherited germline mutations in genes such as BRCA2 confer predisposition. [00:43:14]
- Most sequence variants are **neutral**, many are rare, and only a few are pathogenic or beneficial. [00:49:35]
- **Gain-of-function mutations are usually more disease-causing** than loss-of-function mutations. [00:57:48]
- Current computational pathogenicity predictors have **low accuracy**; wet-lab validation is often needed. [01:05:11]
- Every child carries **60–80 de novo mutations**. [00:46:13]
- Common variants are organised into **haplotype blocks by linkage disequilibrium**, which makes tag-SNP genotyping possible. [00:29:04]
- Most variants fall outside coding regions, but non-coding variants can still cause disease through gene regulation, RNA stability or splicing. [00:56:44]

#### Course logistics mentioned

- The next session will be practical exercises on databases of healthy populations and of patients, studying genetic reports and how variants relate to disease. [01:22:35]

#### Self-test

1. What is the difference between a locus, an allele, a genotype and a phenotype?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>A locus is a specific position in the genome. An allele is one of the sequence versions present at that locus. The genotype is the combination of the two alleles at the locus in an individual. The phenotype is the observable trait resulting from the genotype, such as epilepsy or blindness.</details>

2. In a population of 360 AA, 480 Aa and 160 aa individuals, what are the allele frequencies and which is the minor allele?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Total individuals = 1000; total alleles = 2000. A = (2×360 + 480) / 2000 = 0.6. a = (480 + 2×160) / 2000 = 0.4. The minor allele is a.</details>

3. Why do most small-scale variants fall in non-coding regions?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Because only about 1% of the genome is protein-coding; the rest is non-coding or intergenic, so random mutations are much more likely to fall outside coding regions.</details>

4. Explain how a single-base insertion in a coding region can produce a completely different protein.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>If the insertion is not a multiple of three, it shifts the triplet reading frame. Every downstream codon is changed, often producing a premature stop codon and a truncated, usually non-functional protein.</details>

5. What is linkage disequilibrium, and why is it useful in genetic studies?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Linkage disequilibrium is the non-random association of alleles at nearby loci, inherited together more often than expected by chance. It means genomes consist of haplotype blocks of correlated SNPs, so genotyping a tag SNP can infer other SNPs in the same block without sequencing the whole genome.</details>

6. A mutation occurs in a patient’s liver cell. Explain whether it can be transmitted to the patient’s children and what diseases it might contribute to.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>It is a somatic mutation and cannot be transmitted to children. It will only affect descendants of that liver cell. If it affects genes controlling proliferation or apoptosis, it can contribute to cancer; accumulated somatic mutations also contribute to ageing and neurodegeneration.</details>

7. Why are gain-of-function mutations often more harmful than loss-of-function mutations?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Loss-of-function may still leave some residual activity, and in some cases 50% of normal product is enough. Gain-of-function produces excessive or constitutive activity, like a switch stuck “on”, which is usually more disruptive and more often disease-causing.</details>

8. What evidence helps classify a novel variant found in a patient as disease-causing?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Absence in healthy population databases, presence in another patient with the same phenotype and same/similar mutation, de novo occurrence in healthy parents, location in a disease-relevant gene, and functional wet-lab experiments showing altered protein function.</details>

9. For X-linked recessive inheritance, why are males more frequently affected than females?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Males have only one X chromosome, so a single mutated copy is sufficient to cause disease. Females have two X chromosomes and require two mutated copies to be affected; with one mutated copy they are usually carriers.</details>

10. What is the purpose of a GWAS, and what has it revealed about complex diseases?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>A GWAS compares large case and control groups to identify SNPs and environmental factors associated with complex disease. It has shown that complex traits are associated with many variants of small effect, that pleiotropy is common, and that these small effects still do not fully explain heritability.</details>

#### Related course materials

- [1.6-Human Genomic Variations](materials/07_1.6-Human%20Genomic%20Variations.pdf)

### 16. BE-Molecular Biology- Session 10 — Thu 11 Dec 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/19_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%2010-20251211_140315-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/19_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%2010-20251211_140315-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/19_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%2010-20251211_140315-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary

This session had two parts. The first half was practical: how to use **ClinVar** to look up disease-causing variants in patients and **gnomAD** to look up neutral/population variation, plus how to download both datasets from the **UCSC Table Browser**. The professor worked through examples with **GLDC** (autosomal recessive) and **GRIN1/GRIN2A** (autosomal dominant severe epilepsy/intellectual disability genes). The second half introduced the **human proteome**: the ~20,000 proteins encoded by the human genome, the 20 amino acids as building blocks, the relationship between sequence, structure and function, the four levels of protein structure, and how proteins evolve by gene duplication and domain shuffling.

Key takeaways:

- **ClinVar** is the reference database for **pathogenic/likely pathogenic variants** found in patients; **gnomAD** is the reference for **healthy population variation**.
- **Missense** variants change one amino acid; **frameshift** and **nonsense** variants usually produce shorter, non-functional proteins.
- A variant found in healthy individuals in gnomAD is strong evidence against it causing a severe **autosomal dominant** disease.
- Both ClinVar and gnomAD contain noise: old incorrect disease associations, healthy carriers of recessive diseases, and sequencing errors.
- **Protein sequence determines 3D structure, and structure determines function**; natural selection acts on protein function, not directly on DNA sequence.
- Most proteins are **multi-domain**; there are **fewer domains than proteins** because domains are reused as building blocks.

#### Key concepts

- **ClinVar**: NCBI-hosted database that aggregates genetic variants identified in patients and submitted by diagnostic/research labs, together with the associated disease or phenotype.
- **gnomAD**: database of genome and exome sequences from about one million healthy individuals, used to represent genetic variation not linked to disease.
- **Pathogenic/likely pathogenic**: variants with evidence for causing disease. In ClinVar, these are the classes of interest for monogenic disease.
- **Benign**: neutral variants not associated with disease. The professor said she does not use ClinVar to study benign variants; gnomAD is more appropriate.
- **Missense variant**: a single nucleotide change that changes one codon and therefore one amino acid.
- **Nonsense variant**: a single nucleotide change that creates a premature stop codon, producing a shorter protein.
- **Frameshift variant**: insertion/deletion of nucleotides that is not a multiple of three, shifting the reading frame and usually leading to a premature stop codon.
- **Short variant vs structural variant**: short variants affect fewer than 50 nucleotides; structural variants affect large regions (>50 nucleotides), such as duplications or deletions.
- **Autosomal recessive**: two defective copies of the gene are needed to develop disease; carriers with one copy are healthy.
- **Autosomal dominant**: one defective copy is enough to cause disease; severe dominant disorders are often de novo.
- **De novo variant**: a variant present in the child but not inherited from the parents.
- **SNP/rs code**: a single nucleotide polymorphism common enough in the population to be assigned an “rs” identifier.
- **Domain**: a compact, independently folding structural and functional unit of a protein; many proteins are made of several domains.
- **Primary, secondary, tertiary, quaternary structure**: amino acid sequence; alpha-helices/beta-sheets; 3D folded shape/domain; assembly of multiple polypeptide chains.

#### Detailed notes

##### 1. Practical work with ClinVar

[00:00:00–00:14:25]

ClinVar is hosted by NCBI. Laboratories around the world that identify a patient with a genetic condition and the causative variant upload that variant together with the clinical phenotype. The result is a database of variants related mostly to **Mendelian/monogenic diseases**.

In ClinVar you query by gene name. The professor used **GLDC** as an example:

- GLDC is associated with an **autosomal recessive** monogenic disease.
- For an autosomal recessive disease, a person needs **two defective copies** of the gene to develop the disease.
- With autosomal dominant disease, one non-working copy is enough.
- *Note: the recording briefly says “autosomal dominant” later in the GLDC discussion, but the surrounding explanation makes clear the professor is describing recessive inheritance: needing two copies.*

In the left-hand filter menu, ClinVar contains pathogenicity classifications. The professor stressed that she **does not use ClinVar to look for benign/neutral variants**, only **likely pathogenic and pathogenic**.

For GLDC, filtering to missense variants classified as pathogenic/likely pathogenic gave **142 missense variants**. For each variant, ClinVar shows:

- The transcript, e.g. the **NM** reference transcript.
- The nucleotide change, e.g. at transcript position **2,983** a **G is changed to A**.
- The resulting amino-acid change, e.g. **aspartic acid (Asp, D) to asparagine (Asn, N)**.

[00:09:19–00:10:20]

This one-nucleotide change falls in the coding sequence (CDS), so it changes one codon and therefore one amino acid in the protein sequence. The professor emphasised that even a single amino-acid change can disrupt protein structure and function.

[00:11:20–00:13:25]

She then used **GRIN2A** as a dominant severe disease example. In ClinVar, filtering to likely pathogenic/pathogenic and then to **frameshift variants** shows deletions such as `DEL`. Deleting nucleotides, unless the number is a multiple of three, changes the reading frame; all downstream codons change and the protein usually ends in a premature stop codon, producing a shorter protein.

GRIN2A-related disease causes **epilepsy and intellectual disability** and is very severe. Because affected individuals often have reduced survival or reproductive fitness, these dominant mutations are usually **de novo** rather than inherited from parents.

[00:13:25–00:14:25]

ClinVar also distinguishes:

- **Structural variants**: large regions of the genome duplicated or removed.
- **Short variants**: variants affecting only a few nucleotides, such as frameshift, missense and nonsense.
- Other classes such as **splice-site**, non-coding RNA, and changes near the gene.
- The professor said she usually focuses on **frameshift, missense and nonsense**, because the effect of splice-site variants is difficult to predict and often unclear.

##### 2. Practical work with gnomAD

[00:15:33–00:21:29]

gnomAD collects genomes and exomes from almost **one million healthy individuals** across different ethnic/population groups. It represents variation present in the human population that is **not related to disease**.

Querying the same **GLDC** gene in gnomAD shows many **missense variants** in healthy people. The professor’s point: even in disease-associated genes, there is lots of neutral sequence variation. Many amino-acid changes have no effect because the protein still folds and functions correctly.

For an autosomal recessive gene like GLDC, gnomAD can also contain **healthy carriers** of pathogenic variants: one mutated copy does not cause disease.

On a gnomAD variant page you can see:

- **Allele count**: number of individuals carrying the variant.
- **Population breakdown**, e.g. South Asian, European.
- **Homozygote count**.
- **Allele frequency**.
- **rs code / SNP identifier**, found in external resources.
- Whether the variant was seen in exome or genome sequencing.
- Chromosome and genomic position.

The professor opened a GLDC missense variant found in 159 individuals, mostly European, including one homozygote. She explained that in complex diseases, such common SNPs do not cause disease alone, but combinations of SNPs in different genes plus environmental factors can increase disease probability.

##### 3. In-class exercises with gnomAD and ClinVar

[00:30:06–00:40:09]

**Exercise 1 — gnomAD, GRIN1 p.Thr450Ala.**

The professor queried **GRIN1** in gnomAD and filtered to missense variants. The variant of interest was **threonine 450 to alanine (p.Thr450Ala)**.

Inside the variant page:

- One individual carried the variant.
- That individual was from the **Admixed American** population.
- The variant was found in a **healthy** database.

Because GRIN1 is an **autosomal dominant** gene causing a very severe disease with **epilepsy and intellectual disability**, a variant present in a healthy person is interpreted as **not disease-causing**. If a new patient with a rare condition had the same variant, the laboratory would probably look for another causative variant.

The **rs code** for the variant is available in the external resources section of the gnomAD page.

**Exercise 2 — ClinVar, GRIN1.**

For the same gene in ClinVar, filtering to **pathogenic/likely pathogenic** gave:

- **Structural variants**: 28.
- **Short variants**: 114.
- **Frameshift + nonsense variants**: 12.
- **Missense variants**: 89.

The professor used this to illustrate that in gnomAD there are hundreds of GRIN1 missense variants with no effect, while in ClinVar there are 89 missense variants that really change the structure/function and cause disease. Some protein positions are essential; mutations there cause disease. Other positions tolerate change.

##### 4. Noise, limitations and clinical use of these databases

[00:24:36–00:29:06]

Both ClinVar and gnomAD contain noise:

- **ClinVar noise**: many entries were uploaded years ago when we lacked large healthy-population databases. A variant might have been incorrectly associated with a disease because it was absent from the reference genome at the time. Later, when many healthy people were sequenced, that variant turned out to be neutral.
- **gnomAD noise**:
  - It includes healthy **carriers** of autosomal recessive pathogenic variants.
  - It includes **sequencing errors**, because sequencing machines make mistakes, roughly at some low rate per nucleotide.
- The solution is **more data**: with hundreds of thousands of individuals, incorrect associations can be cleaned.

The professor described what happens when a child with a rare genetic condition is sequenced:

1. Perform exome or genome sequencing of the child and often the parents.
2. Find variants not present in the reference genome.
3. Remove variants present in gnomAD.
4. Identify variants that are **de novo** or otherwise consistent with the inheritance pattern.
5. Look at genes known to be involved in diseases matching the clinical phenotype.
6. If needed, validate in the lab using cell or animal models.

This validation is expensive and slow, which is why **computational approaches** are important. However, the professor stressed that tools such as **SIFT** and **PolyPhen**, which try to predict whether a mutation is pathogenic, **fail completely** for many variants; accuracy is still low. Currently, associations rely mostly on sequence data and observations in other patients with the same condition.

[00:30:28–00:32:21]

When asked why sequencing data are free in public databases, the professor explained:

- Most large sequencing projects are paid with **public funds**, so data must be made available to the scientific community.
- ClinVar is also a collaborative effort: hospitals and diagnostic labs upload variants so that all laboratories can see whether other patients with the same condition share the same mutation.

##### 5. Downloading variants with the UCSC Table Browser

[00:22:32–00:23:36 and 00:43:32–00:46:38]

To download variants:

- Go to UCSC Genome Browser → **Tools → Table Browser**.
- First locate the gene coordinates, e.g. by typing `GRIN1` in the **lookup** field, which gives the genomic region of the gene.
- For healthy population variants:
  - Group: **Variation**.
  - Track: **gnomAD**.
  - Select the latest version, **gnomAD v4**.
- For disease-associated variants:
  - Group: **Phenotypes, variants and literature**.
  - Track: **ClinVar variants**.
- Then choose output fields such as genomic position, reference/alternate nucleotide, allele frequency, allele count, and variant type.
- The downloaded file can be filtered with tools like `grep` to keep only missense, frameshift, upstream, etc.

##### 6. Introduction to the human proteome

[00:47:38–00:50:25]

The **human proteome** is the collection of proteins encoded by the human genome: about **20,000 proteins**, corresponding to about **20,000 coding genes**.

Proteins are the **building blocks and workers of the cell**. Cells differ from tissue to tissue because they express different proteins. DNA transmits information; proteins carry out the functions.

When a student asked whether alternative splicing creates more proteins than coding genes, the professor answered that the additional products are **isoforms** of the same protein: slightly different versions, often expressed more in one tissue than another, but performing essentially the same function. She described them as “different flavours of the same protein.”

Different tissues express different protein sets:

- The brain expresses many proteins.
- Kidney or pancreas express fewer.
- Protein expression depends on cell type, disease state, environment and developmental stage.

##### 7. Main functions of proteins

[00:51:27–00:53:54]

Major protein functions:

- **Enzymes**: speed up reactions. Reactions could happen in the cell, but without enzymes they are so slow that practically they do not occur on biological timescales. Expressing an enzyme allows a reaction to happen when and where the cell needs it.
- **Mechanical support** for cells and tissues.
- **Transport**: carrying molecules or ions across membranes or within the cell.
- **Storage** of amino acids, ions, or molecules.
- **Signalling switches**: receiving a signal outside the cell and transmitting it inside.
- **Receptors**: detecting signals and transmitting them.
- **DNA binding**: many proteins participate in transcription and gene expression.
- **Messengers**: carrying messages between cells or between parts of the cell.
- **Motors**: moving organelles or components within the cytoplasm.
- **Defence and other roles**: antibodies, toxins, hormones.

##### 8. Sequence → structure → function

[00:53:54–00:57:00]

Protein function depends on the **three-dimensional shape** of the protein. If the shape is altered, function changes.

The 3D structure depends completely on the **amino-acid sequence**:

- Identical sequence → identical structure → identical function.
- Sequence comes from translating the CDS using the genetic code.
- DNA sequence → RNA sequence → amino-acid sequence → folded 3D structure → function.

The professor mentioned **AlphaFold** as an algorithm trying to predict structure from sequence, but noted that it still fails in many regions and for many proteins, so accuracy remains limited.

[00:56:00–00:57:00]

A student asked whether **post-translational modifications** influence structure. The professor said they occur once the protein is already folded and usually do not change global structure. They act mainly as **switches**: activating/deactivating the protein or changing whether it binds another protein. They involve small additions such as a methyl or phosphate group and only slightly change conformation.

[00:58:23]

Although mutations occur at the DNA level, **natural selection acts on protein function**. Only nucleotide changes that preserve or improve protein function tend to persist in future generations. Changes that destroy essential protein function reduce survival or reproductive fitness and are removed.

##### 9. Amino acids as building blocks

[00:59:26–01:01:29]

Proteins are made of **20 different amino acids**. Each amino acid has:

- An amino group.
- A carboxylic acid group.
- A side chain that gives it specific physicochemical properties.

The 20 amino acids vary:

- Some are **hydrophobic**.
- Some are **negatively charged**.
- Some are **positively charged**.
- Some can form **hydrogen bonds**.
- Some are small; some are big.

The final protein incorporates the collective properties of all its amino acids.

This matters for variant interpretation: a change from a small amino acid such as glycine to a large one such as arginine is a much bigger chemical change than glycine to alanine, so it is more likely to affect structure and function.

##### 10. Four levels of protein structure

[01:01:29–01:04:38 and 01:16:31]

1. **Primary structure**: the amino-acid sequence.
2. **Secondary structure**: local folding into **alpha-helices** and **beta-sheets**. This occurs because the polypeptide is surrounded by water and the amino acids arrange themselves to maximise hydrogen bonding. There are only two main ways to satisfy the hydrogen-bond potential completely: alpha-helix or beta-sheet. **Loops** are stretches of about 8–20 amino acids connecting helices/sheets.
3. **Tertiary structure**: the fully folded 3D shape of a protein, or of a **domain**.
4. **Quaternary structure**: some proteins must associate with other polypeptide chains to function. Example: **haemoglobin** requires **four copies** of the globin protein to carry out its function.

##### 11. Domains and the evolution of multi-domain proteins

[01:05:46–01:13:12]

A **domain** is a compact, independently folded unit of tertiary structure. Many proteins contain several domains.

The professor showed a large neuronal protein and explained it has **three domains**:

- One domain binds a molecule.
- Another domain binds another molecule.
- The third domain is a channel that opens.

In the example, when the domains do their jobs, the channel opens and ions pass through, transmitting a signal between neurons. The channel domain can be found reused in other proteins combined with other domains.

Evolutionary interpretation:

- Domains are thought to be **ancestral proteins**.
- Early proteins may have been single-domain.
- Genome rearrangements, duplications, deletions and **mix-and-match** events combined ancestral single-domain proteins into today’s **multi-domain proteins**.

The professor used the **Lego analogy**: domains are like a box of Lego bricks. Nature chooses different bricks to build different proteins. There are **fewer domains than proteins**, just as there are fewer Lego brick types than possible constructions.

##### 12. Evolution of new proteins

[01:19:01–01:21:09]

The professor described two main evolutionary mechanisms:

1. **Gene duplication followed by mutation accumulation**:
   - A gene/protein duplicates.
   - Over time, the two copies accumulate different mutations.
   - Eventually they become two different proteins with similar but distinct structures and functions.

2. **Mixing and matching of domains**:
   - Large genomic rearrangements combine regions encoding different domains.
   - This creates new multi-domain proteins by reusing existing functional units.

Selection acts on the **function** of the resulting protein to shape the gene sequence.

[00:42:31]

The professor also noted that of the ~20,000 coding genes in the human genome, about **6,000 genes** are associated with genetic diseases where a single nucleotide change can cause disease. For the remaining ~14,000 proteins, a single change often does nothing.

#### What the professor stressed

- **Use the right database for the right question**: ClinVar for pathogenic/likely pathogenic variants in patients; gnomAD for healthy population variation. Do not use ClinVar to look for benign variants. [00:02:40–00:05:01]
- **Both databases contain noise**, and the main way to reduce it is **more data**. [00:24:36–00:27:52]
- **Frameshift and nonsense variants usually produce shorter, non-functional proteins** because they introduce a premature stop codon. [00:05:01–00:06:07]
- **A variant found in healthy individuals in gnomAD is unlikely to cause a severe autosomal dominant disease.** [00:35:56–00:36:58]
- **Sequence determines structure, and structure determines function.** This central dogma of protein biology was repeated several times. [00:53:54–00:57:00]
- **Selection acts on protein function**, not directly on DNA sequence. [00:58:23]
- **There are fewer domains than proteins.** Domains are reusable building blocks, like Lego. [01:08:00–01:10:09]
- **Pathogenicity predictors such as SIFT and PolyPhen still fail for many variants**; computational prediction from one nucleotide change is unreliable. [00:29:06]

#### Course logistics mentioned

- Scores from the last partial exam and corrections for assignments are available on Moodle. If you want to review the exam, contact the professor to arrange a meeting. [00:00:00]
- At the time of this session, there were **two classes left**: one the following week and one after Christmas. The following class would finish the human proteome topic. [01:21:09]
- The session included the in-class exercises on **human genetic variation** available on the course page. [00:29:56–00:30:06]

#### Self-test

1. You find a missense variant in a healthy adult in gnomAD. For an autosomal dominant severe paediatric disease, what does this strongly suggest?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
It strongly suggests the variant is not disease-causing for that dominant disease: if one copy were sufficient to cause severe disease, healthy adults would not be expected to carry it. A patient with the same variant would likely need another explanation for their phenotype.
</details>

2. Why is ClinVar not the appropriate database for studying neutral or benign variation?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
ClinVar is built from patient reports of disease-associated variants, so benign/neutral variation is not its purpose and may be under-represented or poorly classified. gnomAD is specifically designed to represent healthy population variation.
</details>

3. A coding-region deletion removes 2 nucleotides. Which molecular consequence class applies and why?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
Frameshift. Deleting 2 nucleotides is not a multiple of 3, so it shifts the reading frame downstream, changes all subsequent codons, and usually creates a premature stop codon and a shorter, non-functional protein.
</details>

4. How do both nonsense and frameshift variants tend to produce shorter proteins?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
A nonsense variant directly changes a codon into a stop codon, truncating the protein. A frameshift changes the reading frame, frequently generating a new stop codon early in the coding sequence, which also truncates the protein.
</details>

5. Why can gnomAD contain healthy carriers of pathogenic variants for an autosomal recessive disease?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
In autosomal recessive disease, one mutated copy is not sufficient to cause disease. Carriers are healthy because they still have one functional copy of the gene.
</details>

6. The class found 89 pathogenic/likely pathogenic missense variants in ClinVar for GRIN1 but hundreds of missense variants in gnomAD for the same gene. Explain the difference.  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
Most amino-acid changes are neutral and occur in regions or positions that do not disrupt protein folding or function, so they appear in healthy populations. Only mutations affecting essential positions or making severe chemical changes are pathogenic and appear in ClinVar patients.
</details>

7. Describe the filtering workflow the professor outlined for finding the causative variant in a child with a rare genetic disorder.  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
Sequence the child and parents; identify variants absent from the reference genome; remove variants present in gnomAD; prioritise de novo variants or variants fitting the inheritance pattern; check whether affected genes match the clinical phenotype; if possible, validate in the lab or in animal models.
</details>

8. What does “sequence determines structure determines function” mean, and how do post-translational modifications fit into this idea?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
The amino-acid sequence dictates how the protein folds into its 3D structure, and that structure determines what the protein does. Post-translational modifications generally do not determine global structure; they occur after folding and act as small chemical switches that alter activity, localisation, or binding, with only slight conformational changes.
</details>

9. Why are there fewer domains than proteins?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
Domains are conserved structural/functional units that have been reused and combined during evolution. A limited set of domains can be arranged in many different ways, just as a limited set of Lego bricks can build many different structures, so the number of possible proteins exceeds the number of domain types.
</details>

10. How can gene duplication give rise to a new protein function?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
When a gene duplicates, one copy can continue performing the original function while the other copy accumulates mutations. Over evolutionary time, the two copies diverge in sequence, structure and function, producing a new protein.
</details>

11. Why did the professor say computational tools like SIFT and PolyPhen “fail completely” for many variants?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
Their accuracy is still low; we cannot reliably infer from a single nucleotide change alone whether it will disrupt protein structure and function enough to cause disease. Current associations rely more on population databases, patient cohorts and experimental validation.
</details>

12. What is quaternary structure, and what example was given?  
<details><summary>Answer</summary>  
Quaternary structure is the association of multiple polypeptide chains to form a functional protein. The example given was haemoglobin, which requires four protein subunits to carry oxygen.
</details>

#### Related course materials

- [1.6-Human Genomic Variations](materials/07_1.6-Human%20Genomic%20Variations.pdf)
- [2-The human Proteome](materials/08_2-The%20human%20Proteomev2.pdf)

### 17. BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251215 — Mon 15 Dec 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/20_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251215-20251215_170606-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/20_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251215-20251215_170606-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/20_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20251215-20251215_170606-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary
This session covered the digestive system, the urinary system, and the first part of the endocrine system. For the digestive system, the professor explained the organs involved, the common wall architecture of the digestive tract, its functions, the three phases of digestion, the secretions of the accessory glands, and the sites and mechanisms of absorption. For the urinary system, she explained the organs, the nephron as the functional unit, and the three processes of filtration, reabsorption and secretion. Finally, she introduced the endocrine system: the concept of a hormone, endocrine versus exocrine secretion, receptor types, and the hypophysis (pituitary) as the main link between the nervous and endocrine systems.

Key takeaways:
- Digestion combines enzymatic/chemical processing and mechanical processing (motility), not just enzymes.
- Absorption of most nutrients and water occurs mainly in the small intestine, especially via enterocytes with villi and microvilli.
- The nephron performs three processes: filtration in the renal corpuscle, reabsorption along the tubule, and secretion of additional substances into urine.
- Glomerular filtration is non-selective by size only; cells, platelets and most proteins are not filtered.
- Hormones are chemical signals released into interstitial fluid; water-soluble hormones need cell-surface receptors while lipid-soluble hormones can use intracellular receptors.
- The hypophysis integrates nervous and endocrine control: the neurohypophysis releases brain-produced hormones, while the adenohypophysis is controlled by hypothalamic releasing/inhibiting hormones via the bloodstream.

#### Key concepts
- **Mucosa** — the innermost layer of the digestive tract wall, composed of epithelium, lamina propria and muscularis mucosae.
- **Submucosa** — connective tissue layer beneath the mucosa; can contain glands and ducts from accessory glands.
- **Muscularis externa (muscular layer)** — two smooth-muscle layers of the digestive tract: an inner circular layer and an outer longitudinal layer; drive motility.
- **Digestion** — the mechanical and chemical reduction of ingested food into small molecules that can be absorbed.
- **Cephalic, gastric, intestinal phases** — the three regulatory phases of digestion, triggered before food intake, when food reaches the stomach, and when food reaches the intestine.
- **Chylomicrons** — lipid-rich structures formed inside enterocytes after lipid absorption; they travel via the lymphatic system rather than directly into blood capillaries.
- **Nephron** — the functional unit of the kidney, consisting of the renal corpuscle and a tubular region.
- **Filtration** — non-selective, size-based movement of water and small solutes from glomerular blood into Bowman's capsule.
- **Reabsorption** — return of filtered substances from the renal tubule back to the blood.
- **Secretion** — additional movement of substances from the blood into the renal tubule for elimination in urine.
- **Hormone** — a chemical product released into the interstitial space that acts on target cells possessing the appropriate receptor.
- **Endocrine vs exocrine secretion** — endocrine secretion releases products into interstitial fluid without ducts; exocrine secretion releases products to the exterior (e.g., into the digestive tract) via ducts.
- **Neurohypophysis and adenohypophysis** — the two parts of the hypophysis: neurohypophysis releases hormones produced by hypothalamic neurons; adenohypophysis contains endocrine cells stimulated by hypothalamic releasing/inhibiting hormones.

#### Detailed notes

##### Digestive system: overview and organisation [00:00:00]
The digestive system includes the structures involved in food uptake and processing: the mouth and surrounding organs, the pharynx, the digestive tract (esophagus, stomach, small intestine, large intestine), and accessory glands (salivary glands, liver, pancreas) [00:00:00–00:01:40].

All parts of the digestive tract — esophagus, stomach, small and large intestine — share a common anatomical organisation [00:01:40–00:02:46]:
- **Epithelium** at the border with the lumen; its exact type varies along the tract.
- **Lamina propria** beneath the epithelium (extracellular matrix with immune and support cells).
- **Muscularis mucosae**, a thin layer of smooth muscle.
- These three together form the **mucosa**.
- Below the mucosa: the **submucosa**, then the **muscularis**, organised into an inner circular layer and an outer longitudinal layer.
- Some regions have glands in the submucosa, and ducts carrying secretions from the accessory glands (liver, pancreas) open into the tract.

##### Functions of the digestive system [00:02:46–00:04:33]
1. **Ingestion** — taking in food.
2. **Secretion** — release of liquids that mix with the ingested food.
3. **Motility** — muscular movement that mixes and moves contents along the tract.
4. **Digestion** — mechanical and chemical reduction of large particles/macromolecules into small soluble molecules.
5. **Absorption** — passage of small molecules into the blood.
6. **Elimination / defecation** — expulsion of non-absorbed contents.

##### The three phases of digestion [00:05:45–00:06:50]
- **Cephalic phase** — occurs before food is even ingested; prepares mouth and stomach (e.g., secretion of saliva and gastric juice).
- **Gastric phase** — starts when food reaches the stomach; increased gastric motility and gastric juice secretion.
- **Intestinal phase** — starts when food reaches the intestine.

All phases use both **nervous** and **endocrine** regulation.

##### Secretions into the digestive tract [00:08:44–00:11:57]
- **Saliva** (salivary glands): water, salts, **amylase** (degrades carbohydrates) and **lipases** (degrade lipids).
- **Stomach**: **lipases**, **pepsin** (a protease that cuts proteins into smaller pieces), and **hydrochloric acid (HCl)**. HCl denatures/unfolds proteins, making them more accessible to pepsin.
- **Pancreas**: amylases, lipases, trypsin (a protease), nucleases (degrade nucleic acids), and **bicarbonate** to neutralise the acidic content coming from the stomach.
- **Liver (bile)**: delivers cholesterol and **bile salts** to the intestine. Bile salts emulsify dietary lipids, aiding their digestion. Bile also carries **bilirubin**, a breakdown product of the heme group of erythrocytes; bilirubin is responsible for the colour of faeces and is the route by which heme waste is eliminated.
- **Small intestine**: secretes enzymes for the final enzymatic processing of macromolecules.
- **Mucus** is produced throughout and protects/lubricates the epithelium.

*Note (mine): the professor's summary table/scheme showed that about 9 litres of liquid per day enter the digestive tract (diet plus saliva, gastric juice, bile, pancreatic juice), most of which is absorbed in the small intestine; only a small fraction is lost in faeces [00:11:57].*

##### Absorption sites [00:13:39–00:16:13]
- **Stomach** [00:13:39]: only small amounts of water and a few molecules are absorbed, including alcohol and some drugs. The stomach is not a major absorption site.
- **Small intestine** [00:14:47]: the main site of absorption for water and nutrients. The mucosa is folded into **villi**, and the enterocytes (absorptive epithelial cells) have further membrane folds (**microvilli**) on their lumenal surface, greatly increasing the absorptive surface.
- **Large intestine** [00:16:13]: fewer enterocytes and many more mucus-producing cells; some water and some substances are absorbed, but absorption is much less important here.

##### Transport mechanisms in enterocytes [00:17:25–00:22:01]
- **Glucose and fructose** are taken up largely by **secondary active transport**. The cell uses ATP to generate an ion gradient (e.g., sodium). Sodium then moves into the cell down its gradient, and glucose is **co-transported** with sodium. It is called secondary because ATP is consumed in a different part of the cell to create the gradient, not directly to move the glucose [00:17:25].
- **Amino acids** are likewise taken up by sodium co-transport [00:18:56].
- **Lipids**: small lipids can cross directly through the membrane because of their size and lipid affinity. Larger lipids arrive at the enterocyte surface in **micelles** (emulsified structures), cross the membrane, and are then re-assembled inside the cell into **chylomicrons** — structures that allow hydrophobic lipids to be handled in an aqueous environment [00:18:56–00:20:25].
- Chylomicrons are **not** released directly into blood capillaries; they enter the interstitial space and are carried by the **lymphatic system**, later reaching the bloodstream [00:20:25–00:22:01].

##### Fate of absorbed products [00:22:01]
Absorbed products entering the bloodstream do not go directly to the heart; they first reach the **liver**, which has a major role in metabolic control.

The professor stressed the general view: digestion means both **enzymatic processing of molecules** and **motility (mechanical processing)** — mastication in the mouth, strong mixing/fragmentation in the stomach, and movement along the whole tract. **Absorption occurs mainly in the small intestine** [00:23:32].

##### Urinary system: organs and functions [00:23:32–00:25:06]
The urinary system consists of the **kidneys**, **ureters**, **bladder** and **urethra**. The kidneys produce urine, which is stored in the bladder and eliminated via the urethra.

Functions:
- Elimination of waste residues in urine.
- Regulation of blood composition.
- Regulation of pH (acidity of the internal medium).
- Regulation of fluid volume and arterial pressure (two interrelated characteristics).
- Regulation of osmolarity.
- Endocrine function: the kidneys produce hormones, including the one that regulates erythrocyte production (erythropoietin) [00:25:06].

The kidneys receive a very large blood flow: more than 1 litre of blood per minute arrives, and the final output is about **2 litres of urine per day** [00:25:06].

##### The nephron [00:26:21–00:29:49]
The functional unit of the kidney is the **nephron**, composed of:
- **Renal corpuscle**: located in the kidney cortex (outermost part); the site where blood is filtered. It consists of the **glomerulus** (a network of capillaries) and the **Bowman's capsule** (the structure around the glomerulus that collects the filtered liquid).
- **Tubular region**, with segments in both cortex and medulla:
  - **Proximal convoluted tubule** (initial segment after the renal corpuscle).
  - **Loop of Henle** (middle segment).
  - **Distal convoluted tubule**, which drains into the **collecting duct**.
- One collecting duct receives liquid from more than one nephron.

##### The three processes of the nephron [00:29:49–00:33:23]
1. **Filtration** — occurs in the renal corpuscle. Blood pressure drives the movement of water, ions and small molecules through the filtration membrane into Bowman's capsule.
2. **Reabsorption** — most of the filtered liquid is taken back. Most of the filtered water is reabsorbed; all glucose and amino acids are reabsorbed. Reabsorption is essential because filtration alone would produce far more than 2 L/day.
3. **Secretion** — additional components can be secreted into the urine. Examples: additional ions when they need to be eliminated, urea, and protons (important for pH control).

##### Filtration in detail [00:30:53–00:35:19]
The filtration membrane lies between blood and the liquid in the renal corpuscle. It is formed by:
- The **endothelial cells** of the glomerular capillaries.
- A specialised cell type, the **podocytes**.
- The extracellular matrix / basal lamina between these two cell layers.

This membrane is **non-selective, selecting only by size**. Water, ions and small molecules (glucose, amino acids, bilirubin, urea) pass. Plasma proteins, blood cells, platelets and erythrocytes do **not** pass. The filtered liquid is held in the capsular space and directed into the renal tubule.

##### Reabsorption along the tubule [00:35:19–00:38:01]
Reabsorption occurs along all segments of the tubule, but mostly in the **proximal convoluted tubule** (cortex). There:
- Most of the filtered water is reabsorbed.
- **All glucose and amino acids** are reabsorbed.
- Almost all filtered **bicarbonate** is reabsorbed.

In the **loop of Henle**, smaller percentages of water and ions are reabsorbed. In the **distal tubule**, fine regulation occurs, including fine control of water reabsorption. The **antidiuretic hormone (ADH)** acts here to determine how much water is retained or excreted.

Overall numbers [00:38:01]: about **180 litres of water filtered per day**, with **99% of the filtered water reabsorbed**. Glucose is completely reabsorbed in a healthy individual; proteins are almost not filtered at all.

##### Secretion in detail [00:39:17–00:40:25]
- **Proximal tubule**: urea, ammonium, creatinine residues, and protons can be secreted. Proton secretion here helps in bicarbonate reabsorption.
- **Distal tubule and collecting duct**: protons are also secreted. Here the purpose is **blood pH regulation** — secreting protons into urine reduces protons in the bloodstream, helping to control body pH.

##### Endocrine system: organisation [00:41:35–00:42:47]
The endocrine and nervous systems are the two main coordination systems of the body (with some participation of the immunological system). Endocrine components are organised into two categories:

1. **Endocrine glands** — organs whose unique function is to produce hormones: hypophysis, thyroid gland, parathyroid glands, suprarenal (adrenal) glands, pineal gland.
2. **Other endocrine organs** — organs that produce hormones in addition to other functions: e.g., testicles and ovaries (reproductive function plus hormone production), and most organs of the body.

##### Hormone concept and receptors [00:44:24–00:48:34]
Cells communicate chemically. A hormone is different from gap-junction communication or synaptic communication: hormones are chemical products released into the **interstitial space**, from which they can diffuse and be detected by any cell with the appropriate receptor — a more general, non-specialised cell-to-cell signal.

Receptor location depends on the chemical nature of the hormone:
- **Water-soluble (hydrophilic) hormones** cannot cross the plasma membrane, so the target cell must have a **cell-surface receptor**.
- **Lipid-soluble (lipophilic) hormones** can cross the membrane, so the receptor can be **intracellular**.

Many hormones reach the bloodstream and act on tissues far from their production site; these are called **circulating hormones**. The endocrine system works especially with circulating hormones.

##### Endocrine vs exocrine secretion [00:48:34–00:49:50]
- **Endocrine secretion**: the chemical compound is secreted into the interstitial fluid; no duct is needed.
- **Exocrine secretion**: the product is released to the exterior of the body (e.g., into the digestive tract, which is continuous with the external environment); exocrine glands need ducts. Endocrine glands never have ducts.

##### Hormones from non-specialised organs [00:50:06]
Examples:
- Kidney: **erythropoietin**
- Stomach: **gastrin**, which stimulates gastric juice production
- Heart: a hormone that controls blood pressure and blood volume
- Many other organs also produce hormones; the list is very long.

##### The hypophysis (pituitary) [00:51:20–00:55:57]
The hypophysis is an endocrine gland located beneath the brain and interconnected with it; it is the main point of interaction between the endocrine and nervous systems.

- **Neurohypophysis** [00:51:20–00:52:41]: hormones released here are produced by neurons whose cell bodies are in the brain. Their axons end close to the capillaries, releasing hormones directly into interstitial fluid (no synapse). The two hormones released are **oxytocin** and **antidiuretic hormone (ADH)**.
- **Adenohypophysis** [00:52:41–00:53:42]: contains non-neural endocrine cells that secrete hormones: **growth hormone (GH)**, **thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH)**, **adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH)**, and hormones related to the reproductive system. Different cell types produce different hormones.

**Control of the adenohypophysis** [00:53:42–00:55:57]: these cells secrete their hormones only when stimulated by other hormones produced in the **hypothalamus** (releasing or inhibiting hormones). The hypothalamic hormones do not reach the adenohypophysis by simple diffusion; they enter the bloodstream, travel the short distance through the blood, and are released at the adenohypophysis. So even over a very short distance, this is a circulating-hormone system.

This produces a hierarchical axis: **hypothalamus → hypophysis → third organ (e.g., thyroid, adrenal, gonads)**, controlling functions such as reproduction, metabolism and growth. The professor asked students to review the remaining endocrine glands (thyroid, parathyroid, adrenal, pancreas) and the main hormones of ovaries and testicles from the slides [00:57:25].

#### What the professor stressed
- Digestive system: keep the **general view** — digestion is **enzymatic processing plus motility (mechanical processing)**; absorption is **mainly in the small intestine** [00:23:32].
- Urinary system: the core is the **three processes of the nephron — filtration, reabsorption, secretion** — and understanding where each occurs and what is (and is not) filtered/reabsorbed/secreted [00:29:49–00:33:23].
- Filtration is **non-selective and size-based**; proteins, cells, platelets and erythrocytes are not filtered [00:30:53].
- The **hypophysis is the main link between the nervous and hormonal systems**, and the hypothalamus–hypophysis–target organ axis is relevant for many body functions [00:55:57].
- She explicitly asked students to study from the slides: thyroid, parathyroid, adrenal and pancreas hormones, plus the main hormones of ovaries and testicles, because the session ran out of time [00:57:25].

#### Course logistics mentioned
- The first session after the Christmas holidays will **finish the endocrine system** and **start the nervous system** [00:57:25].
- There is only **one final session** left, and it will focus mainly on the nervous system.
- The professor asked students to **work a little at home** because there is not enough time to cover everything in class [00:57:25].

#### Self-test
1. What are the six functions of the digestive system listed in the lecture?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Ingestion, secretion, motility, digestion, absorption, and elimination (defecation).</details>

2. A cross-section of the small intestine shows epithelium, lamina propria, muscularis mucosae, submucosa, and muscularis. Which three belong to the mucosa, and what is the orientation of the smooth muscle in the muscularis?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The mucosa consists of the epithelium, lamina propria and muscularis mucosae. The muscularis has an inner circular layer and an outer longitudinal layer of smooth muscle.</details>

3. The stomach secretes pepsin and HCl. Why is HCl important for protein digestion even though pepsin is the protease?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
HCl denatures/unfolds proteins, making them less folded and therefore more accessible to pepsin, which cuts them into smaller pieces.</details>

4. Glucose enters enterocytes by sodium co-transport. Explain why this is classified as secondary active transport rather than primary active transport.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
ATP is used by the cell to create a sodium ion gradient (usually at another part of the cell). Glucose then moves into the cell together with sodium down that gradient. The energy for glucose uptake comes indirectly from the ion gradient, not from ATP directly moving glucose, so it is secondary active transport.</details>

5. Why do chylomicrons not enter blood capillaries directly after lipid absorption in the small intestine?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Chylomicrons are large lipid-rich structures. They are released into the interstitial fluid and travel through the lymphatic system, which later delivers them to the bloodstream; they do not pass directly into the blood capillaries.</details>

6. List the three processes performed by the nephron and state where each one occurs.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Filtration occurs in the renal corpuscle (glomerulus + Bowman's capsule). Reabsorption occurs along the renal tubule, mainly in the proximal convoluted tubule. Secretion occurs in specific tubular segments, notably the proximal tubule and the distal tubule/collecting duct.</details>

7. The glomerular filtrate contains water, ions, glucose, amino acids, bilirubin and urea, but not plasma proteins, erythrocytes, leukocytes or platelets. Why?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The filtration membrane is non-selective and selects only by size. It is formed by the glomerular capillary endothelial cells, podocytes, and the extracellular matrix between them. Only water and small molecules can cross; larger elements such as proteins and blood cells are retained in the blood.</details>

8. About 180 L of water are filtered per day but only about 2 L of urine are produced. Explain what accounts for this difference.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Most filtered water (about 99%) is reabsorbed along the renal tubule, mainly in the proximal convoluted tubule, with fine-tuning in the distal tubule under the influence of antidiuretic hormone. Therefore only a small fraction is excreted as urine.</details>

9. A healthy person excretes no glucose in urine. Why?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
All filtered glucose is completely reabsorbed in the renal tubule, mainly in the proximal convoluted tubule, so none remains to be excreted in a healthy individual.</details>

10. Distinguish endocrine from exocrine secretion.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Endocrine secretion releases products (hormones) into the interstitial fluid, without any duct. Exocrine secretion releases products to the exterior of the body, e.g., into the digestive tract, and requires ducts.</details>

11. Cortisol is a lipid-soluble hormone; insulin is a water-soluble hormone. Where would you expect the receptor for each to be located, and why?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Cortisol is lipid-soluble, so it can cross the plasma membrane and its receptor can be intracellular. Insulin is water-soluble and cannot cross the membrane, so its receptor must be on the cell surface.</details>

12. The adenohypophysis is only a few millimetres from the hypothalamus, yet it is controlled by circulating hormones. Explain this.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Hypothalamic neurons produce releasing and inhibiting hormones that enter the bloodstream and travel the short distance to the adenohypophysis, where they leave the bloodstream and stimulate or inhibit the endocrine cells. Even over this very short distance, the signal uses the circulating-hormone pathway, not direct diffusion or synapses.</details>
