### 18. BE-Molecular Biology- Session 11 — Thu 18 Dec 2025

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/21_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%2011-20251218_140227-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/21_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%2011-20251218_140227-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/21_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%2011-20251218_140227-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary

This session finished the human proteome block. It consolidated the relationship between protein sequence, structure, and function, then explained how homologous proteins arise through gene duplication and speciation, distinguishing orthologues from paralogues. The professor demonstrated the sequence–structure correlation with sequence alignments and introduced the thresholds used to infer homology (70% nucleotide identity for genes; >25% amino acid identity for proteins), while warning that real homologues can exist below these thresholds if structural evidence is available. The class then moved to PyMOL visualisation of representative proteins (hemoglobin, antibodies, histones, sirtuins, lac repressor, a membrane channel) to show how structure determines function. Finally, the professor introduced UniProt as the reference protein database and walked through searching with advanced queries, reading a UniProt entry, and answering biological questions such as how many human proteins are reviewed, involved in synapse, or have experimental 3D structures.

Key takeaways:

- A protein’s sequence determines its structure, and structure determines function; therefore, sequence similarity lets us infer structure and function computationally.
- Homologous proteins arise by gene duplication (paralogues) or speciation (orthologues); sequence identity thresholds are used to propose homology.
- Conserved positions in multiple sequence alignments highlight residues that were not tolerated to mutate because they are essential for structure/function.
- UniProt Swiss-Prot is the curated, low-redundancy reference protein database; use gene name + organism for efficient searching.
- Experimental structures (X-ray, electron microscopy) should be preferred over AlphaFold computational models when available.
- The reviewed human proteome contains 20,420 proteins, but experimental 3D structures exist for only 8,605 of them.

#### Key concepts

- **Primary structure**: the linear amino acid sequence of a protein [00:00:40].
- **Secondary structure**: local interactions of residues to satisfy hydrogen-bond potential, mainly alpha helices and beta sheets [00:00:40].
- **Tertiary structure / domain**: a folded, packed region of a protein that can perform a specific function [00:00:40, 00:29:51].
- **Quaternary structure**: a protein made of several polypeptide chains/subunits [00:23:59].
- **Homologous proteins/genes**: two genes or proteins that descend from the same ancestral gene/protein [00:05:38].
- **Orthologous proteins/genes**: homologues created by a speciation event; they belong to different organisms [00:07:02].
- **Paralogous proteins/genes**: homologues created by a gene duplication event; they belong to the same genome [00:07:02].
- **Sequence identity**: the percentage of nucleotides or amino acids that are identical between two aligned sequences [00:09:12].
- **Gene Ontology (GO)**: a controlled vocabulary of keywords describing molecular function and biological process of a protein [00:45:36].
- **Swiss-Prot / reviewed proteins**: UniProt entries manually curated from the literature; low redundancy [00:43:24].
- **TrEMBL / unreviewed proteins**: computationally predicted proteins that are not yet curated; can include redundancy [00:43:24].
- **AlphaFold**: a computational method for predicting protein structure; should be used with caution when no experimental structure exists [00:53:15].

#### Detailed notes

##### Protein structure recap: sequence → structure → function

The professor reviewed the hierarchy of protein structure [00:00:40]:

- Primary structure: the sequence of amino acids, the building blocks of proteins.
- Secondary structure: residues interacting locally to satisfy hydrogen bond potential.
- Tertiary structure: the folded domain that forms a functional part of a protein.
- Quaternary structure: a protein composed of several folded subunits.

A protein can contain several domains, and a functional protein may need to interact physically with other proteins. The professor referred to this network of interactions as the **interactome**: some interactions are permanent, while others are transient and switch processes on or off [00:00:40].

##### Protein evolution and domain mixing

The professor presented evolution using a Lego analogy [00:03:31]:

- An ancestral protein undergoes **gene duplication**, producing two identical proteins.
- Over evolution, each copy accumulates mutations, so they remain similar but not identical.
- They will generally have similar sequence, similar structure, and similar function.
- Domains can be combined and recombined, like Lego blocks, generating diverse protein structures and functions.

##### Homology, orthology, and paralogy

Homologous proteins/genes are those that descend from the same ancestral protein/gene [00:05:38]. Two biological events generate homologues [00:07:02]:

- **Gene duplication**: creates paralogues, which are in the same genome.
- **Speciation**: creates orthologues, which are in different organisms.

Examples given [00:08:08]:

- Horse hemoglobin alpha and human hemoglobin alpha are **orthologues**.
- Human hemoglobin alpha and human hemoglobin beta are **paralogues**.

The professor stressed that we can hypothesize homology because very similar sequences are extremely unlikely to arise by chance de novo [00:09:12, 00:19:13].

##### Sequence identity thresholds and the limits of the rule

The standard guideline proposed in class [00:09:12]:

- For **genes/RNA**: at least 70% nucleotide identity to propose homology.
- For **proteins**: more than 25% amino acid identity to propose homology.

However, the professor answered a question about low identity explicitly [00:15:15, 00:18:12]:

- If identity is below 25%, you **cannot be sure** two proteins are homologous based purely on sequence.
- There are many real cases with only 13–15% sequence identity whose structures are almost identical and whose functions are similar.
- Below 25%, you need additional evidence, especially structural superposition, to propose homology.
- When doing standard database searches, 25% is the practical minimum accepted to suggest homology.

##### Sequence identity correlates with structural similarity

The professor showed pairwise alignments of 9-PAP with 2-ACT and other proteins [00:10:29]:

- High sequence identity → superimposed structures almost identical.
- Lower sequence identity → structures begin to show differences.
- Very low sequence identity (<15%) → some regions differ, but most of the fold can still be quite similar.

The conclusion [00:14:11, 00:19:13]:

- Sequence is completely correlated with structure.
- Therefore, if two proteins have similar sequences, they likely have similar structures.
- Since structure determines function, similar sequences likely perform similar functions.
- This is the basis of computational structure/function prediction without experimental work.

##### Multiple sequence alignments and conserved positions

A multiple sequence alignment of a homologous protein family shows positions kept exactly the same during evolution [00:14:11]. The professor’s interpretation:

- All positions have mutated at some point over thousands of years.
- But mutations in **super-conserved positions** were not tolerated: individuals carrying them did not survive, so those mutations never became fixed.
- Conserved positions are therefore essential for maintaining protein structure and function.
- This allows scientists to identify which residues are essential and which regions can be modified while preserving function.

##### PyMOL tour: structure and function examples

The professor used PyMOL to visualise representative proteins [00:22:50].

###### Hemoglobin: a transporter

- Hemoglobin is composed of **four proteins/subunits**, each with one domain [00:23:59].
- It is mainly alpha-helical, with loops connecting helices [00:25:55].
- Each subunit contains a **heme group** in its core, which binds oxygen.
- Oxygen binding/release is modulated by changes in affinity, allowing oxygen transport.
- The red dots in the structure are water molecules; the chemical groups are not proteins but help the protein perform its function [00:23:59, 00:27:08].

###### Antibody: multi-domain and multi-chain protein

- An antibody is made of several polypeptide chains [00:28:14].
- The blue chain alone contains **four domains**, which appear as separate packed structures [00:29:51].
- The domains are similar, suggesting they arose by gene duplication.
- Each domain can perform a specific function, such as binding the partner chain or binding other proteins.
- Overall antibody function emerges from combining domain functions across chains.

###### Histones: small single-domain DNA-packaging proteins

- Histones are short proteins that wrap DNA [00:31:38].
- Each histone is a short sequence folded into a single domain.
- Histones are similar in sequence, structure, and function, but small differences are important for the overall nucleosome fold.

###### Sirtuin: an enzyme with a catalytic cavity

- Sirtuins are enzymes that speed up reactions by modifying other proteins through post-translational modifications [00:33:56].
- The protein contains an **NAD** chemical group used to perform a chemical reaction on another protein.
- The surface representation shows a specific shape with a hole; the substrate residue fits into this cavity.
- Without this shape and catalytic activity, the reaction would be too slow.

###### Lac repressor: a gene regulator

- The lac repressor binds DNA to stop transcription of a gene [00:36:00].
- The structure shows DNA at the bottom and the protein composed of several copies/subunits.
- The green and blue chains each have two domains: a small DNA-binding domain and a large protein-interaction domain.
- The small domains bind DNA; the large domains interact between proteins.

###### Membrane channel: beta-sheet structure

- A channel protein is built from **beta sheets**, not alpha helices [00:37:58].
- The beta-sheet fold creates channels that span the membrane and allow passage of molecules.
- This illustrates that structure is completely related to function.

The professor’s closing point: whenever you see a protein name, imagine a three-dimensional fold that determines its function; many proteins need other proteins, and most are composed of more than one domain [00:39:15].

##### UniProt: the main protein database

UniProt is the reference database for proteins [00:40:17]. The professor contrasted it with NCBI Reference Sequence:

- NCBI Reference Sequence is useful to relate a protein/isoform to its transcript.
- UniProt is where you find curated information on protein function, structure, variants, and interactions.

UniProt has several sections; the class focused on the **Proteins** database [00:41:18].

##### Searching UniProt efficiently

The professor recommended using the **advanced search** with two fields [00:42:21]:

- Gene name (e.g., GRIN1, which the transcript misheard as “green one”)
- Organism (e.g., Homo sapiens)

This returns a list including computationally predicted, unreviewed proteins. To reduce redundancy and get curated entries, filter by **Reviewed (Swiss-Prot)** [00:43:24]. The reviewed GRIN1 entry is **glutamate receptor ionotropic NMDA1**, length **938 amino acids** [00:43:24].

##### Anatomy of a UniProt entry

Using GRIN1 as the example, the professor walked through the entry sections [00:44:52]:

- **Function**: summary of what the protein does, curated from published papers.
- **Important positions**: e.g., glycine-binding residues.
- **Gene Ontology (GO) keywords**:
  - **Molecular function**: “zoom in” — e.g., glutamate-binding, calcium-ion binding.
  - **Biological process**: “zoom out” — e.g., brain development, calcium ion homeostasis, synapse [00:46:12, 00:47:27].
- **Subcellular location**: e.g., GRIN1 is located in the membrane, with regions inside and outside the cell.
- **Disease and variants**: lists disease-causing mutations with supporting publications. This is more curated than ClinVar but shorter. Also includes non-disease variants.
- **Links to other databases**: e.g., DrugBank for molecules that can bind the protein.
- **Post-translational modifications**: chemical modifications on specific residues.
- **Expression**: tissues expressing the gene.
- **Interactions**: list of proteins interacting with the protein.
- **Structure**: experimental structures from X-ray diffraction or electron microscopy; choose structures covering the whole protein. AlphaFold is a computational model.
- **Sequence/isoforms**: different isoforms from alternative transcripts; all share the same UniProt entry because they are very similar.
- **Download**: download the full text or the **canonical FASTA sequence** [00:55:36].

##### AlphaFold warning

AlphaFold is described as a computational prediction method [00:53:15]. The professor stressed:

- AlphaFold can fail when a protein lacks similar proteins with experimentally determined structures.
- Always prefer experimental structures (X-ray, electron microscopy) when available.
- Use AlphaFold only when no experimental structure exists.

##### UniProt queries and counts

The professor demonstrated advanced searches and gave these results [00:56:37, 01:00:41, 01:18:19]:

- Searching Homo sapiens without filters gives around **200,000 entries**, but this includes redundancy and unreviewed proteins.
- Reviewed human proteins: **20,420** — the current human proteome count in UniProt Swiss-Prot.
- Reviewed human proteins involved in **type 1 diabetes**: **4**.
- Reviewed human proteins with an experimental 3D structure: **8,605**.
- Why so few structures? Experimental structure determination is expensive and can take years per protein.
- Reviewed human proteins of length 1–30 amino acids: **50**.
- Reviewed human proteins associated with **synapse** via Gene Ontology: **1,682**.
- Reviewed human proteins involved in **activation of the immune response**: **403**.
- HIV-2 proteome (one selected isolate): **9 proteins**.

##### Exercise corrections

The professor corrected part of the UniProt exercises [01:09:44]:

- **FGFR3 (Homo sapiens)**:
  - Found via gene name FGFR3 and organism Homo sapiens.
  - Reviewed entry length: **806 amino acids**.
  - Contains **four domains**, e.g., Ig-like C2-type domains at positions 24–126, 151–244, 253–355.
  - Experimental structures cover all domains.
  - Involved in diseases: **achondroplasia**, **Crouzon syndrome**, and other dysplasias; disease-causing mutations are listed in the variant section.
- **EGFR exercise**: locate the variant (e.g., a mutation such as position 428), find its **rs code** and the corresponding **PDB code** from the structure section [01:16:25].
- **Exercise 3**: download sequences of all reviewed human proteins involved in activation of the immune response → 403 proteins.
- **Exercise 4**: download the HIV-2 proteome → 9 proteins for the selected isolate.

##### Recommended follow-up

The professor recommended [01:20:33]:

- Do the PyMOL protein structure/function exercises yourself.
- Do the UniProt exercises to learn how to extract biological answers.
- Use solved versions to check answers.

#### What the professor stressed

- **Sequence, structure, and function are tightly linked**: if two proteins are similar in sequence, they likely share structure and function; this underpins computational prediction [00:19:13, 00:20:36].
- **Homology thresholds**: 70% nucleotide identity for genes and >25% amino acid identity for proteins [00:09:12]. Below 25%, do not claim homology from sequence alone; structural evidence is needed [00:18:12].
- **Conserved positions in multiple alignments** mark residues essential for survival and function [00:14:11].
- **Use UniProt Swiss-Prot reviewed entries** to avoid redundancy and uncurated predictions [00:43:24].
- **Prefer experimental protein structures over AlphaFold** whenever possible [00:53:15].
- **Remember the human proteome counts**: 20,420 reviewed proteins but only 8,605 with experimental structures [00:56:37, 00:59:37].

#### Course logistics mentioned

- After Christmas, the next session will cover **regulation of gene expression**; this will be the last session before the block ends [01:20:33].
- The exam will cover **regulation of gene expression and the human proteome** [01:20:33].
- There is a **Task 3** covering Unit 2 and Unit 3; it is available/posted and due in January, the day before the exam [01:20:33].
- A student noted the Task 3 submission entry said it would open on 11 January and the instructions were not visible; the professor said she would post the link with all task information after class [01:21:34].

#### Self-test

1. Distinguish orthologous and paralogous proteins, using hemoglobin examples.

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Orthologous proteins arise from a speciation event and belong to different organisms, e.g., horse hemoglobin alpha and human hemoglobin alpha. Paralogous proteins arise from a gene duplication event within the same genome, e.g., human hemoglobin alpha and human hemoglobin beta. Both are homologous because they descend from the same ancestral protein.
</details>

2. Why can an ancestral protein give rise to two similar but non-identical proteins?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
After gene duplication, the two identical copies independently accumulate mutations during evolution. Most mutations are neutral or tolerated, so after many generations the copies remain similar in sequence and structure but are no longer identical.
</details>

3. What sequence identity thresholds did the professor give for hypothesizing homology, and what is the limitation below the protein threshold?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
For genes: about 70% nucleotide identity. For proteins: more than 25% amino acid identity. Below 25% amino acid identity, you cannot confidently claim homology from sequence alone. Two proteins may still be homologous at 13–15% identity, but that requires additional evidence, such as structural superposition showing a very similar fold.
</details>

4. Explain the chain of reasoning: similar sequence → similar structure → similar function.

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Because sequence identity correlates strongly with structural similarity, two proteins with similar sequences are expected to fold into similar three-dimensional structures. Since a protein’s function depends on its shape, similar structures imply similar functions. This allows computational prediction of structure and function from sequence without doing experiments.
</details>

5. In a multiple sequence alignment of a homologous protein family, why are some positions perfectly conserved?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
These positions are not allowed to mutate because they are essential for maintaining protein structure or function. Mutations in these super-conserved positions were eliminated by natural selection because individuals carrying them did not survive or reproduce.
</details>

6. What is the biological role of the heme group in hemoglobin, according to the PyMOL example?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The heme group is a non-protein chemical group located in the core of each hemoglobin subunit. It binds oxygen, allowing hemoglobin to transport oxygen. Changes in the protein’s affinity for oxygen determine whether oxygen is bound or released.
</details>

7. In UniProt, why should you filter for reviewed (Swiss-Prot) entries when counting proteins in the human proteome?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Unfiltered UniProt searches include unreviewed (TrEMBL) entries and many redundant or computationally predicted proteins, which can inflate the count. The reviewed Swiss-Prot set is manually curated and low-redundancy; it gives the current high-confidence human proteome of 20,420 proteins.
</details>

8. Why does the professor recommend using experimental structures instead of AlphaFold models?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
AlphaFold is a computational prediction and can fail when the target protein lacks similar proteins with experimentally determined structures. Experimental structures from X-ray crystallography or electron microscopy directly determine atom positions, so they are more reliable. AlphaFold should be used mainly when no experimental structure exists.
</details>

9. How would you use UniProt advanced search to count reviewed human proteins involved in activation of the immune response, and what is the result?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Use advanced search: field “Organism” = Homo sapiens, add Gene Ontology term “activation of immune response”, then filter to reviewed (Swiss-Prot). The result is 403 reviewed human proteins.
</details>

10. How many reviewed human proteins have an experimental 3D structure, and why is this number much lower than the total?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
8,605 of 20,420 reviewed human proteins have an experimental 3D structure. The gap exists because determining a structure experimentally is expensive and can take years for a single protein.
</details>

11. Describe the difference between Gene Ontology “molecular function” and “biological process” as explained in class.

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Molecular function is a “zoom in”: what the protein does at the molecular level, e.g., binding glutamate or calcium ions. Biological process is a “zoom out”: the larger process in which the protein participates, e.g., brain development or synapse function.
</details>

12. What information can you obtain from the “Disease and variants” section of a UniProt entry?

<details><summary>Answer</summary>
It lists disease-causing variants linked to specific diseases, with supporting publications, and may also list variants not associated with disease. It is a curated but shorter alternative to ClinVar for associating mutations with disease.
</details>

#### Related course materials

- [08_2-The human Proteomev2.pdf](materials/08_2-The%20human%20Proteomev2.pdf)
- [Exercises on Protein Structure and Function](https://campusvirtual.urv.cat/mod/url/view.php?id=4185476)
- [Exercises on Uniprot](https://campusvirtual.urv.cat/mod/url/view.php?id=4185478)
- [Exercises on Uniprot (solved)](https://campusvirtual.urv.cat/mod/url/view.php?id=4185479)

### 19. BE-Molecular Biology- Session 12 — Thu 08 Jan 2026

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/22_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%2012-20260108_140204-Meeting%20Recording.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/22_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%2012-20260108_140204-Meeting%20Recording.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/22_BE-Molecular%20Biology-%20Session%2012-20260108_140204-Meeting%20Recording.md)*

#### Summary

This final session covered the **regulation of gene expression**, the third major topic of the molecular biology block. The professor explained that all cells contain the same genome, but cells differ because they express different subsets of genes. The class focused on the main mechanisms that control whether a gene is transcribed: **chromatin structure and accessibility**, **histone modifications**, **ATP-dependent nucleosome remodelling**, **DNA methylation at CpG dinucleotides**, and **transcription factors/enhancers**. She also briefly introduced methods for measuring gene expression: **microarrays**, **RNA-seq**, and **single-cell RNA-seq**, before walking through **UCSC Genome Browser exercises** using the gene *RING1* to visualise expression, CpG islands, histone marks, enhancer-associated marks, transcription factor binding sites, and transcription start sites.

Key takeaways:

- Gene expression is the set of mechanisms by which cells increase or decrease production of RNA and protein products.
- Cell identity and tissue identity are determined by **which genes are expressed**, not by differences in the DNA sequence itself.
- **Heterochromatin** is tightly packed and transcriptionally silent; **euchromatin** is open and transcriptionally active.
- **CpG islands in promoters remain unmethylated in active genes and methylated in inactive genes**; methylation blocks transcription factor binding and can silence genes.
- Histone modifications can be temporary (acetylation, phosphorylation) or more permanent (methylation), and they help control DNA accessibility.
- **Enhancers** can be far away in linear DNA but act at a distance because chromatin remodelling brings them close to their target promoter in 3D.
- Modern transcriptomics (RNA-seq and single-cell RNA-seq) allows quantitative measurement of gene expression and is a major source of data in bioinformatics.

#### Key concepts

- **Regulation of gene expression** – the group of mechanisms by which cells increase or decrease the production of gene products (RNA and, ultimately, proteins) [00:02:11].
- **Short-term vs long-term regulation** – short-term regulation responds to the environment, e.g. stress; long-term regulation is associated with stable cell/tissue identity [00:03:19].
- **Chromatin** – DNA wrapped around histone proteins; its packing state controls whether DNA is accessible to transcription machinery [00:08:34].
- **Heterochromatin** – highly packed, silent DNA where transcription proteins cannot bind [00:09:42].
- **Euchromatin** – less packed, active DNA with exposed regions where transcription can start [00:09:42].
- **Histone modification / histone code** – post-translational chemical modifications on histone tails that change DNA–histone interaction and chromatin state [00:14:57].
- **Nucleosome remodelling complexes** – ATP-dependent protein complexes that slide, evict, or exchange histones to expose DNA regions [00:17:47].
- **DNA methylation** – addition of methyl groups to cytosine in **CpG dinucleotides**; promoter methylation is associated with gene silencing [00:19:58].
- **CpG islands** – regions rich in CpG dinucleotides; about 70% of promoters contain CpG islands; unmethylated CpG islands allow protein binding and transcription [00:22:06].
- **Transcription factors** – proteins that bind DNA to activate or repress transcription; about 1,400 are encoded in the human genome [00:24:14].
- **Enhancers** – non-coding DNA regions, roughly 200–1,000 nucleotides, where transcription factors bind to regulate a gene; can be close to or far from the target gene [00:30:28].
- **Transcriptome** – the collection of RNA molecules expressed in a sample, including their sequence and quantity [00:38:45].
- **Post-transcriptional regulation** – one of the regulatory layers listed by the professor, together with chromatin remodelling, DNA methylation, and transcription factors; not expanded in detail in this session [00:07:24].

#### Detailed notes

##### Why gene expression is regulated

Gene expression is the set of mechanisms by which cells increase or decrease the production of gene products. This can happen **short-term**, in response to the environment, or **long-term**, to establish stable cell/tissue identity [00:02:11–00:03:19].

The professor’s central example: all cells in the body have the same DNA and could potentially express around 20,000 proteins and 40,000 RNA molecules, but no cell expresses everything at once. A **nerve cell** and an **epithelial cell** have the same genome, yet the nerve cell may express the “green” and “yellow” genes, while the epithelial cell expresses the “yellow” and “purple” genes. The green gene would never be expressed in the epithelial cell. Therefore, what makes cells different is the set of RNAs and proteins they produce [00:04:19–00:05:20].

A **short-term** example: if one of two identical nerve cells is under stress, signals can cause a “blue” gene to be transcribed and its protein produced in that cell only. This is an environmental response rather than a stable identity difference [00:06:22].

The main regulators are [00:07:24]:
- chromatin remodelling mechanisms, including histone modifications and ATP-dependent chromatin remodelling complexes,
- DNA methylation,
- transcription factors,
- post-transcriptional regulation.

##### Chromatin structure and accessibility

DNA is a double strand wrapped around **histone proteins**, allowing the large genome to fit inside the nucleus. The DNA–histone interaction is electrostatic. At the chromosome level, DNA can exist as [00:08:34–00:09:42]:

- **Heterochromatin** – highly packed; DNA is not exposed, so transcription proteins cannot bind; genes are silent.
- **Euchromatin** – less packed; many DNA regions are exposed, allowing transcription to start.

Euchromatin comprises only **2–3% of total DNA** but contains **more than 90% of transcription-factor-bound regions**. There is competition between histones and transcription factors for DNA binding: histones tend to block transcription, while some transcription factors activate it [00:10:48].

Chromatin remodelling is therefore defined as the **dynamic modification of chromatin architecture to allow access of DNA to regulatory proteins**, and this controls gene expression [00:11:51–00:12:53].

##### Histone modifications

Histone modifications are **post-translational modifications** on the tails of histone proteins. Adding chemical groups to histone tails changes the interaction between histones and DNA: some modifications pack DNA more tightly, others loosen it, making DNA accessible [00:12:53–00:14:57].

The combination of chemical modifications is known as the **histone code** or **histone marks** [00:15:58].

Common histone modifications include [00:16:01]:
- acetylation,
- methylation,
- phosphorylation,
- deamination,
- ubiquitination.

The professor stressed the difference in stability [00:16:40–00:17:47]:

- **Phosphorylation and acetylation** are usually temporary changes, added or removed in response to the environment.
- **Methylation** is more permanent and is related to stable tissue-specific gene expression.

##### ATP-dependent nucleosome remodelling complexes

These protein complexes use energy to change how DNA wraps around histones. If the transcription start site is buried inside a nucleosome, transcription cannot begin. The remodelling complex can [00:17:47–00:18:48]:

- **nucleosome sliding** – slide histones so the transcription start site becomes exposed,
- **nucleosome eviction** – remove histones, exposing the region,
- **histone exchange** – replace old histones with new ones.

These complexes therefore change which DNA regions are exposed and are also involved in **DNA repair** [00:19:58].

##### DNA methylation and CpG islands

DNA methylation is different from histone methylation: it adds methyl groups to **cytosine in CpG dinucleotides** [00:19:58].

Facts given in class [00:20:15–00:21:50]:

- The human genome contains about **28 million CpG dinucleotides**; approximately **75% are methylated**.
- CpG methylation increases the frequency of spontaneous mutation, especially **C → T**.
- DNA methylation is essential for normal mammalian development and is associated with:
  - genomic imprinting,
  - X-chromosome inactivation,
  - repression of transposable elements,
  - ageing,
  - carcinogenesis.
- Adenine methylation has also been identified.

**CpG islands** are regions rich in CpG dinucleotides. In the human genome, less than 10% of all CpGs are CpG islands, but **about 70% of promoter regions contain CpG islands** [00:22:06–00:23:09].

The key rule, corrected during class [00:43:58–00:45:14]:

- In **active genes**, CpG islands in promoter regions remain **unmethylated**, allowing transcription factors and transcription machinery to bind.
- In **inactive genes**, these CpG islands are **methylated**, creating a steric clash that prevents protein binding and therefore silences the gene.

Inactive gene promoters can also recruit proteins that bind methylated DNA; these proteins can recruit **histone methyltransferases or deacetylases**, promoting histone deacetylation and methylation. Thus, DNA methylation and histone modification are connected [00:24:14].

Abnormal methylation of CpG islands that should be unmethylated can silence genes and may be related to **cancer** [00:22:06].

##### Transcription factors and enhancers

Transcription factors are proteins that bind specific DNA sequences to control gene expression. There are about **1,400 transcription factors** in the human genome. They can act as **activators** or **repressors**, and the same factor may activate some genes while repressing others [00:24:14–00:30:28].

**Enhancers** are non-coding DNA regions, usually **200–1,000 nucleotides**, where transcription factors bind. They are often close to their target gene but can also be **very distal in linear sequence** while still being structurally close in 3D [00:30:28–00:31:58].

The professor gave a detailed example of an enhancer located far from gene B, with another gene in between [00:31:58–00:34:09]:

- In the linear arrangement, a transcription factor bound at the enhancer is too far from gene B to influence it, and it does not regulate gene A.
- Through chromatin remodelling (histone modifications or ATP-dependent remodelling complexes), the enhancer can be repositioned close to gene B.
- Then the enhancer-bound transcription factor can interact with another factor bound at gene B’s promoter, allowing transcription of gene B.

The full activation sequence in response to a signal is [00:26:57–00:29:08]:

1. A signal triggers post-translational modification of a transcription factor.
2. The modified transcription factor moves from the cytosol into the nucleus.
3. The DNA region containing the transcription factor binding site must be exposed: no DNA methylation and no tight wrapping around histones.
4. The transcription factor binds its DNA sequence.
5. RNA polymerase binds to the promoter and starts transcription.
6. RNA and then protein are produced to respond to the environment.

For this to work, both the **enhancer region** and the **promoter region** must be unmethylated and exposed, and transcription factors must be correctly modified and localised [00:34:09].

##### Measuring gene expression

Over the last 15 years, it has become possible to measure which genes are expressed in a given tissue or patient. Differential expression between disease and non-disease samples helps understand disease mechanisms and identify biomarkers [00:35:10–00:36:17].

Three techniques were compared:

- **Microarrays** – detect expression of thousands of genes simultaneously, but only **qualitatively** (expressed vs not expressed); they cannot quantify RNA amount [00:36:17–00:37:41].
- **RNA-seq** – RNA is isolated, fragmented, converted into DNA, and sequenced. It determines the **transcriptome**: the collection of expressed RNA molecules, their sequence, and their quantity [00:37:41–00:38:45]. Bulk RNA-seq captures an **average of thousands of cells**. It can identify post-transcriptional modifications, alternative splice transcripts, SNPs, and gene expression changes over time or between groups [00:38:45–00:39:47].
- **Single-cell RNA-seq** – determines gene expression **for individual cells**, rather than an average across thousands of cells. This is a newer technique [00:39:47].

When comparing expression profiles, researchers usually do not work gene by gene; instead they use **Gene Ontology terms**, which are keywords describing the biological processes or molecular functions of genes. For example, genes involved in the cell cycle may be more active in disease, while lipid metabolism genes may be more active in non-disease [00:39:47–00:42:00].

Methods based on **chromatin immunoprecipitation** can also detect **histone modifications** and **transcription factor binding sites**, linking chromatin marks to gene expression [00:42:00–00:42:24].

The professor emphasised that transcriptomics is a very active area in bioinformatics, producing huge datasets. Together with genome, proteome, and metabolome data, these methods give an increasingly complete picture of a patient, although the causal changes in disease are still not fully understood [00:48:22–00:50:25].

##### UCSC Genome Browser practical exercises

The practical used the UCSC Genome Browser with the gene **RING1** to visualise regulatory elements [00:52:51].

**Gene display** – Use NCBI RefSeq to show the gene with exons and introns; zoom out several times because regulatory elements often lie outside the gene body [00:53:54–00:55:10].

**Expression across tissues** – Use **GTEx transcripts** in the expression section. RING1 is expressed mainly in the brain and not appreciably in other tissues. Different transcripts show different brain-region expression: one transcript was restricted to two brain regions, including cerebellum, while another was expressed in more regions. The exercise then filtered to brain tissues only [00:55:39–01:04:07].

**Single-cell expression** – Use the UCSC single-cell RNA-seq track for a **cortex** dataset. This shows expression levels of RING1 in individual cortex cells, rather than an average of the whole tissue [01:04:36–01:05:46].

**Histone marks and CpG islands** – In the ENCODE Regulation section:

- Display **H3K4me3** histone marks; these appear especially in the region upstream of the gene, suggesting the promoter [01:08:37–01:09:08].
- Display **CpG islands**; islands are visible upstream and inside the gene. CpG islands in the promoter region indicate unmethylated DNA where proteins can bind [01:09:08–01:10:49].
- Display a histone **acetylation** mark associated with enhancers; these marks appear close to the gene and suggest an enhancer region [01:11:56–01:14:17].

To locate the promoter: if the gene is on the forward strand, the promoter is to the left (5′ side). The professor suggested considering roughly **5,000–10,000 nucleotides upstream** to be safe, though the exact promoter length varies. CpG islands can help localise it [01:10:49–01:12:59].

**Transcription factors** – Display ENCODE transcription factor peak clusters. Transcription factor binding peaks overlap the same regulatory region already identified by histone marks and CpG islands, reinforcing that this region regulates RING1 expression [01:17:15–01:20:24].

**Regulatory elements and transcription start sites** – Use **GeneHancer** in the Regulation section to display regulatory elements and transcription start sites. The transcription start site appears as a red line [01:23:09–01:24:12].

The overall message: you can combine expression data, histone marks, CpG islands, transcription factor binding, and regulatory element tracks to identify the regions likely controlling a gene’s expression.

#### What the professor stressed

- **Cell identity comes from the pattern of expressed genes**, not from a different genome [00:05:20].
- **Regulation of gene expression is extremely complex and not fully understood**; we have identified many pieces but not the complete puzzle [00:48:22].
- **Histone methylation is more permanent**, while **acetylation and phosphorylation are more temporary** responses to the environment [00:16:40–00:17:47].
- The key relationship: **CpG islands in promoters are unmethylated in active genes and methylated in inactive genes** [00:43:58–00:45:14]. She explicitly corrected this during the session.
- An enhancer can be far from its target gene in linear sequence but functionally close in 3D because of chromatin remodelling [00:31:58–00:34:09].
- Gene expression measurement, especially **transcriptomics**, is a hot topic in bioinformatics and generates large patient-level datasets [00:49:25].
- For the UCSC exercises, **different histone marks are not all equivalent**: H3K4me3 is associated with promoters, and acetylation marks are associated with enhancers [00:19:22].
- The professor reminded students that genomic databases change constantly: new transcription factors, enhancers, and promoters are still being discovered and added [00:51:46].

#### Course logistics mentioned

- This was the **last class** of the biomedicine course [00:00:00].
- **Task 3** is due around the same time as the exam, approximately one or two days before, in about two weeks [00:00:00, 01:25:36].
- The exam will consist of **short questions** and the professor expects **short answers**, similar to the previous exam [01:25:36].
- The exam will include **genome variation**, **regulation of gene expression**, and **proteins**. She said she would send a message with the exact contents [01:25:36].
- Failed exams can be **reassessed** in about three to four weeks; students can retake exams in which they did not obtain at least a 5 [01:25:36].
- Students can contact her by email or request a tutoring session for questions [01:26:39].

#### Self-test

1. Two cells in the same body have identical genomes, yet one is a nerve cell and one is an epithelial cell. Explain why their proteins differ.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
They express different subsets of genes. Both cells have the potential to express all genes, but regulatory mechanisms such as chromatin accessibility, DNA methylation, histone modifications, and transcription factors activate only certain genes in each cell type. Long-term marks, especially histone methylation, help maintain these tissue-specific expression patterns.
</details>

2. Compare heterochromatin and euchromatin in terms of DNA packing and transcription.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Heterochromatin is highly packed DNA associated with histones; the DNA is not exposed, so transcription factors and polymerase cannot bind and genes are silent. Euchromatin is less packed, exposes DNA regions, allows transcription factor binding, and is transcriptionally active. Euchromatin is only 2–3% of DNA but contains more than 90% of transcription-factor-bound regions.
</details>

3. Why does the professor describe histone acetylation as a temporary change but histone methylation as more permanent?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Acetylation and phosphorylation can be added and removed quickly in response to environmental signals, so they support short-term gene regulation. Methylation tends to remain longer and is associated with stable, long-term cell- or tissue-specific expression patterns.
</details>

4. A promoter contains an unmethylated CpG island. What does this suggest about the gene? What would happen if that island became methylated?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
An unmethylated CpG island in a promoter suggests the gene is active, because transcription factors and the transcription machinery can bind. If the island becomes methylated, the methyl groups create a steric clash that prevents protein binding, so transcription cannot start and the gene is silenced. Abnormal methylation of CpG islands can be associated with cancer.
</details>

5. An enhancer is located far from its target gene in linear DNA, with another gene between them. How can it still regulate the target gene?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Chromatin remodelling can change the 3D conformation of DNA, bringing the enhancer close to the target gene. Then a transcription factor bound at the enhancer can interact with factors bound at the target gene’s promoter, enabling transcription. The linear distance is less important than structural proximity in 3D.
</details>

6. What is the difference between a microarray experiment and an RNA-seq experiment in terms of quantification?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Microarrays are qualitative: they can tell you whether genes are expressed or not, and they can compare expression between samples, but they cannot quantify the amount of RNA. RNA-seq is quantitative: it determines the sequence and the amount of expressed RNA molecules, giving a transcriptome.
</details>

7. Why is single-cell RNA-seq informative compared with bulk RNA-seq?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Bulk RNA-seq averages expression over thousands of cells, so it can hide differences between individual cells. Single-cell RNA-seq measures gene expression in individual cells, revealing heterogeneity within a tissue and expression profiles of specific cell populations.
</details>

8. After finding thousands of differentially expressed genes, why might a researcher use Gene Ontology terms instead of listing every gene?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Gene Ontology terms describe genes with keywords linked to biological processes or molecular functions. Grouping differentially expressed genes into ontology terms helps identify which cellular processes or pathways are activated or inactivated in a disease or tissue, which is more interpretable than a long gene list.
</details>

9. In UCSC Genome Browser, you see H3K4me3 marks, unmethylated CpG islands, and transcription factor peaks immediately upstream of a gene. What do these observations suggest?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
They suggest a promoter/regulatory region. H3K4me3 marks are associated with promoters; CpG islands in promoters are unmethylated in active genes; and transcription factor binding peaks indicate proteins that regulate transcription. Together these features identify the region likely controlling the gene’s expression.
</details>

10. How are DNA methylation and histone modification connected in gene silencing?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Proteins that bind methylated DNA can recruit enzymes such as histone methyltransferases or deacetylases, which add methyl groups to histones or remove acetyl groups. These histone modifications promote a more closed chromatin state, reinforcing silencing. Thus DNA methylation and histone modification cooperate to keep genes inactive.
</details>

11. What is the role of ATP in nucleosome remodelling complexes?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
ATP provides the energy needed to slide nucleosomes, evict histones, or exchange histones, thereby changing which DNA regions are exposed. Unlike simple histone modifications, these remodelling activities require energy.
</details>

12. Define the term “transcriptome”.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The transcriptome is the collection of all RNA molecules expressed in a sample, including both their sequences and their quantities. RNA-seq is used to determine the transcriptome.
</details>

#### Related course materials

- [3 - Regulation of Gene Expression](materials/09_3-%20Regulation%20of%20Gene%20Expression.pdf)

### 20. BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20260112 — Mon 12 Jan 2026

*Content: study notes (deepseek-v4-pro) · [Video](recordings/23_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20260112-20260112_170416-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.mp4) · [Transcript](transcripts/23_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20260112-20260112_170416-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md) · [Study notes](notes/deepseek-v4-pro/23_BE-CellBiology-Physiology-20260112-20260112_170416-Enregistrament%20de%20la%20reuni%C3%B3.md)*

#### Summary

This final session of the course completed the **endocrine system** review and covered the **nervous system** in more detail, then introduced **homeostasis and physiopathology**. The endocrine part focused on how hormones act as chemical messengers, the main endocrine glands, and the recurring three-level regulatory axis: hypothalamus → pituitary (hypophysis) → third organ, illustrated with the female reproductive cycle. The nervous system part covered CNS/PNS organisation, neuron and neuroglia structure, action potentials, synaptic transmission, integration/summation in neural circuits, and sensory/motor pathways. The session ended with definitions of homeostasis, internal vs external environment, negative and positive feedback, and basic clinical concepts such as disorder, disease, symptoms, signs and diagnosis.

Key takeaways:

- Hormones are chemical signals released into interstitial fluid or blood that act only on cells with the appropriate receptor.
- Many endocrine axes use a three-level control system: hypothalamus, hypophysis/adenohypophysis, and a peripheral gland, usually with negative feedback.
- Neurons are excitable, post-mitotic, polarised cells: many dendrites receive input, one axon transmits output.
- Action potentials depend on voltage-gated Na⁺ and K⁺ channels; myelin speeds propagation.
- Integration means a neuron sums many excitatory and inhibitory synaptic inputs and fires only if threshold is reached.
- Homeostasis is maintained by feedback systems; negative feedback is the usual mechanism, while positive feedback is rare and amplifies a response, as in childbirth.

#### Key concepts

- **Hormone** — a chemical product secreted by a cell/endocrine gland into interstitial fluid or bloodstream, which communicates information to other cells that express a specific receptor [00:02:32].
- **Endocrine gland** — an organ specialised mainly in hormone production [00:04:19].
- **Hypophysis (pituitary)** — small endocrine organ beneath the brain; posterior part (neurohypophysis) releases oxytocin and ADH; anterior part (adenohypophysis) releases GH, TSH, ACTH, prolactin, FSH and LH [00:05:36].
- **Releasing/inhibiting hormones** — hypothalamic neuroendocrine signals that stimulate or inhibit adenohypophyseal hormone secretion [00:07:41].
- **Three-level endocrine axis** — hypothalamus → hypophysis → third organ, with final hormone effect on target tissues [00:08:30].
- **Action potential** — a propagating electrical signal in neurons caused by voltage-gated Na⁺ entry and K⁺ exit across the plasma membrane [00:34:40].
- **Neuroglia** — support cells of the nervous system; they divide and provide support, nutrition, protection and myelination [00:33:35].
- **Myelin sheath** — insulating wrapping around axons produced by oligodendrocytes (CNS) or Schwann cells (PNS) that accelerates action potential propagation [00:37:02].
- **Neurotransmitter** — chemical released from a presynaptic terminal that binds receptors on a specific postsynaptic cell, the most common form of synaptic transmission [00:39:??].
- **Integration/summation** — the process by which a neuron combines many synaptic inputs; only if the summed depolarisation reaches threshold is an action potential generated [00:42:56].
- **Homeostasis** — the process whereby the internal environment of an organism tends to remain balanced and stable [00:52:22].
- **Internal environment** — extracellular fluid inside the body: mainly interstitial fluid, plus lymph, cerebrospinal fluid and plasma [00:55:28].
- **External environment** — outside the body, including internal compartments directly connected to the outside such as gut lumen, lung air and urinary tract liquid [00:56:??].
- **Negative feedback** — a response that reverses a change and restores the regulated variable to its set point [01:02:50].
- **Positive feedback** — a response that amplifies the original stimulus; rare and needs an external factor to stop [01:03:57].
- **Sign vs symptom** — a sign is an objective change detectable by a physician; a symptom is a subjective change reported by the patient but not directly observable [01:09:30].

#### Detailed notes

##### 1. Endocrine system: general principles

The endocrine system consists of specialised organs whose main function is hormone production, although almost all body organs produce some hormones [00:01:02]. Its main role, together with the nervous system, is to coordinate the different body systems so the body functions as a whole [00:02:32].

Hormones are released into the interstitial fluid between cells and transmit information to other cells. Some target cells are nearby; other hormones enter the bloodstream and reach distant parts of the body [00:03:12]. Regardless of whether a hormone is hydrosoluble or liposoluble, it always needs a receptor, typically a protein on/in the target cell, for the cell to react [00:04:19].

##### 2. Hypophysis and hypothalamic control

The **hypophysis (pituitary)** is a small organ below the brain that links the CNS and the endocrine system [00:05:36].

- **Neurohypophysis (posterior pituitary)**: produces **oxytocin** and **antidiuretic hormone (ADH)**. These hormones are produced by neurons whose axons end in this area; they release hormone into interstitial space rather than making a synapse [00:05:36].
- **Adenohypophysis (anterior pituitary)**: produces **growth hormone (GH)**, **thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH)**, **adrenocorticotropic hormone (ACTH)**, **prolactin**, **follicle-stimulating hormone (FSH)** and **luteinizing hormone (LH)** [00:06:39].

Adenohypophyseal hormone release is controlled by the **hypothalamus**. Hypothalamic neuroendocrine cells produce releasing hormones that stimulate adenohypophyseal secretion, or inhibiting hormones that suppress it [00:07:41].

##### 3. Three-level axes: hypothalamus → hypophysis → third organ

Most pituitary hormones act on a third organ, creating a three-step regulatory system [00:08:30]:

- **Adrenal gland** (above the kidney): releases **cortisol** only when ACTH arrives from the adenohypophysis. Other adrenal hormones such as **aldosterone, epinephrine and norepinephrine** are released without that adenohypophyseal connection [00:09:49].
- **Thyroid gland**: releases **T3 and T4** after TSH arrives; T3/T4 regulate metabolism. **Calcitonin** is also produced by the thyroid without adenohypophyseal control [00:09:49].
- **Parathyroid glands** (posterior of the thyroid): produce **parathyroid hormone (PTH)**. Together, calcitonin and PTH regulate circulating calcium levels [00:10:57].

This axis arrangement allows inter-regulation, usually through negative feedback [00:14:38].

##### 4. Pancreas and gonads

The **pancreas** has an exocrine function — secreting pancreatic juice for digestion in the small intestine — but also contains **islets of Langerhans**, which form the endocrine pancreas. These islets produce **insulin** and **glucagon**, which regulate blood glucose [00:11:59].

**Ovaries and testicles** are not pure endocrine glands because they also produce germ cells, but their hormone production is important. Ovaries produce mainly **estrogen and progesterone**; testicles produce **testosterone** [00:13:36].

Sex hormone regulation again follows the three-level axis [00:14:38]:

1. Hypothalamus releases **gonadotropin-releasing hormone (GnRH)**.
2. GnRH stimulates the adenohypophysis to secrete **FSH** and **LH**.
3. FSH/LH act on ovaries/testes to stimulate the final sex hormones: testosterone in males, estrogens and progesterone in females.

Negative feedback from the final hormones regulates the whole system [00:14:38].

##### 5. Example: female reproductive cycle

The reproductive cycle lasts about **25–28 days** and illustrates the GnRH–FSH/LH–ovary axis [00:16:09]:

- Early in the cycle, the hypothalamus releases **GnRH**, which activates **FSH** secretion.
- FSH reaches the ovaries and stimulates growth of the cells associated with the oocyte; these cells produce **estrogen**.
- Rising estrogen acts on the **uterus**, making the **endometrium proliferate** and increase in thickness [00:16:09].
- Later, **LH** also increases; when LH reaches the ovary, it triggers **ovulation**, releasing the oocyte [00:17:59].
- After ovulation, the cells that surrounded the oocyte continue producing **estrogen** and also begin producing **progesterone**. Progesterone maintains the endometrium in a **secretory phase** for about 14 days, producing components a fertilized oocyte might need [00:18:33].
- If fertilization does not occur, these ovarian cells degenerate, hormone production falls, the endometrium dies and is shed as **menstruation**, and a new cycle begins [00:19:49].

##### 6. Nervous system: organisation and functions

The nervous system has two main components [00:20:51]:

- **Central nervous system (CNS)**: the encephalon (brain, cerebellum, brainstem) and spinal cord.
- **Peripheral nervous system (PNS)**: nerves, ganglia, enteric plexuses and sensory receptors outside the protection of the cranium and vertebrae.

Functions of the nervous system are coordination — detecting sensory information, processing it in the CNS, and activating motor responses [00:20:51].

The **sensory division** obtains information from the environment or body and transfers it to the CNS. It includes **somatic senses** (temperature, pain, touch) and **special senses** (vision, hearing, taste) [00:22:05].

The **motor division** sends outputs from the CNS to effectors. It has:

- **Somatic nervous system**: voluntary; activates skeletal muscle.
- **Autonomic nervous system**: involuntary; controls smooth muscle, cardiac muscle, glands and the enteric system around the digestive tube [00:23:10].

##### 7. CNS structure: gray and white matter

The brain and cerebellum are folded structures in which neurons are arranged in layers. For example, in the cerebellum, Purkinje cells form a well-structured layer above granular cells; this layered arrangement is important for brain complexity [00:24:??].

In a brain slice, particularly the cerebral cortex, there are two visible regions:

- **Gray matter**: contains neuron cell bodies/somas (where the nucleus is). In the brain, gray matter is on the outside.
- **White matter**: contains axons that connect neurons. In the brain, white matter is on the inside [00:26:24].

In the **spinal cord**, the distribution is the opposite: **gray matter is interior**, containing neuron cell bodies, and **white matter is external**, containing axons that project to and from the encephalon [00:27:33].

##### 8. Peripheral nervous system components

The PNS contains [00:29:13]:

- **Nerves**: bundles of axons, some originating in the CNS, travelling to targets such as muscles.
- **Ganglia**: protuberances containing neuron cell bodies with nuclei; these are specific sensory neurons and autonomic motor neurons.
- **Sensory receptors**: specialised cells that detect sensory changes.
- **Enteric plexuses**: networks of nerves.

##### 9. Cells of the nervous system: neurons and neuroglia

**Neurons** are abundant in brain and spinal cord. They are excitable cells that can generate **action potentials**. They are **post-mitotic** (typically do not divide) and have a polarised structure: a **soma** containing the nucleus, plus **dendrites** and **axons** [00:30:51].

Neuron structure varies. Purkinje cells of the cerebellum have a highly ramified dendritic arbour, while pyramidal cells of the cortex look different. A typical neuron has **many dendrites** but **one single axon**; the axon may branch, but there is only one axon origin from the cell body [00:31:57].

**Neuroglia** are found in similar numbers to neurons and provide support, nutrition and protection. They do divide. In the CNS they include:

- **Astrocytes**: support and nutrition.
- **Oligodendrocytes**: produce myelin sheaths.
- **Microglia**: immune-related protection (macrophages of the CNS).
- **Ependymal cells**: participate in the blood–brain barrier, forming a border between the CNS and other body tissues [00:33:35].

In the PNS the corresponding glial cells are **Schwann cells**, which produce myelin, and **satellite cells**, which support neurons [00:34:40].

##### 10. Action potential and myelination

Neurons generate electrical stimuli called action potentials. At rest, the neuron has a negative membrane potential. If the membrane potential is altered enough to reach **threshold**, **voltage-gated sodium channels** open. Na⁺ enters the cell, causing **depolarisation**. Then **voltage-gated potassium channels** open, K⁺ leaves the cell, and the membrane potential is **restored** [00:34:40].

Action potentials occur at one point and typically propagate along the whole neuron. Because axons can connect to very distant places, this electrical signal enables fast communication over long distances [00:37:02].

**Myelin sheaths**, produced by oligodendrocytes (CNS) and Schwann cells (PNS), isolate the axon from the environment. This insulation makes the electrical stimulus propagate faster [00:37:02].

##### 11. Synaptic transmission

Information flow in a neuron is directional: dendrites receive information, the soma integrates, and the axon transmits the electrical stimulus rapidly to the axon terminals [00:38:25].

When the action potential arrives at the distal axon, the neuron usually releases a chemical compound into the **synapse**. This chemical communication is directed to one specific attached cell. Chemical synapses are more common than electrical (gap junction) synapses. The released chemical is a **neurotransmitter**, and it requires specific receptors on the second cell [00:39:??].

Most synapses occur between the axon of one neuron and a dendrite of another; a postsynaptic neuron may receive many synaptic inputs on its dendrites but has one axon that branches to contact many downstream neurons [00:41:15].

##### 12. Integration, summation and circuits

**Integration** is the process that decides whether a neuron fires given the many synaptic inputs it receives. A neuron may receive thousands of inputs at different times and locations. Each small input alone may be insufficient, but inputs arriving together can summate and reach threshold [00:42:56].

Resting potential can be pushed closer to threshold by **excitatory inputs** or pushed away by **inhibitory inputs**. Only when combined excitatory signals arrive at the same moment and region and the summed depolarisation reaches **threshold** is an action potential generated. Once generated, it propagates along the axon and transmits information to the next neuron [00:44:56].

Most neural processing involves **circuits**, i.e. functional pools of interconnected neurons linked by synapses. Integration occurs at each neuron in the circuit, so sensory input does not always produce a motor response — it depends on the summed activity of the network [00:48:22].

##### 13. Sensation, perception and motor output

**Sensory receptors** are specialised structures that detect environmental changes (chemical composition, pressure, light, etc.). Detection by sensory receptors can produce **sensations**, the conscious detection of changes in the external or internal environment. Sensations include general somatic sensations (cold, heat, pain, visceral internal sensations) and special senses (smell, taste, vision, hearing, balance) [00:49:27].

**Perception** is more complex: it is the conscious interpretation of sensations [00:51:19].

Motor responses are produced by the motor division. Skeletal muscle activation occurs only at the **neuromuscular junction**, a synapse between a motor neuron and a skeletal muscle cell. The neurotransmitter is **acetylcholine**; when released, the muscle cell detects it and responds with contraction [00:52:22].

##### 14. Homeostasis: internal vs external environment

**Homeostasis** is the process whereby the internal environment of an organism tends to remain balanced and stable — for example, regulated temperature, glucose and calcium levels [00:52:22].

The **internal environment** is the fluid inside the body but outside cells. About **two-thirds** of total body fluid is intracellular; the remaining extracellular fluid is the internal environment. It is mainly **interstitial fluid**, along with **lymph**, **cerebrospinal fluid** and **plasma** [00:55:28].

The **external environment** is any place outside the body, but also internal compartments that connect directly to the outside because they are not interstitial fluid or plasma. Examples given: the mouth, stomach, intestine lumen, air in the lungs, and liquids in the urinary system [00:56:??]. The external environment is not controlled by the body, while the internal environment is tightly regulated. Large external temperature changes cause only minimal internal changes thanks to homeostatic mechanisms [00:58:20].

Homeostasis is mainly coordinated by the **nervous and endocrine systems** [00:59:27].

##### 15. Feedback systems

Homeostatic control always has the same general structure [01:00:29]:

- A **regulated variable** (e.g. blood pressure, calcium concentration, temperature).
- A **receptor/sensor** that detects changes in the variable.
- An **afferent pathway** sending information to a **control centre**.
- The control centre, sometimes nervous and sometimes endocrine, integrates and sends **efferent signals** to effectors.

This is called a **feedback system**; it continuously supervises, controls, evaluates and modifies the regulated variable [01:00:29].

**Negative feedback** is the most common type. The response reverses the change produced by the stimulus [01:02:50]. Examples:

- **Blood pressure**: increased pressure is detected by receptors; the control centre reduces heart rate, lowering blood pressure.
- **Glucose**: high glucose triggers insulin secretion to lower glucose; low glucose triggers glucagon secretion to raise glucose [01:03:57].

**Positive feedback** is rare and amplifies the stimulus. The example used was **childbirth**: stretching of the cervix activates oxytocin production from the neurohypophysis; oxytocin causes uterine contractions; contractions push the baby against the cervix, increasing stretching; this produces more oxytocin and stronger contractions. The loop continues until an external input interrupts it, specifically birth [01:06:06].

##### 16. From homeostasis to disease

An **imbalance** is instability in maintaining homeostasis; if it occurs, it may lead to disorder or disease [01:08:28].

- **Disorder**: any abnormality in structure or function of the body.
- **Disease**: a specific disorder characterised by a set of distinguishable symptoms and signs.
- **Systemic disease**: a disease that affects many parts of the body simultaneously [01:09:30].
- **Symptom**: a subjective change in body function, not apparent or detectable by a physician; something only the patient can describe.
- **Sign**: an objective change in body function/structure that a physician can detect.
- **Diagnosis**: recognising a disorder or disease by studying the patient’s symptoms and signs together [01:10:56].

#### What the professor stressed

- The **three-level regulation** hypothalamus → hypophysis → third organ appears repeatedly; it is a central pattern for understanding endocrine control and is reinforced by **negative feedback** [00:08:30], [00:14:38].
- The distinction between **gray matter** (cell bodies) and **white matter** (axons), and the fact that their arrangement is **reversed between brain and spinal cord**, was explicitly explained and is the kind of structural fact likely to be examined [00:26:24], [00:27:33].
- The functional polarity of neurons — **many dendrites in, one single axon out** — was emphasised as “quite important to understand how neurons work” [00:31:57].
- **Integration/summation** was repeated and illustrated: a single synaptic input is not enough; the neuron fires only if the summed inputs reach threshold [00:42:56], [00:44:56].
- The **internal vs external environment** distinction, especially that gut/lung/urinary lumina are **external** because they connect to the outside, was highlighted [00:55:28], [00:56:??].
- **Negative feedback is the usual mechanism; positive feedback is rare** [01:02:50].
- The professor listed definitions of **symptom, sign and diagnosis** clearly at the end, suggesting they are exam-relevant [01:09:30].

#### Course logistics mentioned

- This was the **last session** of the course [00:00:00].
- The session might run slightly beyond the scheduled end; students leaving early can catch the end in the uploaded recording [00:00:00].
- **Next week** there is the **second partial exam** of this part of the subject at the same time; students must be **connected synchronically** that day [01:10:56]. According to the course page, this corresponds to **Partial Exam 4 (Unit 5 – Physiology), 19 January 2026, 17:05–18:00**.

#### Self-test

1. Why is the lumen of the stomach considered part of the external environment, while interstitial fluid is part of the internal environment?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
The internal environment is the extracellular fluid inside the body but outside cells: mainly interstitial fluid, lymph, cerebrospinal fluid and plasma. The stomach lumen is directly connected to the exterior and is not interstitial fluid or plasma, so it is classified as external environment even though it is inside the body. The same applies to lung air and urinary tract liquid.
</details>

2. A patient has damage to oligodendrocytes. Which part of the nervous system is affected, and what consequence would you expect for action potential propagation?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Oligodendrocytes are CNS glial cells that produce myelin sheaths around CNS axons. Loss of oligodendrocytes causes demyelination in the brain/spinal cord; action potentials propagate more slowly or may fail, impairing fast communication in the CNS.
</details>

3. In the hypothalamic–pituitary–adrenal axis, high cortisol levels would be expected to feed back and suppress which upstream hormones?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
High cortisol should feed back negatively to reduce hypothalamic releasing hormone (CRH-type signal, as general releasing hormone) and adenohypophyseal ACTH secretion, thereby reducing further cortisol production. The professor emphasised that these three-level axes are regulated by negative feedback.
</details>

4. Why is the posterior pituitary (neurohypophysis) unusual as an endocrine structure?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Its hormones — oxytocin and antidiuretic hormone — are produced by neurons whose axons end in the posterior pituitary. The neurons release hormone into interstitial space rather than making a typical synapse.
</details>

5. A neuron receives one excitatory input on a distal dendrite and two inhibitory inputs near the soma. Based on summation, will it fire? Explain.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Probably not. Excitatory inputs bring the membrane potential closer to threshold; inhibitory inputs move it away. Because inhibition is near the soma and there are two inhibitory inputs, the net summation would likely prevent threshold from being reached. The neuron fires only if the summed depolarisation at the trigger zone reaches threshold.
</details>

6. Compare the locations of gray and white matter in the cerebral cortex and the spinal cord.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
In the cerebral cortex, gray matter is outside (cell bodies) and white matter is inside (axons). In the spinal cord the pattern is reversed: gray matter is central and white matter is peripheral.
</details>

7. Explain why positive feedback during childbirth is useful but also dangerous if not interrupted.
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Positive feedback amplifies the stimulus: cervix stretching → oxytocin release → uterine contraction → more stretching. This produces progressively stronger contractions needed for birth. It is dangerous because it continues to amplify unless an external event (birth itself) interrupts the loop.
</details>

8. How does the pancreas carry out both exocrine and endocrine functions?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Most of the pancreas secretes pancreatic juice into the small intestine (exocrine digestion). Within the pancreas, the islets of Langerhans form the endocrine pancreas, producing insulin and glucagon to regulate blood glucose.
</details>

9. A patient tells the doctor “I feel very tired,” but the doctor observes nothing measurable. Is “tiredness” a symptom or a sign? Why?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
It is a symptom, because it is a subjective change felt by the patient that the physician cannot directly detect or measure. A sign would be an objective change the physician can observe or measure.
</details>

10. If blood glucose falls too low, which endocrine response restores homeostasis, and is this negative or positive feedback?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Low glucose triggers glucagon secretion from the endocrine pancreas, which activates metabolism to release glucose into the bloodstream. This is negative feedback because it opposes the original change and returns glucose toward normal.
</details>

11. A drug blocks voltage-gated sodium channels in neurons. What will happen to action potentials and synaptic transmission?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Blocking voltage-gated Na⁺ channels prevents the depolarisation phase of the action potential. Without action potentials, the signal cannot propagate along the axon, so neurotransmitter release at the synapse will not be triggered and transmission to the postsynaptic cell fails.
</details>

12. Why can myelin increase the speed of action potential propagation?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>
Myelin sheaths insulate the axon from the surrounding environment. This insulation allows the electrical stimulus to propagate faster along the axon than in unmyelinated fibres.
</details>
